# Research Record — Gamification, game feel, and engagement mechanics

Verified literature behind the learning engines. Generated from a multi-agent review (five literature areas researched in parallel, every claim then adversarially fact-checked by an independent reviewer that attempted to refute it).

**How to read the status tags.** `CONFIRMED` = the citation exists and the claim represents it accurately. `MISCHARACTERIZED` = the paper is real but the claim overstated it — the correction is quoted. `UNVERIFIABLE` = the citation could not be confirmed; do not build on it. Evidence strength: `STRONG` = replicated or meta-analytic, `MODERATE` = one well-powered study, `WEAK` = small-n, children-only generalised to adults, or non-peer-reviewed.

**Rule for using this file:** only `CONFIRMED` findings at `MODERATE` or `STRONG` may drive a parameter change. Everything else is context.

See `docs/LEARNING.md` for the resulting architecture and the tunables each of these findings justifies.

---

## Invisible / backend engagement mechanics in highly-retentive games, and the academic literature underpinning them

### Genshin Impact's 5-star gacha uses a two-stage pity curve: a published 0.6% base rate with a published hard pity at pull 90, plus an UNPUBLISHED soft-pity ramp that begins around pull ~74 and adds roughly +6 percentage points per pull until it saturates. The published 'consolidated' rate of 1.6% is only reachable because of the hidden ramp — the arithmetic of 0.6% flat + guarantee at 90 cannot produce 1.6%, which is how the community originally deduced soft pity existed.

- **Status:** CONFIRMED · MODERATE
- **Citation:** HoYoverse in-game 'Details' rate disclosure (mandated by China's Ministry of Culture loot-box probability disclosure rules, in force 1 May 2017); community statistical model built on ~25 million logged pulls (HoYoLAB, 'Statistical model for Genshin Impact's droprates', 2021); Xiao, Henderson, Newall et al., 'Gaming the system: suboptimal compliance with loot box probability disclosure regulations in China', Behavioural Public Policy (Cambridge)
- **URL:** https://www.hoyolab.com/article/497840
- **Peer reviewed:** None
- **Sample / design:** Community reverse-inference from a pooled dataset of ~25M user-logged pulls as of June 2021; maximum-likelihood fit of a per-pull hazard function. Not datamined from binary — inferred from outcome distributions. The base rate, consolidated rate, 50/50 rule and 90-pull guarantee ARE officially published; the shape of the ramp is NOT.
- **Effect size:** Base 0.6%/pull; ramp starts pull 74 (63 on weapon banner); ~+6pp per pull thereafter; 100% at pull 90 (77 weapon). Consolidated realised rate ~1.6%. The modal 5-star arrives at pull ~75-79, not 90 — i.e. most players never see the guarantee fire.
- **Kind:** INVISIBLE_MECHANIC
- **Evidence type:** PRODUCT_OBSERVATION
- **Ethical status:** NEEDS_CARE — the pity ceiling itself is genuinely pro-player (it caps worst-case loss and is why regulators tolerate the system); the concealment of the ramp is not. Concealment exists so the reward reads as fortune rather than as a purchase instalment. In a paid randomised-reward context this is DARK_PATTERN-adjacent; the underlying idea is redeemable only once money is removed.
- **Mechanism:** Two mechanisms stacked. (1) It converts a memoryless variable-ratio schedule (which produces despair-inducing long droughts) into a bounded one — the player can always compute a worst case, which sustains goal-directed persistence rather than learned helplessness. (2) The escalating hazard produces a rising anticipation gradient near pity, the same 'near-miss/almost-there' arousal profile documented in machine gambling. Crucially, hiding the ramp means the player experiences the win as luck rather than as a scheduled payout, preserving the illusion of contingency.
- **Piano-app application:** Strip the money and keep the bounded-worst-case idea. If Fingerly ever randomises what it serves — which passage to drill next, which exercise variant — never use a memoryless draw. Track pulls-since-X per item and ramp the selection weight so nothing can be starved: 'you will not go more than N sessions without the piece you're actually trying to learn.' Concretely: a weighted sampler over drill candidates where weight = base * (1 + k * sessions_since_last_served), with a hard forced-select ceiling. And unlike gacha, SHOW the counter — spec §2 bans vague instruction, and 'next scale-drill in 2 sessions' is a blunt metric, which is the house style. Zero-allocation note: this is a small fixed array of counters, pre-allocated, no per-frame work.
- **Verifier correction:** Two small fixes. (a) The community model is by 'Cgg', published on HoYoLAB June 2021, built on ~25 million pulls from the genshin-wishes.com database (not a HoYoverse dataset) — soft pity 74 on character/standard banner, 63 on weapon banner, per-pull rate rises by base_rate x 10 (= 6pp) until it hits 100% at pull 90 (77 weapon). Model's fitted consolidated rate 1.60521% vs HoYoverse's published 1.600%. (b) The historical narrative — 'which is how the community originally deduced soft pity existed' — is not documented in any of the cited sources; KQM/HoYoLAB both derive soft pity from wish-tally distributions (clustering of 5-stars at 74-80), not from the 1.6% arithmetic. Drop that causal story or downgrade it to 'consistent with'.
- **Replication:** Not a psychological claim; no replication question. Evidentiary status is 'large community datamine, method disclosed' rather than 'developer-documented'. HoYoverse has never published the ramp, so every number for the ramp shape is inferred from player-logged pulls. Multiple independent community models (KQM TCL, paimon.moe, genshin-wishes) converge on 74/+6pp/100%-at-90, which is about as good as undocumented-internals evidence gets — but it remains inference, not disclosure.
- **Verifier notes:** The arithmetic argument itself checks out and I verified it independently: flat 0.6% with a hard guarantee at 90 gives expected pulls = sum_{k=0}^{88} 0.994^k = 69.1, i.e. an effective rate of ~1.45%, not 1.6%. A 1.6% consolidated rate implies ~62.5 pulls average, which requires elevated rates before 90. So the claim's core inference is sound. Regulatory framing is correct: China MoC notice 文市发〔2016〕32号 (Dec 2016) took effect 1 May 2017. Caveat on the Xiao et al. citation: that paper is about aggregate compliance with disclosure rules across the 100 top-grossing PRC iPhone games (loot boxes in 91/100), NOT an analysis of Genshin's soft pity concealment — it supports the regulatory context only, and should not be cited as evidence about HoYoverse specifically.

### Fire Emblem Heroes runs a linear-accumulator pity that is fully disclosed in-game: the 5-star rate starts at 3% (typically split focus/non-focus) and increases by +0.25 percentage points for every 5 summons made without a 5-star, resetting on any 5-star. Nintendo/Intelligent Systems later bolted on two anti-frustration layers: a hard guarantee at 120 summons without a 5-star, and a 'spark' charge system where three off-focus 5-stars force the next 5-star to be the featured unit.

- **Status:** UNCHECKED
- **Citation:** Fire Emblem Heroes in-game 'Summoning Rates / Appearance Rates' disclosure; Fire Emblem Wiki (Fandom), 'Summon (Heroes)'; TV Tropes AntiFrustrationFeatures/FireEmblemHeroes (catalogues the dated additions)
- **URL:** https://fireemblem.fandom.com/wiki/Summon_(Heroes)
- **Peer reviewed:** None
- **Sample / design:** Directly published by the operator in-game and mirrored on wikis; not inferred. The evolution over time (guarantee and spark added years after launch) is documented via patch notes rather than a single study.
- **Effect size:** +0.25pp per 5 summons from a 3% base; hard 100% at 120 non-5-star summons; deterministic focus unit after 3 off-focus 5-stars on a banner.
- **Kind:** BOTH
- **Evidence type:** PRODUCT_OBSERVATION
- **Ethical status:** NEEDS_CARE — same monetised-randomness core as Genshin, but the transparency and the retrofitted guarantees are genuine harm-reduction. The disclosed-linear-ramp pattern is the ethically defensible version of pity.
- **Mechanism:** Contrast with Genshin is the interesting part: FEH's curve is gentle, linear, and DISCLOSED, and it still retains. That is evidence that the retention work is done by the bounded-worst-case guarantee and the visible progress counter — not by concealment. A visible, monotonically-filling bar toward a certain outcome recruits the Zeigarnik/goal-gradient effect: effort accelerates as a tracked goal nears completion.
- **Piano-app application:** This is the version worth copying, minus money. Any 'chance' element in Fingerly should be shown as a filling deterministic bar, not hidden. Better still: use the structure for practice scheduling rather than rewards. A piece you have failed to clear accumulates priority every session it is skipped, and at a fixed threshold it is force-scheduled. Player-facing copy is a number, not encouragement: 'Bach Inv. 1 bars 9-12: 4 sessions untouched, forced next session.'

### Left 4 Dead's AI Director computes a per-Survivor 'Intensity' scalar in real time (raised by taking damage, being incapacitated, and killing Infected at close range; decaying over time) and uses the team maximum to drive a four-state pacing machine — Build Up, Sustain Peak, Peak Fade, Relax — that gates all Infected spawning. During Relax no wanderers, mobs or Specials spawn at all; Relax runs roughly 30-45 seconds and terminates early if the Survivors start moving toward the objective again.

- **Status:** CONFIRMED · MODERATE
- **Citation:** Michael Booth (Valve), 'Replayable Cooperative Game Design: Left 4 Dead' and 'The AI Systems of Left 4 Dead', GDC 2009 — primary developer talks, slides hosted by Valve; Valve Developer Community, 'Left 4 Dead 2/Scripting/Director Scripts'
- **URL:** https://cdn.akamai.steamstatic.com/apps/valve/2009/GDC2009_ReplayableCooperativeGameDesign_Left4Dead.pdf
- **Peer reviewed:** None
- **Sample / design:** First-party design disclosure by the system's author at GDC 2009, plus the shipped scripting API which exposes the state names and tunable spawn parameters to mappers. This is documented fact, not community inference; the exact intensity coefficients are not published.
- **Effect size:** Relax ~30-45s, ended early on player movement; spawn population is set to full during Build Up and zeroed during Relax. Booth's stated goal was procedurally generating unlimited variation from a small team's authored content.
- **Kind:** INVISIBLE_MECHANIC
- **Evidence type:** INDUSTRY_TALK
- **Ethical status:** BENIGN — serves experience quality, not spend. Nothing about it extracts money or time; if anything the Relax state shortens sessions by letting tension resolve.
- **Mechanism:** It enforces a sawtooth rather than a monotone difficulty line. Sustained high arousal saturates and becomes noise; sustained low arousal becomes boredom. By forcing a trough after every peak, the Director keeps re-establishing contrast so the next peak lands. It also implements the flow channel in the time domain rather than the skill domain — instead of asking 'is this player good enough', it asks 'has this player had enough recently'. The early-exit-on-movement rule is elegant: it hands pacing control back to the player without telling them they have it.
- **Piano-app application:** Directly transplantable and probably the single highest-value idea here for Fingerly. Model 'practice intensity' per session: raise it on error density, tempo relative to target, and consecutive failed repeats; decay it on clean passes. Gate what the session serves next on a four-state machine — Build Up (introduce the hard passage), Sustain Peak (drill it), Peak Fade (same passage at reduced tempo), Relax (a piece already mastered, or free play, ~30-60s, exited early if the player starts playing on their own). This gives the closed-ended session shape SPEC §2 demands without a single line of cheerleading copy. Implementation fits the hot-path rules: the intensity scalar updates on the MIDI delivery thread from already-parsed events, one float, no allocation.
- **Verifier correction:** Verified against the primary slide decks (both PDFs live at Valve's CDN under /apps/valve/2009/), but three details need fixing. (1) 'gates ALL Infected spawning' is false — the deck has an explicit slide: 'Boss Encounters NOT affected by adaptive pacing' (Tank/Witch), reasoning that 'Overall pacing affected too much if they are missing'. (2) Relax is not a total spawn blackout: the Minimal Threat Population slide reads 'No Wanderers UNTIL TEAM IS CALM / No Mobs / No Special Infected (although existing Specials may attack)' — wanderers resume within Relax once the team calms, and Specials already in play keep attacking. (3) Intensity rises 'When nearby Infected dies, inversely proportional to distance' — i.e. any nearby Infected death, not specifically the player killing at close range. Also: the deck containing this pacing material is 'The AI Systems of Left 4 Dead', which was Booth's AIIDE 2009 keynote; only 'Replayable Cooperative Game Design' carries the GDC2009_ filename. Attributing both to GDC 2009 is imprecise.
- **Replication:** n/a — this is documented engineering, not a psychological effect, and it is about as well-evidenced as game internals get (first-party slides published by the developer with the actual state machine and constants). Per the review rules an industry talk is MODERATE at best: it is strong evidence that Valve BUILT this, and no evidence at all about whether it produces the claimed player experience. Booth presents zero player-outcome data.
- **Verifier notes:** Everything else is verbatim-accurate. Direct quotes from the deck: Intensity increases 'When injured by the Infected, proportional to damage taken', 'When the player becomes incapacitated', 'When player is pulled/pushed off of a ledge by the Infected'; 'Decay Survivor Intensity towards zero over time'; 'Do NOT decay Survivor Intensity if there are Infected actively engaging the Survivor'. 'Track the max intensity of all 4 Survivors' confirms the team-maximum. Sustain Peak = 'Continue full threat population for 3-5 seconds after Survivor Intensity has peaked'. Relax = 'Maintain minimal threat population for 30-45 seconds, or until Survivors have traveled far enough toward the next safe room, then resume Build Up.' Booth's own summary slide is worth quoting in any writeup: 'Algorithm adjusts pacing, not difficulty — Amplitude (difficulty) is not changed, frequency (pacing) is' and 'Survivor Intensity estimation is crude, yet the resulting pacing works.'

### Both Crash Bandicoot 2/3 and Resident Evil 4 ship undisclosed difficulty rubber-banding. Naughty Dog's DDA silently grants Crash an Aku Aku mask after roughly 5-6 deaths in a section and converts ordinary crates into checkpoint crates; RE4 maintains a hidden 'rank' from 1-10 (with an internal 1000-10999 progress sub-value) that modifies damage dealt and taken, headshot-critical chance, hitbox sizes, enemy aggression/speed/accuracy, knife durability, ammo drop quantities, and which enemy types are eligible to spawn. RE4 (2023) applies adaptive rank on every difficulty except Professional.

- **Status:** MISCHARACTERIZED · WEAK
- **Citation:** Andy Gavin (Naughty Dog co-founder), 'Crash Bandicoot – An Outsider's Perspective (part 8)', 2011, including Jason Rubin's comments — 'Our mantra became to help weaker players without changing the game for better players'; Speedrun.com RE4 console guide, 'Rank / Difficulty adjustment analysis' (community reverse-engineering of memory values)
- **URL:** https://all-things-andy-gavin.com/2011/02/16/crash-bandicoot-an-outsiders-perspective-part-8/
- **Peer reviewed:** None
- **Sample / design:** Crash: first-party developer blog, so the existence and intent are documented fact; the specific '5-6 deaths' threshold is community-catalogued (Bandipedia) rather than developer-stated. RE4: the rank system's existence and variable set are established by memory-watching and speedrun-community reverse engineering across two decades — reliable but NOT officially published by Capcom. Treat the exact per-rank coefficients as community-derived.
- **Effect size:** Crash: ~5-6 deaths in a section triggers a free mask and extra checkpoints. RE4: rank 1-10 spanning ammo drops, enemy stats and hitbox size; Professional is documented as excluded in the remake.
- **Kind:** INVISIBLE_MECHANIC
- **Evidence type:** JOURNALISM
- **Ethical status:** NEEDS_CARE — the intent genuinely serves the player (anti-churn at the frustration cliff, not monetisation), but the concealment creates a consent problem and produces measurable player anger on discovery. The honest version is Hades' God Mode. Never combine hidden assistance with a leaderboard, a score, or a progress metric the user will later trust.
- **Mechanism:** Attribution protection. If the game visibly lowers difficulty, the subsequent success is attributed to the game, which destroys the competence satisfaction that SDT says is the point. Invisible assistance lets the player attribute the win to themselves. This is why Gavin's mantra is framed as 'without changing the game for better players' — the adjustment is asymmetric, applying only where the alternative is quitting. The cost is real: RE4's system is widely disliked once discovered, because retroactively learning your wins were subsidised deletes them.
- **Piano-app application:** Use the asymmetry, refuse the concealment. Fingerly should absolutely detect the frustration cliff — N consecutive failed attempts on a passage — and intervene (drop target tempo, shrink the loop to two bars, thin the texture to one hand). But since the app's whole value is an honest picture of the user's ability, the intervention must be stated as a blunt metric, e.g. 'tempo target 92 → 76 after 6 failed passes'. Hidden assistance would corrupt the one thing a practice app sells: trustworthy measurement. Concretely: never fudge the note-accuracy timing window adaptively; adjust the exercise, never the ruler.
- **Verifier correction:** The Crash citation is wrong. The DDA material is in 'Making Crash Bandicoot – part 6' (7 Feb 2011), not part 8. Part 8, 'Crash Bandicoot – An Outsider's Perspective' (16 Feb 2011), is a guest piece by JASON KAY of Activision — not Jason Rubin — about the E3 1996 reception, and it only references DDA in passing ('Although I didn't understand how the Dynamic Difficulty Adjustment (discussed in part 6) actually worked...'). Fix the part number and note that Kay != Rubin. The substance in part 6 does check out, quoting Rubin: 'if a novice player died a lot of times, we could give them an Aku Aku at the start of a round'; 'if you died a lot when running from the boulder, we could just slow the boulder down a little each time'; 'If you died too much a fruit crate would suddenly become a continue point'; and the mantra 'help weaker players without changing the game for better players'. But 'roughly 5-6 deaths in a section' appears nowhere in the source — Rubin says only 'a lot of times' / 'too much'. That number is unsourced and should be deleted. Separately, 'RE4 (2023) applies adaptive rank on every difficulty except Professional' is contradicted by the same community reverse-engineering the claim relies on: Professional does carry adaptive difficulty in the remake, just over a narrow band (starts at DA rank 10, rises to 11 after ~4 kills, drops back at 0 ActionPoints).
- **Replication:** n/a — game internals. Crash: developer-attested, so genuinely documented (once the citation is fixed). RE4: entirely undocumented by Capcom; everything comes from players watching memory addresses. Treat every specific number as provisional, and note that the community itself disagrees about the remake's Professional behaviour — which is exactly the pattern that should trigger caution rather than confident restatement.
- **Verifier notes:** The RE4 rank structure IS corroborated: the speedrun.com console guide 'Rank / Difficulty adjustment analysis' (speedrun.com/re4console/guides/3sahj) exists, and the documented structure is 'a value between 1 and 10, and another one between 1000 and 10999. The first is the actual rank, the second is the rank progress bar', with the rank equal to the leading digit(s) of the progress value. Effects on damage dealt/taken, critical headshot chance and hitbox sizes are documented. I could not independently verify the full enumerated list — knife durability, enemy accuracy, and 'which enemy types are eligible to spawn' were not confirmed from a primary or method-disclosed source. Trim that list to what the guide actually documents. EVIDENCE TYPE is mislabelled: this is a developer blog post plus community memory-watching, not journalism, and Capcom has never documented Game Rank.

### Retentive games routinely replace true randomness with streak-breaking randomness. Modern Tetris (Tetris Guideline) uses the 7-bag Random Generator — shuffle one of each of the seven tetrominoes, deal all seven, refill — which mathematically caps any piece drought at 12 pieces, versus NES Tetris where 13-piece droughts are commonplace and 50-60 piece droughts occur. XCOM: Enemy Unknown goes further and biases the dice against reported odds: on Easy and Normal with four or fewer soldiers, displayed hit chance is multiplied by 1.2, and any shot with a displayed ≥50% chance gains +15 percentage points (absolute) per consecutive miss with no cap, resetting on a hit; on Normal, alien hit chance drops 10pp per consecutive alien hit.

- **Status:** CONFIRMED · MODERATE
- **Citation:** Tetris Guideline / 'Random Generator', TetrisWiki and Hard Drop Tetris Wiki (the 7-bag is a licensing requirement for Tetris-branded games, so this is published spec); XCOM values are read directly from the shipped DefaultGameCore.ini and documented at UFOpaedia, 'Difficulty (EU2012)'
- **URL:** https://tetris.wiki/Random_Generator
- **Peer reviewed:** None
- **Sample / design:** Tetris: formal published guideline, documented fact. XCOM: extracted from shipped game config files, so also documented fact rather than inference — though Firaxis did not advertise it and the displayed hit percentage remains un-corrected on screen, which is the deceptive part.
- **Effect size:** Tetris: max drought 12 pieces (one I at the head of a bag, the next at the tail of the following bag). XCOM: 1.2x multiplier, +15pp per consecutive miss uncapped, -10pp alien accuracy per consecutive alien hit, displayed values capped at 95% under those conditions.
- **Kind:** INVISIBLE_MECHANIC
- **Evidence type:** PRODUCT_OBSERVATION
- **Ethical status:** Tetris 7-bag: BENIGN — published, symmetric, purely anti-frustration. XCOM: NEEDS_CARE — the assistance is pro-player but the UI actively lies about the odds it is showing, which is the line between 'kind design' and 'deception'.
- **Mechanism:** Humans do not have an intuition for independent trials; the clustering illusion means true randomness reads as rigged. Streak-breaking randomness reads as fair BECAUSE it is unfair. The XCOM case also targets the specific emotion that makes people quit — the perceived-injustice spike after a missed 'sure thing' — by making that exact event self-correcting. Note the ethical fork: Tetris changes the distribution and tells you (it's in the spec); XCOM changes the distribution while continuing to display the un-adjusted number.
- **Piano-app application:** If Fingerly ever shuffles anything the user notices — order of drills in a warm-up, which of five problem passages comes up — use bag-shuffle, not independent sampling. Fill a bag with each pending item once, shuffle, deal, refill; this guarantees no passage is skipped twice in a row and no passage repeats three times, with zero allocation (shuffle a pre-allocated IntArray in :core, Fisher-Yates in place). Take the Tetris lesson, not the XCOM one: never let a displayed metric — timing accuracy, tempo, note-hit percentage — differ from the number actually used internally.
- **Verifier correction:** Two precision fixes. (1) The XCOM figures are verbatim-correct for NORMAL — UFOpaedia's Normal 'Player Bonuses' section reads: 'The games will cheat for you on Normal difficulty, with 4 or fewer soldiers: Chance to hit is 120% of displayed value. For hits that have at least a 50% chance of hitting, chance to hit is increased by 15% (absolute) per consecutive miss, with no upper limit. Alien chance to hit is reduced by 10% (absolute) for each consecutive hit.' On EASY the same three bonuses apply only 'If you have PRECISELY 4 soldiers'; dropping to 3 or fewer triggers a different, larger stack (+15pp per missing squad member, a second +15pp-per-consecutive-miss capped at 30, and alien accuracy -25pp per missing member). So 'on Easy and Normal with four or fewer' is right for Normal and slightly wrong for Easy. (2) 'The 7-bag is a licensing requirement for Tetris-branded games, so this is published spec' overstates. The Tetris Guideline is a private licensing document that The Tetris Company has never published; the 7-bag rule is documented by community wikis (TetrisWiki, Hard Drop) reverse-engineering shipped Guideline games. It is well-established, but it is not published spec.
- **Replication:** n/a — deterministic algorithm plus config values, no psychological effect at stake. The XCOM material is the strongest item in the whole set for the claim's actual thesis (a UI that displays a probability it is not using), and it is confirmed by a source that quotes the numbers, not by folklore. The ethics split the claim draws (Tetris BENIGN because symmetric and published; XCOM NEEDS_CARE because the UI misreports) is well-founded on the verified facts.
- **Verifier notes:** The 12-piece cap is a correct mathematical consequence and is stated as such on TetrisWiki: the worst case is a piece appearing first in one bag and last in the next, giving 12 pieces between occurrences. The NES comparison ('droughts of 13 are commonplace, and even 50 to 60 pieces with a dry well is not unheard of') tracks TetrisWiki's own wording almost verbatim, so it is a faithful quotation rather than an independent finding. One sourcing caveat: I could not confirm that the XCOM values are literally 'read directly from the shipped DefaultGameCore.ini' — UFOpaedia presents them as prose in the difficulty tables and points to a separate 'When The Game Cheats...' page for the mechanism; the ini provenance is asserted, not shown. Soften to 'documented at UFOpaedia from game files'.

### Spotify replaced a mathematically correct Fisher-Yates shuffle with a deliberately non-uniform algorithm in 2014 because users complained that true randomness 'wasn't random'. The replacement spreads each artist's tracks approximately evenly across the queue rather than allowing the clusters that uniform permutation inevitably produces.

- **Status:** CONFIRMED · MODERATE
- **Citation:** Lukáš Poláček, Spotify Labs engineering blog, 'How to shuffle songs?' (2014) — first-party engineering disclosure; widely reported, e.g. TechXplore, 'Spotify deals with random shuffle and us mortals' (2015)
- **URL:** https://engineering.atspotify.com/2014/02/how-to-shuffle-songs
- **Peer reviewed:** None
- **Sample / design:** First-party engineering blog post describing the motivating user complaints and the algorithm change. No controlled experiment published; the evidence for the user-complaint premise is Spotify's own account.
- **Effect size:** Not quantified publicly. The qualitative claim is precise: uniform shuffle produced same-artist runs of three-plus, which generated support complaints; the stratified shuffle eliminated them.
- **Kind:** INVISIBLE_MECHANIC
- **Evidence type:** PRODUCT_OBSERVATION
- **Ethical status:** BENIGN — no money or time is extracted, nothing is misrepresented (Spotify published the change), and the user gets the experience they actually wanted. This is the canonical example of a 'hidden hand' that is purely in the user's service.
- **Mechanism:** The clustering illusion. People's mental model of 'random' is closer to 'evenly spread' than to 'independent draws'. When a system's correctness and the user's perception of fairness diverge, perception wins for retention purposes. This is the cleanest available example of the general principle behind pity systems, 7-bag, and rubber-banding: perceived fairness is a designed property, not an emergent one.
- **Piano-app application:** Same conclusion as the Tetris item, and it generalises to review scheduling. If Fingerly builds spaced repetition over passages, do not let the scheduler serve three items from the same piece back to back just because their intervals coincide — stratify by parent piece so a review set feels varied. Also applies to the note-highway: any randomised visual element (particle spawn, colour jitter) should be stratified, since clumping reads as a rendering bug.
- **Verifier correction:** One nuance: 'replaced Fisher-Yates' overstates it slightly. Per the post, Fisher-Yates is retained inside the new scheme — 'We also shuffle the songs by the same artist among each other. Here we can use Fisher-Yates shuffle or apply the same algorithm recursively.' What was replaced is the top-level uniform permutation over the whole playlist. Also worth stating: the algorithm is adapted from an existing method, not invented — 'we found a blog post The art of shuffling music by Martin Fiedler that solves precisely the same problem. However, his algorithm is complicated and could be very slow in some cases, so we modified it to better suit our needs.'
- **Replication:** n/a — engineering disclosure, not a psychological finding. Note that the underlying perceptual premise (humans misread clustered uniform randomness as non-random) is itself a well-replicated result in the judgment literature (clustering illusion / representativeness), which Spotify correctly invokes. Rated MODERATE only because the review rules reserve STRONG for replicated/meta-analytic effects; as product documentation this is as strong as evidence gets.
- **Verifier notes:** I retrieved the original post (labs.spotify.com/2014/02/28/how-to-shuffle-songs/, via Wayback) and verified it verbatim. Key passages: 'some people don't want the same artist playing two or three times within a short time period'; 'the old saying says that the user is always right, so we decided to look into ways of changing our shuffling algorithm so that the users are happier. We learned that they don't like perfect randomness'; 'The main idea is very similar to the methods used in dithering'; 'For each artist we take their songs and try to stretch them as evenly as possible along the whole playlist'; spacing 'will vary randomly from about 20% to 30%' around the even interval, with a random starting offset. Spotify explicitly frames the user complaint as Gambler's fallacy / clustering illusion. Rollout was desktop-first ('now rolled out to everyone using our desktop client and other clients will follow soon'). This is the cleanest-sourced claim in the set — first-party, contemporaneous, technically specific, and self-disclosed.

### Matchmaking is being explicitly optimised for retention and spend rather than fairness. EA Digital Platform researchers with UCLA and Northeastern published EOMM (Engagement Optimized Matchmaking), which models each pairing's effect on a churn/disengagement probability and solves for the graph matching that minimises total predicted churn — showing equal-skill matchmaking is a degenerate special case of EOMM under assumptions that 'rarely hold in reality'. Separately, Activision holds US patent 9,993,735 covering matchmaking that pairs a non-spending player with a player who owns a desirable item specifically to drive a microtransaction, and EA holds matchmaking-optimisation patents. Relatedly, mobile titles including Clash Royale, PUBG and Fortnite have used bot opponents in early matches so new players bank wins during the FTUE.

- **Status:** MISCHARACTERIZED · MODERATE
- **Citation:** Chen, Xue, Kolen, Aghdaie, Zaman, Sun, Seif El-Nasr, 'EOMM: An Engagement Optimized Matchmaking Framework', WWW 2017 / arXiv:1702.06820; Activision US Patent 9,993,735 'Multiplayer video game matchmaking optimization'; Naavik, 'A Primer on Great Online Matchmaking' (industry analysis of bot-seeded FTUE)
- **URL:** https://arxiv.org/abs/1702.06820
- **Peer reviewed:** None
- **Sample / design:** EOMM: evaluated on real player data from a shipped EA title, reporting 'significant improvement in enhancing player engagement' over existing matchmaking — note the published abstract does NOT give a headline percentage, and I could not verify a specific effect size. CRITICAL DISTINCTION: the papers and patents are documented fact; deployment of engagement- or spend-optimised matchmaking in any specific shipped title is NOT confirmed and is community speculation. Activision publicly denied using the patented system. Bot-seeded FTUE in named mobile titles is industry-analyst reporting, not first-party disclosure.
- **Effect size:** Not reliably quantified in public sources. Claimed direction: reduced three-round churn versus skill-based matchmaking.
- **Kind:** INVISIBLE_MECHANIC
- **Evidence type:** PEER_REVIEWED
- **Ethical status:** Split. FTUE scripted wins: NEEDS_CARE — genuinely reduce early churn and are arguably kind, but they lie about the player's standing and set up a difficulty cliff. Spend-optimised matchmaking (the Activision patent): DARK_PATTERN, unambiguously — it engineers envy to trigger a purchase. EOMM as published: NEEDS_CARE, since the objective function is retention, and retention only sometimes coincides with the player's interest.
- **Mechanism:** Churn is concentrated immediately after specific emotional events — a loss streak, a lopsided defeat, an early defeat by a new player. If you can predict the event, you can schedule around it. The FTUE bot-win version works on self-efficacy: early wins establish a belief of competence before the player has the skill to earn one, and that belief carries them through the losses that follow. The EOMM version is the same mechanism pointed at the operator's metric rather than the player's experience.
- **Piano-app application:** Fingerly has no multiplayer, so the transferable part is the FTUE finding: the first session must produce a genuine, earned win. Design session one so a complete beginner plays something recognisable and correct within a couple of minutes — a two-note ostinato at slow tempo over an accompaniment, not a scale. Do NOT fake it: no inflated accuracy score, no widened timing window. Instead pick a task that is genuinely trivially winnable, so the honest metric reads well. That is the difference between a scripted win and a real one, and it also satisfies SPEC §2's ban on decision menus before playing — session one starts with the piano, not a difficulty picker.
- **Verifier correction:** The patent citation is wrong and the two patents are swapped. US 9,993,735 IS titled 'Multiplayer video game matchmaking optimization' — but it is assigned to ELECTRONIC ARTS INC, granted 12 June 2018 (inventors Aghdaie, Kolen, Mattar, Sardari, Xue, Zaman), and it does NOT describe spend-driven pairing; it is a retention/churn-prediction matchmaking patent. The patent that actually describes the microtransaction mechanic is US 9,789,406 B2, 'System and method for driving microtransactions in multiplayer video games', assignee ACTIVISION PUBLISHING INC, granted 17 October 2017, which states the microtransaction engine 'may match a more expert/marquee or otherwise influential player (e.g., clan leader) with a junior player to encourage the junior player to make game-related purchases', and may 'match the marquee player with another player (e.g., a junior player) that does not use or own the item'. The claim has attached Activision's mechanism to EA's patent number and title. Fix both.
- **Replication:** EOMM is a published, peer-reviewed framework with an offline evaluation on EA telemetry — it demonstrates the OPTIMISATION TARGET exists and is tractable, not that any shipped title runs it. There is no independent replication of EOMM's engagement gains, and no public audit of any live matchmaker's objective function. The claim's ethical reading (retention objective != player interest) is fair, but the empirical base for 'matchmaking IS being optimised for spend' in shipped games is thin: one peer-reviewed offline study, two patents (one misattributed, one disclaimed), and one consultancy blog.
- **Verifier notes:** EOMM itself is verified exactly as cited: 'EOMM: An Engagement Optimized Matchmaking Framework', Zhengxing Chen, Su Xue, John Kolen, Navid Aghdaie, Kazi A. Zaman, Yizhou Sun, Magy Seif El-Nasr, WWW 2017, arXiv:1702.06820, and the abstract does say it 'proves that equal-skill based matchmaking is a special case of EOMM on a highly simplified assumption that rarely holds in reality'. Two important framing caveats. (a) A granted patent is evidence of what a company sought to protect, NOT evidence of deployment — Activision publicly disclaimed having implemented 9,789,406 in a shipped game, and no independent verification of deployment exists. Do not present the patent as documented practice. (b) The bot sub-claim traces to a single industry-analysis article (Naavik), which says popular mobile titles 'such as Clash Royale, PUBG, and Fortnite' have used bots to give new players 'the wins (and thus the confidence) that lift the chances of retention'. Only Fortnite is developer-confirmed — Epic announced bots alongside skill-based matchmaking for Chapter 2 Season 1 (Sept 2019). Clash Royale and PUBG Mobile bot-seeding is widely reported by players but is not documented by Supercell or Krafton/Tencent. Downgrade those two to community-reported. EVIDENCE TYPE 'PEER_REVIEWED' applies only to EOMM; patents and Naavik are not peer-reviewed.

### King tunes Candy Crush Saga's 20,000+ levels on a 'complexity staircase' — newer players are shown only simple blockers, more complex blockers unlock as levels are passed — and deliberately alternates hard and light levels rather than escalating monotonically, 'providing players with momentum and breathing room'. Pass rates for new levels are predicted before release by AI bots simulating large numbers of playthroughs, with human judgement overriding on feel.

- **Status:** UNCHECKED
- **Citation:** John Davies, Senior Product Manager, King — 'Crush & Tell' vodcast series, reported by PocketGamer.biz, 'Crafting Candy Crush's difficulty: Blockers, level design, AI and the complexity staircase'; related first-party discussion of measuring wins/losses/attempts per level reported by MobileGamer.biz
- **URL:** https://www.pocketgamer.biz/crafting-candy-crushs-difficulty-blockers-level-design-ai-and-the-complexity-staircase/
- **Peer reviewed:** None
- **Sample / design:** First-party product manager statements in a company vodcast, reported by trade press. IMPORTANT: King has NOT published target pass rates, difficulty percentages, or the specific level numbers at which generosity ends. Widely repeated claims — 'levels 1-35 are rigged to be won', 'by level 200 they want you spending' — are community speculation and low-quality secondary sourcing; I found no first-party or datamined support for specific numbers. What IS documented: the staircase, the deliberate difficulty oscillation, and bot-predicted pass rates.
- **Effect size:** Not disclosed. Documented qualitatively: monotonic escalation is explicitly rejected in favour of a sawtooth.
- **Kind:** INVISIBLE_MECHANIC
- **Evidence type:** INDUSTRY_TALK
- **Ethical status:** NEEDS_CARE — the staircase and the oscillation are legitimately good pedagogy and would be fine in any non-monetised product. They become extractive only where they interlock with the lives economy: a hard level is worth more when failing it costs a metered resource you can buy back.
- **Mechanism:** Two things. The staircase is progressive mechanic disclosure — it removes the cognitive load of learning rules and the challenge of applying them from the same moment, which is the standard failure mode of tutorials. The deliberate hard/easy oscillation is the same sawtooth as the L4D Director: an easy level after a hard one converts relief into momentum, and it also creates the contrast that makes the next hard level feel like an event rather than a wall.
- **Piano-app application:** Two direct lifts. (1) Complexity staircase for notation and technique: do not show key signatures, dynamics, articulation and pedal simultaneously to a beginner — gate each on a demonstrated pass, one new symbol per session at most. This is the operational form of SPEC §2's 'no theory prerequisites': the theory arrives as a mechanic, not as a lesson. (2) Never let session difficulty rise monotonically. Schedule each session as hard-passage / easy-consolidation / hard-passage / mastered-piece, and end on something the user can already play. (3) Steal the bot idea for QA: run a synthetic MIDI player at defined error rates against the scoring engine to sanity-check that a passage's rated difficulty matches its actual pass rate before shipping it.

### Energy/lives/stamina systems are explicitly engineered to shape session length and frequency, then monetised at the friction point. Candy Crush Saga: 5 lives, one regenerating every 30 minutes, $0.99 for an instant refill, with individual attempts lasting 30 seconds to 2 minutes. Puzzle & Dragons: stamina gates dungeon entry, and a $1 Magic Stone buys a continue inside a 10-second window immediately after failure. Genshin Impact: Original Resin regenerates at 1 per 8 minutes against a 160 cap (raised to 200 in v4.7), so a full bar takes 21h20m — tuned just under 24h so a daily login never wastes regeneration. Industry guidance targets a 15-30 minute session from a full bar and 3-4 sessions per day.

- **Status:** CONFIRMED · WEAK
- **Citation:** Adrian Crook & Associates, 'Energy Systems: Lessons from Top Freemium Games' (free-to-play consultancy analysis with per-title numbers); Genshin Impact Wiki, 'Original Resin'; GDC Vault, 'Design and Monetization Strategies in Highly Successful F2P Games'
- **URL:** https://adriancrook.com/energy-systems-lessons-top-freemium-games/
- **Peer reviewed:** None
- **Sample / design:** Practitioner analysis aggregating published store prices, observed regeneration rates and revenue figures across named titles. The per-game numbers are directly observable in the shipped products. The '15-30 min / 3-4 sessions per day' target is stated design guidance, not an experimental result.
- **Effect size:** Candy Crush: 5 lives, 30 min/life, $0.99 refill, 30s-2min per attempt. P&D: $1 continue in a 10s post-failure window. Genshin: 1 resin/8min, 160 cap, 21h20m to full.
- **Kind:** BOTH
- **Evidence type:** INDUSTRY_TALK
- **Ethical status:** DARK_PATTERN in its monetised form. The cap is manufactured scarcity in a digital good with zero marginal cost, and the refill price point sits deliberately at the frustration peak. HOWEVER, the session-shaping half — a bounded session that ends before fatigue — is separable and genuinely valuable; Clash Royale's soft version (unlimited play, metered rewards) is the less coercive variant.
- **Mechanism:** The cap creates artificial scarcity, which does three things at once: it makes each attempt feel consequential (loss aversion on a metered resource), it forces a scheduled return that builds habit through repeated context-cued opening, and it places a purchase decision at the exact moment of peak frustration — P&D's 10-second post-failure window is the purest example, buying at the emotional trough before appraisal kicks in. The Genshin 21h20m figure is the tell: it is derived from the daily login interval, not from anything about the gameplay.
- **Piano-app application:** Take the session bound, refuse the scarcity. SPEC §2 already bans open-ended sessions, and this literature says why bounded sessions retain: they end while the user still has appetite, which is what makes tomorrow's session start. Set a fixed session budget — 15-20 minutes, or a fixed number of passage reps — and hard-stop it, showing the metrics. Critically, do NOT gate the second session: if the user wants to keep playing the piano, the app must never be the thing that stops them. The correct implementation is 'the structured session is over, here is your data, the keyboard still works' — a soft system, not lives. Any Fingerly feature that made the user wait to practise would be indefensible in a learning tool.
- **Verifier correction:** The numbers all match the cited consultancy source — Candy Crush 5 lives (expandable to 8 via the paid 'Charm of Life'), one per 30 min, $0.99 instant refill, attempts 'between 30 seconds and 2 minutes'; P&D $1 Magic Stone continue within a 10-second window after failure; 'allow for 15 to 30 minutes of gameplay from a full energy bar' and 'aim for a setup that allows 3-4 sessions per day, provided they are spaced out'. But the Genshin argument is internally self-defeating as written. Resin regenerates 1 per 8 min and the cap was raised from 160 to 200 in v4.7 (announced 22 May 2024) with the regen rate unchanged — so a full bar now takes 200 x 8 = 1600 min = 26h40m, NOT 'just under 24h'. The 'tuned just under 24h so a daily login never wastes regeneration' argument only ever held for the 160 cap (21h20m) and was broken by the very change the claim itself cites. Either date-stamp the argument to pre-4.7 or drop it.
- **Replication:** n/a — no psychological effect is asserted, only design parameters and a consultant's targets. Nothing here is peer-reviewed or replicated. The claim's strongest defensible form is narrow: these specific games shipped these specific caps and price points, and at least one F2P consultancy openly recommends tuning sessions to 15-30 min. The causal story ('engineered to shape session length, then monetised at the friction point') is the consultancy's own framing, restated — it is testimony about intent from an interested party, not evidence of effect.
- **Verifier notes:** Sourcing is the weak point. Adrian Crook & Associates is a free-to-play design consultancy's marketing blog, not a study — its 'industry guidance' numbers (15-30 min, 3-4 sessions) are one consultant's prescriptive recommendation, not a measured industry norm, and should never be cited as 'industry guidance targets'. The per-title figures are also snapshots: Candy Crush's refill price and life cap vary by region, platform and era, and the $0.99 figure has not been stable. I could not verify a GDC Vault session titled 'Design and Monetization Strategies in Highly Successful F2P Games' — that citation should be checked or removed before use; as written it reads like a plausible-sounding placeholder. The Genshin Resin mechanics (1 per 8 min, 160 -> 200 in 4.7) are confirmed by multiple independent outlets.

### Duolingo's streak system — the most heavily-instrumented daily-reset mechanic in consumer software — was built through 600+ A/B experiments run against DAU. Documented wins: raising the Streak Freeze allowance from one to two (and testing three) significantly improved return rates and drove DAU growth, with the two-to-three delta much smaller than the one-to-two delta; a 'Streak Repair' path letting a lapsed user earn a broken streak back by completing extra lessons within a window; and an eight-word explanation of the mechanic ('Start a day to extend your streak, but miss a day and it resets') that alone produced over 10,000 new DAU. Streak Society gates social identity at 7 days, with further tiers at 30, 100 and 365.

- **Status:** UNCHECKED
- **Citation:** Jackson Shuttleworth, Group Product Manager, Duolingo Retention Team — interview on Lenny's Podcast, 'Behind the product: Duolingo streaks'
- **URL:** https://www.lennysnewsletter.com/p/behind-the-product-duolingo-streaks
- **Peer reviewed:** None
- **Sample / design:** First-party product leadership account of a large A/B programme at consumer scale (Duolingo DAU in the tens of millions). Effects are reported as directional wins with one absolute number (+10k DAU); no confidence intervals, no independent replication. Treat the specific figures as company-reported.
- **Effect size:** Streak Freeze 1→2 = significant DAU gain, 2→3 = much smaller; 8-word explainer = >10,000 incremental DAU; 600+ experiments on the single feature.
- **Kind:** BOTH
- **Evidence type:** INDUSTRY_TALK
- **Ethical status:** NEEDS_CARE — genuinely the closest of these mechanics to serving the user, since daily practice really is what produces language (and piano) skill, and the mercy layers are real harm reduction. But streaks demonstrably generate anxiety and produce degenerate behaviour (one-minute token lessons that satisfy the streak without learning), and the DAU objective can diverge from the learning objective. The dark version is a streak you can only protect by paying.
- **Mechanism:** A streak converts a diffuse goal ('learn Spanish') into a concrete, losable asset, and loss aversion on that asset is roughly twice as motivating as the equivalent gain. But the raw mechanic is brittle: one missed day destroys the accumulated stake and, with it, the reason to return — which is exactly the churn event. Streak Freeze and Streak Repair are the mercy layer, structurally identical to gacha pity: they bound the worst case so a single bad day cannot end the relationship. Shuttleworth's own framing of Repair is the key insight — users must EARN the streak back, because a freely-restored streak has no value and therefore no motivating power. And the 8-word-explainer result is the sleeper finding: the mechanic underperformed for years purely because users did not understand it.
- **Piano-app application:** The single most transferable finding for Fingerly, with three specific rules. (1) Implement a streak, but define the day's requirement as a real practice session, not app-open — and make the requirement blunt and stated. (2) Ship the mercy layer from day one, not as a later patch: two free skip-days on a rolling basis, plus a repair path where a lapsed streak is restored by completing two sessions in one day. Earned, never gifted, never sold. (3) Steal the explainer result — one sentence of plain mechanic description beats any amount of motivational copy, and it fits SPEC §2's three-sentence ceiling and blunt-metrics rule exactly. Explicitly avoid: streak-loss push notifications with guilt framing, and any streak-protection purchase.

### Hades ships the opt-in, fully-disclosed inverse of hidden rubber-banding. God Mode grants 20% damage resistance immediately, then +2% for every subsequent run that ends in death rather than escape, capped at 80%. It can be toggled on or off at any time from the options menu, and locks the player out of no achievements or content.

- **Status:** CONFIRMED · MODERATE
- **Citation:** Supergiant Games in-game implementation and options text; Greg Kasavin (Creative Director, Supergiant) interviews — Inverse, 'Hades devs reveal how God Mode solves the worst thing about the genre'; CanIPlayThat, 'Hades God Mode origins explained by Supergiant Games'
- **URL:** https://www.inverse.com/gaming/hades-god-mode-interview
- **Peer reviewed:** None
- **Sample / design:** Shipped, documented in-game, with designer intent stated in interviews. There is also an independent academic study using Hades to probe frustration tolerance and physiology (arXiv:2401.14878), indicating the title is a live object of PX research.
- **Effect size:** 20% base damage resistance, +2% per death, 80% cap, reversible, no content lockout.
- **Kind:** VISIBLE_UI
- **Evidence type:** PRODUCT_OBSERVATION
- **Ethical status:** BENIGN — the reference implementation for ethical DDA. Opt-in, visible, reversible, sub-JND increments, no penalty, no monetisation. Every other DDA system in this list should be judged against it.
- **Mechanism:** It solves the RE4 consent problem without losing the anti-churn benefit. The player opts in, so the assistance cannot retroactively invalidate their sense of achievement — they already know. The graduated +2% is the clever part: it is deliberately below the just-noticeable-difference threshold per step, so the player never experiences a moment where the game visibly gets easier; they simply find themselves getting further, which reads as their own improvement. Kasavin's stated goal was structural rather than charitable — in a roguelike, a player stuck for five hours 'is not experiencing the cool part', i.e. the assistance exists to keep content flowing, and content flow is what actually retains.
- **Piano-app application:** Adopt this pattern wholesale for Fingerly's difficulty assistance. If a passage has been failed repeatedly, offer an explicit, toggleable assist — reduce target tempo, or widen the required accuracy for progression — stated as a number and reversible at any time. Ramp it in small increments per failed session so no single step is perceptible, and cap it. Do not hide it, do not gate content behind not using it, and keep the underlying honest measurement visible alongside the assisted one ('92 BPM target, assisted 76, your best clean pass 71'). This is the one place where the games literature and a practice tool's need for honest measurement fully agree.
- **Verifier correction:** Accurate as stated; two small additions worth carrying. (1) Toggling is unavailable on a Hell Mode save file — God Mode can be switched on/off at any time on a non-Hell-Mode file only. (2) Turning God Mode off removes the resistance but PRESERVES the accumulated percentage for when it is switched back on, which matters for the claim's 'reversible' framing (it is reversible without losing progress). Mechanically it is delivered as a boon called Deus Ex Machina.
- **Replication:** n/a — product mechanic, directly observable, no psychological effect asserted. The unsupported step is the implicit one the claim's ethics note makes: that opt-in visible assistance produces equivalent anti-churn benefit to hidden DDA. No study compares them. Hades is a good normative model, not evidence about efficacy.
- **Verifier notes:** The 20% / +2% per death / 80% cap and the no-achievement-lockout are confirmed by the Hades Wiki and by GamesRadar's dedicated 'Hades God Mode and Achievements' piece; the Inverse interview with Greg Kasavin exists and covers the design intent ('If you want to experience more of that godlike might, here is the mode for you'). Note that the exact numbers come from the community wiki and press coverage rather than a Supergiant spec document — but they are trivially player-verifiable in the options screen, so provenance is not a concern here. This is the only item in the set where opt-in, visibility, reversibility and no-penalty all hold simultaneously, so the claim's use of it as the reference implementation is well-founded.

### The academic backbone: Csikszentmihalyi's flow channel — the narrow band where perceived challenge matches perceived skill, bounded by anxiety above and boredom below — is operationalised for games in two canonical works. Sweetser & Wyeth's GameFlow (ACM Computers in Entertainment, 2005) decomposes it into eight evaluable elements (concentration, challenge, skills, control, clear goals, feedback, immersion, social interaction). Hunicke's Hamlet system (ACE 2005), built as a library set inside the Half-Life engine, uses inventory-theory methods to predict player 'supply and demand' and covertly adjusts drops and enemy parameters — and reports that subjects did not perceive the changes, which Hunicke explains via change blindness. Jenova Chen's flOw MFA thesis (2006) argues the opposite implementation: give the player subconscious in-game CHOICES that let them self-select difficulty, rather than inferring it from incomplete data.

- **Status:** CONFIRMED · WEAK
- **Citation:** Csikszentmihalyi, 'Flow: The Psychology of Optimal Experience' (1990); Sweetser & Wyeth, 'GameFlow: A Model for Evaluating Player Enjoyment in Games', ACM Computers in Entertainment 3(3), 2005 (~2,300 citations); Hunicke, 'The Case for Dynamic Difficulty Adjustment in Games', ACE '05, DOI 10.1145/1178477.1178573; Hunicke & Chapman, 'AI for Dynamic Difficulty Adjustment in Games'; Chen, 'Flow in Games' MFA thesis, USC, 2006
- **URL:** https://dl.acm.org/doi/10.1145/1178477.1178573
- **Peer reviewed:** None
- **Sample / design:** GameFlow is a literature-synthesis model validated by expert review, later revisited in 'GameFlow 2020: 15 Years of a Model of Player Enjoyment'. Hamlet's evaluation is explicitly described by Hunicke as PRELIMINARY — small-scale, and I was unable to verify specific death-rate numbers or sample size from accessible sources, so do not cite figures for it. The change-blindness explanation for non-detection is Hunicke's interpretation.
- **Effect size:** Not meaningfully quantified. The durable results are qualitative: (a) covert adjustment can go undetected, (b) players report feeling 'cheated' by adjustment they detect, (c) the eight GameFlow elements are individually assessable.
- **Kind:** INVISIBLE_MECHANIC
- **Evidence type:** PEER_REVIEWED
- **Ethical status:** BENIGN as theory; the ethics live entirely in the implementation. Hunicke's covert branch is NEEDS_CARE for the consent reasons above. Chen's player-choice branch is the version compatible with self-determination theory and with any tool making truth claims about the user's ability.
- **Mechanism:** Flow predicts that engagement collapses in both directions, which is why every mechanic above is really a controller trying to hold one variable inside a band. The Hunicke/Chen split is the design decision that actually matters: infer difficulty from telemetry and adjust covertly (effective, consent-free, resented on discovery), or expose difficulty as an in-fiction choice the player makes without experiencing it as a menu (slower, self-determination-preserving). Chen's approach is strictly better where the product's value depends on the user trusting its measurements.
- **Piano-app application:** Fingerly's core loop is a flow controller: challenge = target tempo × passage complexity, skill = measured accuracy and timing variance. Estimate skill from the last N attempts and pick the next target to sit just above it. Follow Chen, not Hunicke: express the adjustment as a choice embedded in play rather than a settings menu — e.g. after a failed pass, the next loop simply starts slower and the tempo number visibly changes, so the player sees the adjustment without being asked to make a decision (SPEC §2 bans decision menus before playing, and this satisfies both). GameFlow's eight elements also make a usable pre-ship checklist: clear goals (which bars, what tempo), immediate feedback (<15ms per SPEC §1 — the latency budget IS a flow requirement, since feedback delayed past ~100ms breaks the action-awareness merging flow depends on), and control.
- **Verifier correction:** All five works exist with the stated identifiers, and the Half-Life detail is verbatim-correct — I pulled the Hunicke & Chapman paper directly: 'Hamlet is a Dynamic Difficulty Adjustment (DDA) system built using Valve's Half Life game engine' and 'The Hamlet system is primarily a set of libraries embedded in the Half Life game engine.' One sub-claim I could NOT verify from primary text: that the ACE '05 paper reports subjects did not perceive the changes AND explains this via change blindness. The ACE paper is paywalled (ACM DL returns 403) and its published abstract says only that it 'offers preliminary evaluation results which challenge common assumptions about player enjoyment and adjustment dynamics'. The change-blindness attribution is widely repeated in the secondary DDA literature but I could not source it to Hunicke's own words. Flag it as unverified or obtain the PDF before restating it as fact.
- **Replication:** This is the item most at risk of being over-rated, so: flow theory is a descriptive framework measured by self-report experience sampling, not a replicated quantitative law. The challenge-skill-balance premise specifically has mixed empirical support, and the various channel/quadrant models have never converged. GameFlow has no replication in the effect-size sense because it makes no quantitative predictions. Hunicke's DDA result is a small preliminary study that has not, to my knowledge, been independently replicated. Rated WEAK: the citations are all real and correctly described, but none of them constitute replicated evidence that covert DDA improves experience — they establish that the idea was formalised and prototyped.
- **Verifier notes:** Calibration on each source. GameFlow: confirmed at ACM CIE 3(3), July 2005, eight elements exactly as listed (concentration, challenge, skills, control, clear goals, feedback, immersion, social interaction), ~2,305 citations. But its own validation was expert reviews of exactly TWO real-time strategy games (one high-rated, one low-rated) — it is a heuristic evaluation checklist, never validated as a predictive model with effect sizes, and its citation count reflects convenience, not evidential weight. Hunicke ACE '05: the evaluation is self-described as 'preliminary'; the companion Hunicke & Chapman paper describes the player study as still prospective ('We will attach a simple heart-rate monitor to a variety of players...'), i.e. the empirical base is thin. Chen: confirmed, USC MFA 2006, presented at the GDC 2006 Experimental Gameplay Workshop; flOw is described as 'a practice and testbed for Embedded Difficulty Adjustment theory formulated in Jenova Chen's game design thesis' — the claim's 'player-choice branch' reading is accurate.

### Self-determination theory provides the non-cynical account of why games retain: satisfaction of autonomy, competence and relatedness independently predicts enjoyment, continued play, and short-term well-being change. Ryan, Rigby & Przybylski ran four studies (participants playing 1, 2 and 4 games respectively, plus an online multiplayer community survey) and found in-game autonomy and competence associated with enjoyment, game preference, and pre-to-post-play well-being shifts; study 4 confirmed all three needs independently predict enjoyment and future play. Notably, competence and autonomy perceptions tracked the intuitiveness of the game CONTROLS, not just the content.

- **Status:** CONFIRMED · WEAK
- **Citation:** Ryan, R. M., Rigby, C. S., & Przybylski, A. K. (2006). 'The Motivational Pull of Video Games: A Self-Determination Theory Approach.' Motivation and Emotion, 30(4), 344-360. DOI 10.1007/s11031-006-9051-8. See also Przybylski, Rigby & Ryan (2010), 'A Motivational Model of Video Game Engagement', Review of General Psychology.
- **URL:** https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s11031-006-9051-8
- **Peer reviewed:** None
- **Sample / design:** Four studies: three lab/quasi-experimental with participants playing 1, 2 and 4 games and completing need-satisfaction and well-being measures pre/post; one large survey of an online multiplayer community. Widely replicated and now the dominant motivational framework in games research.
- **Effect size:** Specific coefficients not retrieved here; the robust, replicated finding is that the three needs are independent predictors and that competence/autonomy satisfaction predicts positive well-being change while their frustration predicts negative change.
- **Kind:** INVISIBLE_MECHANIC
- **Evidence type:** PEER_REVIEWED
- **Ethical status:** BENIGN — this is the framework for judging the others rather than a mechanic. Its practical use is as a filter: if a feature increases engagement by frustrating a need rather than satisfying one, it is extractive regardless of its metrics.
- **Mechanism:** SDT separates two things that look identical from the outside: engagement driven by need satisfaction (intrinsic, sustainable, associated with well-being GAINS) and engagement driven by need frustration or obligation (extrinsic, compulsive, associated with well-being LOSSES). This is the diagnostic that distinguishes every BENIGN mechanic above from every DARK_PATTERN one. Energy systems, spend-optimised matchmaking and streak guilt operate by frustrating autonomy — you must return now, on our schedule. The L4D Director, God Mode and 7-bag operate by supporting competence. The controls finding is the underrated one: perceived competence is partly a property of input latency and control intuitiveness, not just of difficulty tuning.
- **Piano-app application:** Use SDT as Fingerly's design filter, and note that it directly justifies two things already in SPEC. (1) The <15ms keypress-to-visual budget is not a polish item — Ryan et al. tie perceived competence to control intuitiveness, so latency is a motivational requirement, and visible jank breaks the same thing. (2) The §2 ban on praise/cheerleading copy is SDT-correct: contingent praise is an external reward that undermines intrinsic motivation, whereas blunt informational feedback ('bars 9-12, 71 BPM clean, 4 BPM up from Tuesday') satisfies competence directly. For autonomy, ensure the user chooses what to work on at some granularity even inside a structured session, and never make the app the reason they stop playing. Relatedness is the need Fingerly structurally cannot serve (no backend, no accounts) — accept that and lean harder on the other two rather than faking social features.
- **Verifier correction:** All five works exist with the stated identifiers, and the Half-Life detail is verbatim-correct — I pulled the Hunicke & Chapman paper directly: 'Hamlet is a Dynamic Difficulty Adjustment (DDA) system built using Valve's Half Life game engine' and 'The Hamlet system is primarily a set of libraries embedded in the Half Life game engine.' One sub-claim I could NOT verify from primary text: that the ACE '05 paper reports subjects did not perceive the changes AND explains this via change blindness. The ACE paper is paywalled (ACM DL returns 403) and its published abstract says only that it 'offers preliminary evaluation results which challenge common assumptions about player enjoyment and adjustment dynamics'. The change-blindness attribution is widely repeated in the secondary DDA literature but I could not source it to Hunicke's own words. Flag it as unverified or obtain the PDF before restating it as fact.
- **Replication:** This is the item most at risk of being over-rated, so: flow theory is a descriptive framework measured by self-report experience sampling, not a replicated quantitative law. The challenge-skill-balance premise specifically has mixed empirical support, and the various channel/quadrant models have never converged. GameFlow has no replication in the effect-size sense because it makes no quantitative predictions. Hunicke's DDA result is a small preliminary study that has not, to my knowledge, been independently replicated. Rated WEAK: the citations are all real and correctly described, but none of them constitute replicated evidence that covert DDA improves experience — they establish that the idea was formalised and prototyped.
- **Verifier notes:** Calibration on each source. GameFlow: confirmed at ACM CIE 3(3), July 2005, eight elements exactly as listed (concentration, challenge, skills, control, clear goals, feedback, immersion, social interaction), ~2,305 citations. But its own validation was expert reviews of exactly TWO real-time strategy games (one high-rated, one low-rated) — it is a heuristic evaluation checklist, never validated as a predictive model with effect sizes, and its citation count reflects convenience, not evidential weight. Hunicke ACE '05: the evaluation is self-described as 'preliminary'; the companion Hunicke & Chapman paper describes the player study as still prospective ('We will attach a simple heart-rate monitor to a variety of players...'), i.e. the empirical base is thin. Chen: confirmed, USC MFA 2006, presented at the GDC 2006 Experimental Gameplay Workshop; flOw is described as 'a practice and testbed for Embedded Difficulty Adjustment theory formulated in Jenova Chen's game design thesis' — the claim's 'player-choice branch' reading is accurate.

### The compulsion-loop literature and the loot-box evidence base establish where these mechanics tip into harm. The compulsion loop is a designed cycle of anticipation-action-reward-repeat, strengthened by a variable-ratio reinforcement schedule — the schedule Skinner showed produces the highest, most persistent response rates with no post-reinforcement pause. Zendle & Cairns (2018) surveyed 7,422 gamers and found spending on loot boxes associated with problem-gambling severity at η² = 0.054, versus η² = 0.004 for spending on non-randomised in-game items — an order-of-magnitude difference isolating the randomisation itself as the harmful component. The finding was replicated by the same authors in 2019. Schüll's 'Addiction by Design' documents the same design toolkit in machine gambling.

- **Status:** MISCHARACTERIZED · STRONG
- **Citation:** Zendle, D. & Cairns, P. (2018). 'Video game loot boxes are linked to problem gambling: Results of a large-scale survey.' PLOS ONE 13(11): e0206767 (plus published correction and a 2019 replication); Schüll, N. D. (2012). 'Addiction by Design: Machine Gambling in Las Vegas', Princeton University Press; Skinner, B. F., schedules of reinforcement
- **URL:** https://journals.plos.org/plosone/article?id=10.1371%2Fjournal.pone.0206767
- **Peer reviewed:** None
- **Sample / design:** Cross-sectional survey, n = 7,422 self-selected gamers, PGSI problem-gambling measure against self-reported loot-box spend, with a non-random in-game purchase condition as the discriminating comparison. Authors state explicitly that causal direction is UNRESOLVED — loot boxes may act as a gateway, or problem gamblers may be drawn to loot boxes. Correlational, self-selected sample; treat as a strong association, not a causal claim.
- **Effect size:** η² = 0.054 (loot-box spend vs problem gambling) against η² = 0.004 (other in-game spend). ~13x difference in variance explained.
- **Kind:** INVISIBLE_MECHANIC
- **Evidence type:** PEER_REVIEWED
- **Ethical status:** DARK_PATTERN where randomised rewards are paid for with real money. The compulsion loop as a bare structure (clear action → immediate feedback → visible progress → next action) is BENIGN and is in fact what a good practice tool is; what makes it predatory is (a) randomised outcomes, (b) real money, (c) artificial scarcity gating the action.
- **Mechanism:** Variable-ratio schedules are maximally resistant to extinction: because reward timing is unpredictable, absence of reward carries no information that the behaviour has stopped paying, so responding persists indefinitely. Layering a pity ceiling on top does not remove this — it adds a goal gradient. The Zendle comparison is the crucial evidence: it is specifically the randomisation, not the spending or the cosmetic reward, that carries the association with harm. That distinction is what makes it possible to keep the loop structure while removing the harm.
- **Piano-app application:** Build the loop, delete the variable ratio. Fingerly's loop should be action (play the passage) → immediate deterministic feedback (<15ms, per SPEC §1) → visible progress (tempo, accuracy, bars cleared) → next action, with a FIXED-ratio relationship between effort and outcome: the same performance always yields the same result. Never randomise reward magnitude, never introduce chance-based unlocks, never gate practice behind a metered resource. Concretely, the anti-pattern to avoid is 'clear a passage, get a random cosmetic' — replace with 'clear a passage at 80 BPM, the target becomes 84 BPM', which is the same loop with the uncertainty moved from an RNG into the user's actual skill. The uncertainty that makes practice compelling should be 'can I do it', not 'what will I get'.
- **Verifier correction:** The numbers are right; the inference is not. Verified: n = 7,422, eta-squared = 0.054 for loot box spending vs 0.004 for other microtransaction spending, PLOS ONE 13(11) e0206767, correction published 14 March 2019 (e0214167), and the 2019 replication is real — 'Loot boxes are again linked to problem gambling: Results of a replication study', PLOS ONE 14(3) e0213194, n = 1,172 (SMALLER than the original, not larger), eta-squared = 0.051 in a sample that 'did not self-select into a loot box study'. But 'an order-of-magnitude difference ISOLATING THE RANDOMISATION ITSELF as the harmful component' is an unsupported causal leap. Both studies are cross-sectional self-report surveys and the authors state plainly that 'it is impossible to understand the direction of the causal relationship'. Comparing two eta-squared values across two predictors in a correlational survey does not isolate a causal component; problem gamblers plausibly select into randomised spending. Restate as: loot box spending is more strongly associated with problem gambling than non-randomised spending is, replicated, direction of causation unknown.
- **Replication:** This is the one item that earns STRONG, but only for the narrow association. Direct replication by the original authors in 2019 (eta-squared 0.051 vs 0.054, and importantly in a non-self-selected sample, addressing the original's biggest confound). Independent meta-analytic confirmation: Garea, Drummond, Sauer, Hall & Williams (2021), 'Meta-analysis of the relationship between problem gambling, excessive gaming and loot box spending', International Gambling Studies 21(3), 460-479, finds a consistent association across studies. So: association replicated and meta-analytic = STRONG. Causal attribution to randomisation = not established by any of this. Rate the association STRONG and the mechanism claim UNVERIFIED.
- **Verifier notes:** Second and more serious problem: the Skinner framing is the exact folklore this review targets. Variable-ratio schedules producing high steady response rates and strong resistance to extinction is a real operant finding, but its application here is analogical, not measured — no one has demonstrated that any commercial compulsion loop operates on a VR schedule in humans. And it is self-refuting against this very claim set: pity systems (claims 1 and 2) are explicitly NOT variable-ratio. A hard guarantee at pull 90 or summon 120 bounds the ratio and makes the reinforcement increasingly predictable as the counter climbs — the opposite of the schedule Skinner showed maximises persistence. Calling pity-gated gacha a 'Skinner box on a variable-ratio schedule' is precisely wrong. Also: eta-squared = 0.054 is ~5% of variance, a small-to-medium effect; the authors' own defensible phrasing is that it is 'neither small, nor trivial' and larger than some established risk factors, which is a much narrower claim than the one made here. Schüll (2012, Princeton UP) is real and correctly characterised as ethnographic/documentary, not quantitative evidence.

---

## Visible UI/UX feedback design — "game feel" / "juice", reward-moment sequencing, and the timing numbers behind a moment of success

### The canonical juice demo establishes that juice is a *layer stack* applied to an unchanged simulation, and that the irreducible minimum is tweening/easing plus per-event variation — not particles. Jonasson & Purho rebuild a grey Breakout live by adding, roughly in order: tweened/eased motion (lerp, `a += (b-a)*0.1`), squash-and-stretch on paddle and ball, colour flashes, ball trails, particle bursts on brick break, screenshake, camera zoom/tilt/wobble, sound with rising pitch on consecutive hits, animated background, and score numbers that tween to their target rather than snapping.

- **Status:** MISCHARACTERIZED · WEAK
- **Citation:** Martin Jonasson & Petri Purho, "Juice it or lose it", Nordic Game Jam 2012 (live-coded Breakout demo, playable with a slider per effect)
- **URL:** https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Fy0aCDmgnxg
- **Peer reviewed:** None
- **Sample / design:** No study. Live demonstration with A/B toggles per effect; the persuasion is perceptual, not statistical. Widely replicated as a teaching exercise (e.g. Longwelwind's browser reproduction).
- **Effect size:** Not measured.
- **Kind:** BOTH
- **Evidence type:** INDUSTRY_TALK
- **Ethical status:** BENIGN
- **Concrete example:** The Breakout demo's score counter: the true score jumps instantly, but a separate display variable lerps toward it, so a 500-point hit shows as a visible spin-up. Same trick in Vampire Survivors' gold counter and Balatro's chip/mult scoring, where the count-up is the reward moment.
- **Mechanism:** Easing converts discrete state changes into continuous motion, which the visual system tracks as a single caused object rather than two unrelated frames. The rising-pitch chain turns repeated identical successes into a perceived *sequence* with direction, so the 5th hit feels like more than the 1st even though the game logic is identical.
- **Piano-app application:** Fingerly's cheapest high-yield win is easing everywhere the spec currently implies snapping: the accuracy/score readout at section end should tween up over ~400ms rather than appearing; the highway's hit-line marker should squash on note-on and ease back rather than toggling colour. The rising-pitch-on-consecutive-hits idea is the one to *avoid* directly — SPEC forbids audio synthesis for live play, and the user's actual piano is already producing pitch. Port the mechanic to the visual channel instead: consecutive correct notes raise the saturation/height of the hit-line glow monotonically, resetting on error. Note the zero-allocation rule: tweens must be struct-based/pooled, not allocating a Tween object per note.
- **Verifier correction:** The talk and the 'Juicy Breakout' demo are real (grapefrukt.com/f/games/juicy-breakout/, source at github.com/grapefrukt/juicy-breakout; also on GDC Vault). Detailed contemporaneous notes of the talk confirm: paddle squash/stretch on mouse speed, ball resize/stretch/wobble on impact, ball turns white on collision (colour flash), ball trajectory trail, smoke puffs and per-collision particles, blocks that fall/shrink/spin/darken, screen shake via Perlin noise, pitch changes on successive hits (harmonized), and background music. NOT confirmed in any documented account of the talk: the specific lerp formula `a += (b-a)*0.1`, camera zoom/tilt/wobble, or score numbers tweening to their target. The control is a TAB toggle menu, not 'a slider per effect'. Most importantly, the talk does not rank effects or argue any 'irreducible minimum' — that ordering-of-importance is the claimant's editorial gloss on an unmeasured live demo.
- **Replication:** N/A — no empirical study exists behind this talk. No one has run a controlled test of the specific claim that tweening beats particles.
- **Verifier notes:** Evidence type is a jam talk with zero measurement, so 'establishes' is the wrong verb — it demonstrates. The Vampire Survivors / Balatro count-up examples are the claimant's own product observations; neither studio has documented borrowing this from the talk. Note the claim's central thesis (more/faster layered feedback = better) is partially contradicted by Kao 2020 (inverted-U) and Kao et al. CHI 2024 (amplification reduced competence).

### Nijman's talk is the most actionable ordered checklist in the field: ~30 discrete tweaks applied one at a time to a dull shooter.
