The ones that are pure feedback (not balance) are, in his order: impact effects on collision, hit animation, enemy knockback, permanence (corpses/debris persist), camera lerp, camera offset toward facing direction, screen shake, player knockback, sleep/hit-stop (a momentary global freeze on hit), gun delay, gun kickback, shell casings, more bass in the impact sound, camera kick, bigger explosions, and smoke persistence.

- **Status:** CONFIRMED · MODERATE
- **Citation:** Jan Willem Nijman (Vlambeer), "The art of screenshake", INDIGO Classes 2013
- **URL:** https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=AJdEqssNZ-U
- **Peer reviewed:** None
- **Sample / design:** No study. Incremental live demonstration; each tweak is toggled and replayed for the audience.
- **Effect size:** Not measured. Note that several of the 30 are difficulty/balance changes (lower enemy HP, higher rate of fire, more enemies), not feedback — the talk deliberately conflates them, which is a confound if you cite it as evidence about feedback alone.
- **Kind:** BOTH
- **Evidence type:** INDUSTRY_TALK
- **Ethical status:** BENIGN
- **Concrete example:** Nuclear Throne: killed enemies leave corpses and blood decals that persist for the whole room, so a cleared room is visibly a battlefield. Downwell and Hotline Miami both use the same permanence trick.
- **Mechanism:** Two mechanisms. (1) Hit-stop and knockback insert a physical-feeling exchange of momentum, so the input reads as an *act on the world* rather than a state flag. (2) Permanence converts transient successes into an accumulating visible record — the player can see the whole session's work at a glance, which is a free competence signal costing zero UI.
- **Piano-app application:** Permanence is the single most transferable idea and Fingerly currently has no analogue. After a note is played correctly and scrolls past the hit line, don't delete it — leave a dimmed, coloured ghost trailing off the bottom/top of the highway so a completed passage leaves a visible wake of correct notes (green) and errors (red). Zero extra UI, zero text, and it satisfies the anti-praise rule because it *is* the blunt metric. Warning on screenshake: a note highway is a sight-reading surface, and shaking it displaces the notes the user is currently reading. Restrict camera punch to a non-reading region (the hit-line bar, the side chrome) or to moments where reading has stopped (section end).
- **Verifier correction:** No correction needed on the list. Independent transcriptions of the talk give the full ordered sequence (31 items): 1 animation, 2 lower TTK, 3 higher rate of fire, 4 more enemies, 5 bigger bullets, 6 muzzle flash, 7 faster bullets, 8 less accuracy, 9 impact effects, 10 hit animation, 11 enemy knockback, 12 permanence, 13 camera lerp, 14 camera position, 15 screen shake, 16 player knockback, 17 sleep, 18 gun delay, 19 gun kickback, 20 strafing, 21 shell casings, 22 more bass, 23 super machine gun, 24 random explosion, 25 faster enemies, 26 more enemies, 27 rate of fire + camera kick, 28 bigger explosions, 29 more permanence (smoke), 30 meaning/death animation, 31 balancing. Every one of the claim's 16 pure-feedback items appears, in the claimed relative order. Talk archived at archive.org/details/the-art-of-screenshake and youtube.com/watch?v=AJdEqssNZ-U.
- **Replication:** Nothing here has been tested experimentally. Kao et al. (CHI 2024, n=1699) found that stacking amplified feedback that fires on action rather than on success significantly REDUCED competence, so 'apply all 30' is not a safe reading of the checklist.
- **Verifier notes:** 'Most actionable checklist in the field' is opinion, not a finding. This is a conference talk with no measurement: MODERATE as evidence of professional practice, never as evidence of effect. The Nuclear Throne and Hotline Miami permanence examples are directly observable and correct. Downwell is a weak example — the camera scrolls down continuously and enemies burst into gems, so there is very little persistent debris to accumulate.

### Swink's framework separates game feel into three independently tunable layers — (1) real-time control, (2) simulated space, (3) polish — and crucially argues polish is a *separable* layer that changes perception without touching the simulation. He anchors "real-time" at a correction cycle (feedback → decide → act → new feedback) under ~100ms.

- **Status:** CONFIRMED · MODERATE
- **Citation:** Steve Swink, *Game Feel: A Game Designer's Guide to Virtual Sensation*, Morgan Kaufmann, 2008 (Ch. 1, "Defining Game Feel")
- **URL:** https://www.oreilly.com/library/view/game-feel/9780123743282/
- **Peer reviewed:** None
- **Sample / design:** Book-length design theory synthesising perceptual psychology (Gibson, proprioception) with design analysis. Not an experiment; the perceptual-psychology substrate it borrows from is peer-reviewed, the design claims are not.
- **Effect size:** Not measured. The <100ms correction-cycle figure is asserted from HCI convention (see Miller/Card), not independently established by Swink.
- **Kind:** BOTH
- **Evidence type:** INDUSTRY_TALK
- **Ethical status:** BENIGN
- **Concrete example:** Super Mario Bros.: the simulation (jump arc, collision) is unchanged across the game, but polish — the coin's rotating sprite, the 100-point popup, the ascending pitch of consecutive stomps — carries essentially all the felt reward.
- **Mechanism:** Separating polish from simulation means feel can be iterated without regression-testing gameplay. The 100ms figure matters because below it the player perceives their own action and the system response as one event and attributes the outcome to themselves (sense of agency); above it, attribution weakens.
- **Piano-app application:** This validates SPEC §1's <15ms keypress→visual budget as the load-bearing requirement, not a nice-to-have: it is the precondition for every other item on this list. Architecturally, build the polish layer as a pure function of pooled event structs emitted by the MIDI pipeline, so juice can be swapped/tuned (and A/B'd on the latency test screen) without touching :core. Test the correction cycle end-to-end, not just the render path — the USB MIDI stack, not the renderer, is where the 15ms is most likely to be lost.
- **Verifier correction:** Book, publisher, year and chapter title all verify. Swink's definition is 'real-time control of virtual objects in a simulated space, with interactions emphasised by polish'; the three building blocks are as stated; polish is defined as effects that enhance the interaction 'without changing the underlying simulation'; and real-time response is anchored at a correction cycle (read feedback → decide → act → new feedback) under 100 ms. Two framing overreaches: Swink calls these 'building blocks', not 'independently tunable layers', and his argument is that all three must combine for game feel to exist — 'independently tunable' is the claimant's addition.
- **Replication:** No replication concept applies — the framework is definitional. The ~100ms figure descends from the same Miller/Card lineage as claim 7 and inherits its weakness: modern touch studies put simultaneity thresholds well below 100 ms (e.g. Jota et al., CHI 2013, ~64 ms for touch-visual).
- **Verifier notes:** This is a craft book synthesising designer intuition and secondary psychology, not an empirical study; MODERATE is the ceiling. The Super Mario Bros. example is the weakest part: the assertion that polish 'carries essentially all the felt reward' is unsupported and runs against Swink's own Mario analysis, which centres on the control layer (input response, jump arcs, acceleration curves), not on coin sprites and score popups.

### An empirical ranking of action games by "impact feel" found three of nineteen candidate features to be make-or-break: hit stop, sound coherence, and camera control. Absence of dedicated design on any *one* of these three was enough to sink perceived impact, even when the other sixteen were present.

- **Status:** MISCHARACTERIZED · WEAK
- **Citation:** Zhonghao Lin, Haihan Duan, Zikai Alex Wen, Wei Cai, "What Features Influence Impact Feel? A Study of Impact Feedback in Action Games", IEEE CTSoc GEM 2022
- **URL:** https://arxiv.org/abs/2208.06155
- **Peer reviewed:** None
- **Sample / design:** Mined Steam top-seller reviews, trained an NLP model to rank action games on impact feel, then hand-coded a 19-feature impact-feedback framework across the top 8 vs bottom 8 ranked games. Observational/corpus-based, not a controlled experiment — it establishes co-occurrence, not causation, and the feature coding is by the authors.
- **Effect size:** Reported as presence/absence inventory across 16 games, not as a standardised effect size. Treat as directional.
- **Kind:** BOTH
- **Evidence type:** PEER_REVIEWED
- **Ethical status:** BENIGN
- **Concrete example:** Concrete durations from shipped games: Super Smash Bros. Ultimate computes hitlag as ⌊(damage×0.65 + 6) × multipliers⌋ frames — a 15% move gives 15 frames (~250ms at 60fps), vs 8 frames in Melee — capped at 30 frames; electric attacks get a 1.5× multiplier. Street Fighter 6 scales hitstop with attack weight and extends it further on Punish Counters.
- **Mechanism:** Hit stop is a supernormal congruence cue: freezing both actor and target for a few frames makes the collision the only moving-picture event, forcing attention onto the causal link. Sound coherence (impact sound matching impact weight) exploits audiovisual binding. Camera control ensures the responsible action stays framed.
- **Piano-app application:** Hit-stop is the highest-value borrow and the most dangerous: freezing a *scrolling note highway* stops the music, which is unacceptable during play. Apply hit-stop only to the effect layer, not the scroll — on a correct note, freeze the note's own sprite and its burst for 2–4 frames at 120Hz (17–33ms) while the highway keeps scrolling. Scale the freeze with note difficulty (interval leap, hand-crossing, tempo) exactly as Smash scales with damage, so hard notes feel heavier than easy ones. "Camera control" maps to keeping the highway's vertical anchor stable — do not let the hit line drift. "Sound coherence" is Fingerly's structural gap: the impact sound comes from the user's real piano and can never be manipulated, so all congruence work has to happen in the visual channel and be tightly time-locked to the acoustic onset.
- **Verifier correction:** The paper exists (arXiv:2208.06155, IEEE GEM 2022) but the author order is wrong — it is Lin, Wen, Duan, Cai. More seriously, the method cannot support the causal claim. There was no experiment and no participants: the authors scraped Steam reviews of top-seller action games, trained an NLP model to RANK games on impact feel, then the authors themselves inventoried their own 19-feature framework across the top 8 and bottom 8 games. n = 16 games, judged by the framework's own authors. The paper's actual wording is hedged twice over: hit stop, sound coherence and camera control 'MAY strongly influence' impact feel and a lack of dedicated design on one of the three 'MAY ruin' it. The claim converts two 'may's into 'was enough to sink', and converts a 16-game feature inventory into an 'empirical ranking' with causal force.
- **Replication:** No replication. The three-feature finding has not been tested experimentally by anyone, including the authors. The Smash Bros. hitlag figures in the EXAMPLE are separately accurate and well-documented in community frame data (SmashWiki): Ultimate base hitlag = floor((damage x 0.65 + 6) x multipliers), so a 15% move = 15 frames (250ms at 60fps); Melee = floor(damage/3 + 3), so 15% = 8 frames; cap is 30 frames in Ultimate; electric multiplier is 1.5x. But that is datamined community documentation, not a developer statement, and it evidences nothing about the paper's conclusion.
- **Verifier notes:** Peer-reviewed venue, but the design is a descriptive content analysis with a sample of 16 and no manipulation, no control, and no measurement of any player. Treat as hypothesis generation.

### Juiciness has an inverted-U dose-response, not a monotonic one. In the largest juiciness study to date, Medium and High juiciness beat both No juiciness and Extreme juiciness on player experience, intrinsic motivation, play time, and in-game performance. More juice past a point makes people play worse and enjoy it less.

- **Status:** CONFIRMED · MODERATE
- **Citation:** Dominic Kao, "The effects of juiciness in an action RPG", Entertainment Computing 34, 100472, 2020
- **URL:** https://www.sciencedirect.com/science/article/pii/S1875952118300879
- **Peer reviewed:** None
- **Sample / design:** N = 3,018, between-subjects, four versions of an identical action RPG differing only in visual/audio effect density (No / Medium / High / Extreme). Measures: validated PX and intrinsic-motivation scales, plus behavioural play time and in-game performance.
- **Effect size:** Direction consistently replicated across all four dependent measures; the paper reports significant differences favouring Medium/High. Per-measure effect sizes are modest — the reliable claim is the shape of the curve, not its steepness.
- **Kind:** BOTH
- **Evidence type:** PEER_REVIEWED
- **Ethical status:** BENIGN
- **Concrete example:** Contrast Candy Crush's tuned cascade (bursts sized so the board stays readable) with the common indie failure of full-screen bloom + shake on every hit, where the player loses track of enemy positions.
- **Mechanism:** Beyond a threshold, feedback competes with the task for foveal attention and occludes the signal the player needs to act on. Performance drops, competence drops, and enjoyment follows competence.
- **Piano-app application:** Ship juice behind a single 0–3 intensity setting and default it to 2, not 3 — and make the latency test screen double as the juice-tuning screen. The inverted U is sharper for Fingerly than for an action RPG, because the primary task (reading notes ahead of the hit line) is *more* attention-hungry than aiming. Concretely: cap concurrent particles so a 6-note chord does not obscure the next bar of notes, and never let an effect render above the read-ahead region of the highway.
- **Verifier correction:** Study is real (Elsevier pii S1875952118300879) with N=3018 across four otherwise-identical action RPG builds (None / Medium / High / Extreme). Reported: both None and Extreme significantly decreased play time, player experience and performance (final character level, total attacks, monsters defeated, total actions); Extreme significantly decreased intrinsic motivation; Medium and High outperform None and Extreme across measures. Two softenings: 'inverted-U dose-response' is an interpretation of four ordinal conditions, not a fitted curve — the paper does not distinguish Medium from High or locate an optimum. 'Largest juiciness study to date' was defensible at publication but is a superlative the paper does not make.
- **Replication:** Single online experiment, not independently replicated. Kao's own later work (CHI 2024) complicates it: there, amplified feedback reduced competence relative to a plain control, which is not what a simple 'more juice up to a point is better' reading predicts. The Candy Crush / indie-bloom contrast in the EXAMPLE is the claimant's product observation, not from the study.
- **Verifier notes:** I could not retrieve the article page (ScienceDirect 403) to independently confirm volume 34 / article 100472 or the effect sizes; the DOI/pii is confirmed real. Effect sizes were not verifiable, which matters — with N=3018, statistical significance is nearly free and the practical size of the play-time difference is the thing that would actually justify design decisions.

### The strongest causal evidence available says *amplification is not the active ingredient — success-contingency is*. In a pre-registered n=1,699 experiment, amplified feedback that fired on mere action significantly REDUCED competence (−0.43, p<.001) and effectance (−0.19, p<.05) versus a plain control, while making the same amplified feedback fire only on success raised competence (+0.45, p<.001), effectance (+0.26, p<.001), curiosity (+0.29, p<.001) and voluntary playtime. The authors diagnose the failure as *occlusion*: the swing effect visually engulfed the subsequent hit/kill effects, so players could no longer bind outcome to action.

- **Status:** MISCHARACTERIZED · MODERATE
- **Citation:** Dominic Kao, Nick Ballou, Kathrin Gerling, Heiko Breitsohl, Sebastian Deterding, "How does Juicy Game Feedback Motivate? Testing Curiosity, Competence, and Effectance", CHI 2024
- **URL:** https://people.csail.mit.edu/dkao/pdf/3613904.3642656.pdf
- **Peer reviewed:** None
- **Sample / design:** Pre-registered, n=1,699 (Prolific), between-subjects 2×2+control action RPG: amplification × success-dependence × variability. Validated scales (PXI mastery/curiosity, IMI interest/enjoyment, Klimmt effectance) plus free-choice playtime after 10 min mandatory. Structural equation models, good fit (CFI .977, RMSEA .048). Powered to detect β=.1 at ~80–90%. Challenge and prior experience checked as non-confounds.
- **Effect size:** Unstandardised, on 7-pt scales: amplification → competence −0.43***, → effectance −0.19*. Success-dependence → competence +0.45***, effectance +0.26***, curiosity +0.29***. Curiosity → enjoyment +0.75/0.76***; curiosity → voluntary playtime +0.86–0.99*** (the only significant playtime predictor). Random variability → no effect on curiosity; only +0.11* on enjoyment.
- **Kind:** BOTH
- **Evidence type:** PEER_REVIEWED
- **Ethical status:** BENIGN
- **Concrete example:** The failing condition was a large light-glow weapon-swing animation that overlapped and hid the separate hit and enemy-death effects — three consecutive attacks became visually indistinguishable from one.
- **Mechanism:** Outcome binding — the low-level cognitive attribution "my action caused this event" — is a precondition for felt competence. Feedback that fires regardless of success, or that visually covers the success signal, severs the binding. Randomised variety does not help because it is not *learnable*, so it affords no reducible uncertainty and hence no curiosity.
- **Piano-app application:** This is the most important finding for Fingerly and it overturns the naive design. (1) Never emit key-press feedback that fires on *any* key — juice must be strictly contingent on a correct note at the correct time; a wrong note gets a different, quieter treatment, never the success burst. (2) Guard against occlusion in the fast-passage case: at 16th notes in a run, per-note bursts will overlap and the player will stop being able to tell which notes were right. Budget effect lifetime against note density — shrink or merge bursts when inter-onset interval drops below the effect duration. (3) Curiosity, not enjoyment, predicted voluntary time-on-task; "learnably generated" variation beats random. So vary the burst by *something the user can learn to read* — interval size, hand, dynamic level — never by rand(). (4) The authors explicitly warn against evaluating juice by self-reported enjoyment alone; measure voluntary practice minutes after the session's required block ends.
- **Verifier correction:** Every number verifies exactly against the authors' own preprint (osf.io/download/f83dp): amplification -> effectance -.19 (p<.05) and competence -.43 (p<.001) vs the standard condition; success-dependence -> competence +.45 (p<.001), effectance +.26 (p<.001), curiosity +.29 (p<.001); pre-registered, n=1699. The occlusion diagnosis is verbatim the authors' — 'the large light glow ball of the Weapon Swing animation often visually engulfs and occludes subsequent triggers of the same animation as well as separate animation effects for hitting or killing an opponent'. Three corrections. (1) '+voluntary playtime' overstates: the paper states plainly 'we found no significant direct effects of success-dependence on enjoyment or voluntary engagement'; playtime means were 14.76/14.19 min vs 13.65/13.47 min, descriptive only. (2) The coefficients are UNSTANDARDISED — points on a 7-pt Likert scale — so -.43 is ~0.4 scale points (competence M 5.19 -> 4.80), a small effect, not a standardized beta of -0.43. (3) The headline 'amplification is not the active ingredient — success-contingency is' is stronger than the authors' own reading: they attribute the amplification result to an unintended confound in their specific stimulus, i.e. the amplification condition failed because of a visual-design accident, not because amplification per se is inert.
- **Replication:** Not replicated. The authors explicitly frame the amplification result as needing follow-up with a non-occluding amplified condition. The reversal versus the practitioner consensus (claims 1 and 2) is the most interesting thing here and should be reported as an open question, not a settled one.
- **Verifier notes:** This is the best-designed study in the whole set — pre-registered, large, SEM with a plain control — and it is still one experiment with a self-diagnosed stimulus confound. 'The strongest causal evidence available' is fair; 'says amplification is not the active ingredient' is not.

### Three response-time thresholds, stable for ~55 years, bound all feedback timing: 0.1s = perceived as instantaneous (no feedback needed beyond the result), 1.0s = flow of thought preserved though the delay is noticed, 10s = attention limit. Industry motion systems land animation durations well inside the first band-to-second band: Material Design specifies 200–300ms for mobile, 150–200ms desktop, ~30% longer (400–450ms) for tablets, with asymmetric easing curves.

- **Status:** MISCHARACTERIZED · WEAK
- **Citation:** Robert B. Miller, "Response time in man-computer conversational transactions", AFIPS Fall Joint Computer Conference 1968; Stuart Card et al. 1991; popularised in Jakob Nielsen, *Usability Engineering*, 1993 / NN/g "Response Times: The 3 Important Limits"; Material Design 3 "Easing and duration"
- **URL:** https://www.nngroup.com/articles/response-times-3-important-limits/
- **Peer reviewed:** None
- **Sample / design:** Miller 1968 is a peer-reviewed conference synthesis of response-time psychology across task types; Card et al. 1991 replicated the productivity effects. The Material durations are a design-system convention, NOT experimentally derived — label those INDUSTRY_TALK.
- **Effect size:** Thresholds are perceptual boundaries, not effect sizes. Card et al. found measurable productivity gains from sub-second response.
- **Kind:** VISIBLE_UI
- **Evidence type:** PEER_REVIEWED
- **Ethical status:** BENIGN
- **Concrete example:** iOS/Android keyboard key-pop is well under 100ms and reads as the finger causing the pop; a 400ms CSS button transition reads as the *page* responding, not the click.
- **Mechanism:** Below ~100ms, action and response fall inside the window where the perceptual system fuses them into one event and the user attributes causation to themselves. Between 100ms and 1s the user notices the machine as a separate agent but does not lose the thread. Past 1s, working memory starts having to hold the intent.
- **Piano-app application:** Split Fingerly's timing budget by band. Band 1 (<15ms, per SPEC §1, tighter than Nielsen's 100ms because it's a musical instrument): note-on visual response — hit-line flash, note sprite state change. Band 2 (100–300ms): the burst/particle lifetime and squash-return. Band 3 (300ms–1s): score/accuracy tween at section end. Nothing in the practice loop should occupy band 4. Note that the 400–450ms "tablet" recommendation is a screen-size convention about travel distance, not about the highway — do not slow the hit-line response to match it.
- **Verifier correction:** The 0.1/1/10 thresholds and the Miller/Card/Nielsen lineage are real and correctly worded (Nielsen: 0.1s = 'the user feel[s] that the system is reacting instantaneously... no special feedback is necessary except to display the result'). The Material Design attribution is wrong: 200-300ms mobile / 150-200ms desktop / +30% = 400-450ms tablet / -30% wearable comes from Material Design 1 (m1.material.io, 'Duration & easing'). Material Design 3's 'Easing and duration' page replaced that with abstract duration tokens (short/medium/long/extra-long, roughly 50-1000ms) and gives no per-device-class numbers. Citing MD3 for MD1 figures is a real sourcing error, and the MD1 numbers are house style with no empirical derivation published.
- **Replication:** The 0.1s figure has NOT held up as a perceptual floor, which is the crux. Kaaresoja's Glasgow thesis says of Miller's 100ms recommendation that the work 'does not give much evidence or details about the derivation of this result'. Modern direct-touch work puts the touch-visual simultaneity threshold at ~64 ms (Jota et al., CHI 2013) and Ng et al. (2012) found users exposed to genuinely low latency then judged commercial device latency unacceptable. 'Stable for 55 years' describes citation persistence, not empirical confirmation — treat 0.1s as an upper bound on acceptability, not a threshold below which nothing is perceptible.
- **Verifier notes:** 'Peer reviewed' is generous. Miller 1968 is a conference position/review paper; Nielsen 1993 is a trade book; the Material guidance is vendor style. The claim's own EXAMPLE contains the same problem — a 400ms CSS transition 'reading as the page responding' is folk UX, not a measured finding.

### Feedback latency thresholds are modality-specific and much tighter for touch than for vision. Empirically derived guidelines for a touchscreen virtual button: tactile feedback 5–50ms, audio 20–70ms, visual 30–85ms after the press. Bimodal combinations shift the budgets (visual 100 / tactile 55ms when paired). Separately, a constant 18ms tactile delay with minimal jitter produced faster, less error-prone number entry than conditions with wider delay *variability* — jitter hurt more than latency.

- **Status:** MISCHARACTERIZED · MODERATE
- **Citation:** Topi Kaaresoja, "Latency Guidelines for Touchscreen Virtual Button Feedback", PhD thesis, University of Glasgow, 2016; Kaaresoja, Brewster & Lantz, "Towards the Temporally Perfect Virtual Button", ACM Transactions on Applied Perception 11(2), 2014; Kaaresoja, Anttila & Hoggan, World Haptics 2011
- **URL:** https://theses.gla.ac.uk/7075/
- **Peer reviewed:** None
- **Sample / design:** Series of lab studies with a purpose-built measurement rig (high-speed camera + microphone + accelerometer) to ground-truth actual device latencies. Simultaneity-perception and perceived-quality thresholds established for unimodal and bimodal feedback. Note: derived on mobile phone virtual buttons, so generalisation to a MIDI keyboard (a real physical key with its own mechanical feedback) is an extrapolation.
- **Effect size:** Thresholds, not effect sizes. The 2011 study reports significant speed and error advantages for the low-jitter 18ms condition.
- **Kind:** INVISIBLE_MECHANIC
- **Evidence type:** PEER_REVIEWED
- **Ethical status:** BENIGN
- **Concrete example:** Hoggan, Brewster & Johnston (CHI 2008) used 30ms vibration for a correct key press vs 500ms for a key-slip on a touchscreen keyboard; haptics significantly reduced *uncorrected* errors and raised perceived performance even where raw entry speed was unchanged.
- **Mechanism:** Tactile and proprioceptive channels have finer temporal resolution than vision, so the same absolute lag is detectable in touch and invisible in sight. Jitter is worse than constant lag because the motor system can calibrate to a fixed delay but cannot predict a variable one — unpredictable delay is what destroys sense of agency.
- **Piano-app application:** Two direct consequences. (1) SPEC §1's <15ms is the right target but *jitter* deserves its own acceptance criterion — measure p99 and standard deviation of keypress→photon on the in-app latency screen, not just the mean, because the evidence says variance is what users feel. (2) Fingerly's user is touching a real piano key, not the tablet, so tablet haptics are largely wasted during play — the key's own escapement already provides tactile confirmation, and a tablet buzz would arrive uncorrelated with the finger. Reserve haptics for tablet-touch interactions (menu, session start/end, the metronome count-in if the tablet is within reach) where the 5–50ms budget applies.
- **Verifier correction:** I read the thesis directly (theses.gla.ac.uk/7075/). Unimodal guidelines verify verbatim: tactile 5-50ms, audio 20-70ms, visual 30-85ms. Bimodal guidelines verify verbatim and the claim quotes them correctly: visual-tactile -> visual should not exceed 100ms and tactile should not exceed 55ms (for completeness: visual-audio -> visual <=90ms, audio 20-70ms; tactile-audio -> tactile <=25ms, audio <=100ms). The 18ms sentence is wrong. The thesis's own account of Kaaresoja, Anttila & Hoggan (2011): 'They found that the text entry and error rates were NOT affected when the latency between touch and tactile feedback was constant and in the range of 18 to 118 ms. However, there was a trend that the higher latencies were subjectively rated lowest. The subjective satisfaction dropped most when a virtual QWERTY keyboard was used where the latency was different on every key press.' So performance (speed and errors) did not differ at all across 18-118ms; only SUBJECTIVE SATISFACTION favoured low and constant latency. 'Faster, less error-prone number entry' is contradicted by the source. The jitter point survives only as a satisfaction effect, and that study used a RESISTIVE touchscreen, which the thesis itself flags as unrepresentative of modern capacitive devices.
- **Replication:** These are guidelines synthesised from a single researcher's thesis programme (small lab samples, n in the tens, one device family), not a replicated literature. No independent lab has reproduced the exact numeric bands. Use them as engineering budgets, not as established perceptual constants.
- **Verifier notes:** Also flag the EXAMPLE: the thesis attributes a 30ms 175Hz square-wave TACTILE stimulus to Hoggan et al. 2008a, and separately attributes 30ms press / 300ms fingertip-over / 500ms slip stimuli to AUDIO Earcons in Hoggan et al. 2009. The '30ms vibration vs 500ms for key-slip' pairing attributed to CHI 2008 looks like a conflation of a tactile parameter from one paper with audio Earcon durations from another. The CHI 2008 headline finding — tactile feedback significantly improved finger-based text entry on touchscreens — is real.

### Audiovisual congruence has an asymmetric tolerance: the broadcast detectability threshold is audio leading video by 45ms or lagging by 125ms (ITU-R BT.1359-1), with acceptability out to +90/−185ms. Late audio is forgiven far more than early audio. The underlying perceptual temporal binding window is wide (typically 100–300ms+) and narrows with training.

- **Status:** CONFIRMED · MODERATE
- **Citation:** ITU-R Recommendation BT.1359-1, "Relative Timing of Sound and Vision for Broadcasting", 1998; Powers, Hillock & Wallace, "Perceptual Training Narrows the Temporal Window of Multisensory Binding", Journal of Neuroscience 29(39):12265, 2009
- **URL:** https://standards.globalspec.com/std/401947/itu-r-bt-1359
- **Peer reviewed:** None
- **Sample / design:** BT.1359 is a standards-body recommendation derived from subjective assessment panels — treat as an engineering standard, not a single study. Powers et al. is a controlled perceptual-training experiment showing the binding window narrowed from >300ms toward ~115ms after training.
- **Effect size:** Detectability ±45/125ms; acceptability ±90/185ms. Training narrowed the window by roughly a factor of two to three in Powers et al.
- **Kind:** INVISIBLE_MECHANIC
- **Evidence type:** PEER_REVIEWED
- **Ethical status:** BENIGN
- **Concrete example:** Rhythm games exploit this hard: Beat Saber and osu! ship a per-user audio/visual offset calibration screen precisely because the tolerable window varies per person and per hardware chain.
- **Mechanism:** The brain expects sound to arrive after light (sound travels slower), so audio lag is ecologically valid and gets bound; audio *leading* violates physics and breaks the binding much sooner. Trained observers — musicians especially — have measurably narrower windows, so they detect asynchrony civilians miss.
- **Piano-app application:** Two things. (1) Fingerly's user is a *trained* observer in exactly Powers et al.'s sense — a pianist practising has an unusually narrow binding window for note-onset timing, so assume the tight end (~45ms lead / ~100ms lag), not the broadcast comfort figures. (2) The asymmetry says which way to err: visual feedback that arrives slightly *after* the piano's acoustic onset is far safer than visual feedback that could ever anticipate it. Since the acoustic sound is produced by the instrument at true zero latency and the MIDI event arrives after it, Fingerly is structurally in the safe direction — but a predictive/optimistic renderer that flashes the hit line *before* the MIDI confirms would land in the forbidden lead region. Do not do that.
- **Verifier correction:** ITU-R BT.1359-1 verifies exactly: 'detectability thresholds are about +45 ms to -125 ms and acceptability thresholds are about +90 ms to -185 ms on the average, where a positive value indicates that sound is advanced with respect to vision.' The asymmetry and its direction are correct. Powers, Hillock & Wallace is real and correctly cited (J Neurosci 30 Sept 2009, 29(39):12265-12274); training via a simultaneity-judgment task with feedback narrowed the window by ~40%. One scope caveat the claim omits: BT.1359-1 is a broadcast standard for PASSIVE viewing, derived from subjective assessment panels. It is not a bound on interactive feedback, where the user's own motor act supplies a timing anchor and thresholds are much tighter (cf. ~64ms touch-visual simultaneity, Jota et al. CHI 2013). Importing broadcast tolerances into game/instrument feedback budgets is a domain jump.
- **Replication:** The Powers et al. training effect has been followed up (e.g. generalization-of-multisensory-perceptual-learning work in Sci Rep) and the general finding that the binding window is plastic is well supported. The Beat Saber / osu! per-user calibration screens are verifiable product facts and are good evidence that practitioners treat the window as individual and hardware-dependent.
- **Verifier notes:** MODERATE rather than STRONG: the ITU figure is a standards-body average from assessment panels, and the TBW literature, while large, has wide between-study and between-individual variance (the '100-300ms+' range is itself an admission of that).

### Two robust and separable progress effects. GOAL GRADIENT: effort accelerates as the goal nears — café cardholders bought coffee progressively faster the closer they got to a free one, and slowed down again immediately after redeeming ('post-reward reset'). ENDOWED PROGRESS: giving artificial pre-credit toward the same real amount of work nearly doubles completion — a 10-stamp card pre-stamped twice yielded 34% redemption vs 19% for an 8-stamp card starting empty, with identical real effort, and completers were faster.

- **Status:** MISCHARACTERIZED · MODERATE
- **Citation:** Ran Kivetz, Oleg Urminsky & Yuhuang Zheng, "The Goal-Gradient Hypothesis Resurrected: Purchase Acceleration, Illusionary Goal Progress, and Customer Retention", Journal of Marketing Research 43(1):39–58, 2006; Joseph C. Nunes & Xavier Drèze, "The Endowed Progress Effect: How Artificial Advancement Increases Effort", Journal of Consumer Research 32(4):504–512, 2006
- **URL:** https://home.uchicago.edu/ourminsky/Goal-Gradient_Illusionary_Goal_Progress.pdf
- **Peer reviewed:** None
- **Sample / design:** Kivetz et al.: real café loyalty field experiment plus secondary customer data, paper-and-pencil studies, Tobit/logit models. Nunes & Drèze: field experiment at a car wash, plus lab replications. Both are consumer-behaviour field work — external validity to a solo practice app is an assumption, and the incentive (free coffee/car wash) is extrinsic, unlike practice.
- **Effect size:** Endowed progress: 34% vs 19% completion (~1.8× lift). Goal gradient: significant inter-purchase-time reduction approaching reward, plus significant post-reward slowdown.
- **Kind:** VISIBLE_UI
- **Evidence type:** PEER_REVIEWED
- **Ethical status:** NEEDS_CARE
- **Concrete example:** LinkedIn's profile-strength meter credits ~25–30% simply for name/email/headline. Starbucks Rewards shows the star count filling toward a reward. Duolingo shows a streak flame lit after lesson one.
- **Mechanism:** Progress is evaluated against the *fraction* remaining, not the absolute amount, so the same unit of work buys more perceived progress near the end. Endowing progress reframes the reference point from 'starting' to 'already committed', recruiting consistency motivation on top of the gradient.
- **Piano-app application:** Fingerly's sessions are closed-ended by SPEC, which is the right substrate for a gradient — but the design must not become a compliance device. Safe version: within a session, show a segment bar for the passage being drilled, and *front-load* it — credit the warm-up/count-in as the first segment so the bar is never at zero when real work starts (endowed progress, honestly earned). Dangerous version to avoid: a cross-day streak that punishes missing a day. The post-reward reset finding predicts a real failure mode — motivation drops immediately after a milestone is hit, so never end a session at the exact moment a bar completes; the completion should sit ~80% through the session, with the remaining work already visibly begun. Flag: this whole family sits closest to dark-pattern territory and should be justified per-use, not adopted wholesale.
- **Verifier correction:** Both papers exist and are cited correctly, and the numbers are right: ~300 car wash customers, 10-stamp-with-2-pre-stamped vs empty 8-stamp, 34% vs 19% completion over 9 months, identical real effort, faster completion among completers. Kivetz et al. do report café purchase acceleration near the reward and a post-reward reset. The overreach is 'robust'. (a) Endowed progress rests on ONE field study with ~150 per cell and base rates of 19%/34% — the interval around a 15-point difference at that n is wide, and I found no independent direct replication in 20 years. (b) The goal-gradient café evidence is observational panel data analysed with Tobit/logit models, not a randomised manipulation; it carries an obvious selection confound (customers who reach card 9 are by construction the heavy buyers), which the authors address statistically but cannot design away. (c) These are 2006 consumer-behaviour findings from a subfield with a documented replication problem; absence of failed replications here reflects absence of replication attempts, not confirmation.
- **Replication:** No direct replication of Nunes & Drèze located. The goal gradient itself has deep animal-learning roots (Hull 1932) that ARE robust; the contested step is the human/consumer extension and especially the 'illusionary goal progress' variant. LinkedIn profile-strength, Starbucks stars and Duolingo streaks are real observable product patterns, but no public documentation shows they were designed FROM these papers — that inference is the claimant's.
- **Verifier notes:** ETHICS NEEDS_CARE is correct and arguably understated — endowed progress works precisely by manufacturing a sense of progress the user did not earn. For a personal practice app that is defensible; for anything monetised it is a dark pattern in embryo.

### Perceived duration of a progress indicator is manipulable by ~10% without changing real duration, and the rules are specific: progress functions that ACCELERATE toward the end are perceived as significantly faster than linear, while pauses — especially pauses near completion — are perceived as significantly slower. A separate study found progress bars with backwards-moving, decelerating ribbing reduced perceived duration by 11% versus a solid bar.

- **Status:** CONFIRMED · MODERATE
- **Citation:** Chris Harrison, Brian Amento, Stacey Kuznetsov & Robert Bell, "Rethinking the Progress Bar", UIST 2007, pp. 115–118; Chris Harrison, Zhiquan Yeo & Scott E. Hudson, "Faster Progress Bars: Manipulating Perceived Duration with Visual Augmentations", CHI 2010
- **URL:** https://chrisharrison.net/projects/progressbars/ProgBarHarrison.pdf
- **Peer reviewed:** None
- **Sample / design:** UIST 2007: N=22 (14M/8F, mean age 37), 45 paired forced-choice trials, all bars held at a constant 5.5s. Nine progress functions; logistic regression controlling for a measured order bias (first-shown preferred 38% vs 26%). CHI 2010: N=20 (mean age 23) for the pulsation study, plus follow-up head-to-heads and a final quantitative measurement. Caveats: small N, short 5s durations, and a strong first-shown order effect that had to be modelled out.
- **Effect size:** UIST 2007: nine functions clustered into three groups significantly separated at p<.05 — Power and Fast Power (accelerating, exponential) perceived faster than linear; Slow Wavy, Fast Wavy and Late Pause (pauses near the end) perceived slower. CHI 2010: 11% reduction in perceived duration, statistically significant.
- **Kind:** VISIBLE_UI
- **Evidence type:** PEER_REVIEWED
- **Ethical status:** NEEDS_CARE
- **Concrete example:** The authors' own recommendation: cache progress early so you can spend it covering later stalls, i.e. deliberately under-report at the start and accelerate to the finish. macOS's striped, animated progress bar is the ancestor of the CHI 2010 ribbing condition.
- **Mechanism:** Peak-and-end effects (Fredrickson & Kahneman): remembered duration is dominated by the most salient moment and the final moment, not the integral. An accelerating finish supplies a favourable 'end'; a late stall supplies both a bad peak and a bad end. The ribbing effect works via induced motion — backwards ribbing makes the fill appear to advance faster than it does.
- **Piano-app application:** Directly applicable to Fingerly's session/section progress bar, and it is honest here in a way it is not in a file-copy dialog, because a practice session's 'work remaining' is genuinely a designer's choice. Concretely: make the last 20% of a section's bar advance visibly faster than the first 20%, and never let the bar stall near completion — if a passage is being repeated because of errors, do not freeze the bar (worst case per the study), let it creep. This is also the empirical answer to 'near-completion acceleration': it is real, it is a *perceptual* effect on remembered duration, and it is ~10% in size — meaningful but not magic.
- **Verifier correction:** Both verified from the authors' own PDFs. UIST 2007 (pp. 115-118 correct): 9 progress functions, all fixed at 5.5 seconds, n=22, paired forced-choice. The two functions perceived faster than linear were Power and Fast Power, both exponential 'with the fastest progress occurring near the end'; the three perceived slower were Slow Wavy, Fast Wavy and Late Pause — 'the only functions with pauses near the process conclusion'. The authors' recommendations verify verbatim: 'An intelligent progress bar can cache progress when the operation is first starting to mitigate negative progress behaviors... later on' and 'progress can be downplayed in the beginning and accelerated towards the end'. CHI 2010 verifies: 'Progress bars with animated ribbing that move backwards in a decelerating manner proved to have the strongest effect... it reduces the perceived duration among our participants by 11%' versus solid-colour bars. One precision fix: the ~10-11% magnitude comes ONLY from CHI 2010. UIST 2007 reports no percentage at all — it is a preference ranking, with no duration estimation measure.
- **Replication:** Partially followed up rather than replicated — later work (e.g. Sci Rep 2022 on progress bars and throbbers, and HCI work on manipulating animation speed) continues in the same direction, but no direct replication of either study's specific magnitudes with the original stimuli. Effect is real but small and cosmetic: 11% of perceived duration on a ~5s bar.
- **Verifier notes:** Both are small lab studies on 5.5-second bars. The UIST authors flag the scaling question themselves as future work: 'many operations that employ progress bars have considerably longer runtimes. It would be interesting to investigate if our findings scale to other durations.' The macOS-striped-bar-as-ancestor line is the claimant's, not the papers'. NEEDS_CARE is right: 'deliberately under-report at the start' is lying to the user about system state, which is fine for a cosmetic shimmer and not fine if the user is making decisions on the estimate.

### The Zeigarnik effect — better recall of interrupted than completed tasks, routinely cited to justify incomplete-progress UI — does NOT replicate. A 2025 meta-analysis of 59 publications found a weighted recall ratio of 0.99 across 37 studies (i.e. no advantage at all) and dz = 0.15 from the 8 studies with extractable effect sizes. The *resumption* tendency (the Ovsiankina effect) does hold. Practitioners citing 'Zeigarnik' for progress-bar design are almost always describing Ovsiankina.

- **Status:** CONFIRMED · STRONG
- **Citation:** Interruption, recall and resumption: a meta-analysis of the Zeigarnik and Ovsiankina effects, Humanities and Social Sciences Communications, 2025
- **URL:** https://www.nature.com/articles/s41599-025-05000-w
- **Peer reviewed:** None
- **Sample / design:** Systematic review and meta-analysis, 59 publications (38 on Zeigarnik). Recall ratio pooled over 37 studies; effect-size meta-analysis limited to 8 studies with usable data — a real limitation, and the early literature is heavily publication-biased toward positive results.
- **Effect size:** Weighted recall ratio 0.99 (37 studies) — null. dz = 0.15 (8 studies) — negligible. Ovsiankina (resumption): supported as a general tendency.
- **Kind:** VISIBLE_UI
- **Evidence type:** PEER_REVIEWED
- **Ethical status:** BENIGN
- **Concrete example:** Netflix's 'Continue Watching' row and Duolingo's 'finish your lesson' prompt work — but via resumption and cue availability, not via superior memory for the unfinished item.
- **Mechanism:** The original 1927 result likely depended on experimenter authority and high task involvement, conditions rarer in modern samples, plus small-N publication bias. What survives is behavioural: people tend to return to interrupted tasks. That is a resumption tendency, not a memory effect.
- **Piano-app application:** Actionable as a *negative* result: do not design Fingerly's UI on the assumption that leaving something visibly unfinished will make the user remember it better — that mechanism isn't there. What is supported: make resumption frictionless. On session end, persist the exact bar/passage where practice stopped and surface it as the single default action on next launch, with no decision menu (which also satisfies SPEC §2's ban on decision menus before playing). If any spec text or research doc cites Zeigarnik as justification, correct it to Ovsiankina and downgrade the confidence.
- **Verifier correction:** Verified directly from the paper (Hum Soc Sci Commun (2025) 12:962, doi 10.1057/s41599-025-05000-w, authors Romain Ghibellini & Beat Meier). Screening produced 'a final sample of 59 publications. Of these, 38 investigated the Zeigarnik effect, 20 investigated the Ovsiankina effect, and 1 investigated both.' Weighted recall ratio = 0.99 including Zeigarnik's own 1927 data (N=38) and 0.99 excluding it (N=37); the authors note Zeigarnik's original computations are inflated. Cohen's dz = 0.15 weighted across N=8 publications. Ovsiankina resumption is robust (individual studies 61-91% resumption). Two small precisions: the units are PUBLICATIONS, not studies; and the authors' conclusion is more nuanced than 'does not replicate' — they argue the Zeigarnik effect may depend on situational influences and individual differences (experimenter authority, situational demands, task involvement) that were common historically and are rare now, concluding it 'lacks universal validity' rather than that it is nonexistent.
- **Replication:** This IS the replication evidence. dz=0.15 with a pooled ratio of 0.99 across ~60 publications is about as close to a null as a literature gets. Netflix 'Continue Watching' and Duolingo lesson prompts are real product patterns; attributing them to resumption + cue availability rather than memory is the claimant's reasoning, not the paper's, but it is consistent with the paper.
- **Verifier notes:** This is the only STRONG item in the set and the one most worth acting on: it is a meta-analysis, it is recent, and it directly retires a piece of folklore that this design area repeats constantly. The claim's own framing — that practitioners citing 'Zeigarnik' for progress UI are usually describing Ovsiankina — is a sound inference and is the useful takeaway.

### Reward-reveal ceremonies produce measurable arousal proportional to rarity, and the anticipation phase itself is arousing before any content is known. Legendary vs rare loot box outcomes: subjective arousal ηp²=.78, urge to open more ηp²=.38, skin conductance response amplitude ηp²=.23, post-reinforcement pause 5.70s vs 4.91s (ηp²=.35). Skin conductance was significantly elevated during the ~2s box-shaking animation before any item appeared. The same machinery in slot machines produces losses-disguised-as-wins, which players physiologically and cognitively categorise as wins.

- **Status:** MISCHARACTERIZED · STRONG
- **Citation:** Larche, Musielak & Dixon, "Rare Loot Box Rewards Trigger Larger Arousal and Reward Responses, and Greater Urge to Open More Loot Boxes", Journal of Gambling Studies, 2019; Dixon, Harrigan et al., "Losses disguised as wins in modern multi-line video slot machines", Addiction 105(10), 2010; Dixon et al., "Using Sound to Unmask Losses Disguised as Wins", Journal of Gambling Studies 2014
- **URL:** https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC7882574/
- **Peer reviewed:** None
- **Sample / design:** Larche et al.: two experiments, N=47 (online, 49 loot-box videos) and N=40 (lab, skin conductance, mouse force, post-reinforcement pauses). Dixon et al.: lab studies with psychophysiological measures on real multi-line slot software. Both use gambling-adjacent stimuli; participants were Overwatch players / adult gamblers.
- **Effect size:** Very large for subjective arousal (ηp²=.78), large-to-moderate for urge (.38), PRP (.35), and SCR amplitude (.23). Dixon: LDWs and true wins equally arousing.
- **Kind:** BOTH
- **Evidence type:** PEER_REVIEWED
- **Ethical status:** DARK_PATTERN
- **Concrete example:** Overwatch loot boxes shake for ~2s, then burst and rain items, never revealing contents until the end. CS:GO cases run a decelerating horizontal reel that slows past near-miss rarities before settling — a direct import of slot-machine reel deceleration.
- **Mechanism:** Anticipation and reward are dissociable (Berridge & Robinson's wanting vs liking; Schultz's dopamine prediction error). The reveal delay is where the wanting signal lives; sequential resolution (reel-by-reel, shake-then-open) keeps outcome uncertainty alive as long as possible. The dark version, per Dixon, is that celebratory audiovisual feedback attached to a net loss causes the loss to be *encoded* as a win — and removing the sound removes the illusion.
- **Piano-app application:** Take the *structure*, refuse the *deception*. Legitimate borrow: at section completion, run a short anticipation→reveal→payoff sequence (~200ms hold, then the accuracy number counts up, then the visual payoff) rather than dumping the result instantly — the delay is where the felt reward is, and the finding that SCR rises during a content-free 2s shake shows the anticipation carries real affect on its own. Hard prohibitions for Fingerly, and they should probably be written into SPEC: (a) never randomise the reward — Fingerly has no loot, and variable-ratio reward on a practice app is straightforwardly exploitative; (b) never apply the success ceremony to a poor result, which is exactly the losses-disguised-as-wins failure and directly violates SPEC §2's 'blunt metrics only' rule; (c) keep the anticipation under ~500ms — a 2s box-shake in a practice loop is dead time, and the CHI 2024 result says what matters is that the feedback be legibly contingent on real success, not that it be drawn out.
- **Verifier correction:** The numbers are right; the inference is not. Verified: n = 7,422, eta-squared = 0.054 for loot box spending vs 0.004 for other microtransaction spending, PLOS ONE 13(11) e0206767, correction published 14 March 2019 (e0214167), and the 2019 replication is real — 'Loot boxes are again linked to problem gambling: Results of a replication study', PLOS ONE 14(3) e0213194, n = 1,172 (SMALLER than the original, not larger), eta-squared = 0.051 in a sample that 'did not self-select into a loot box study'. But 'an order-of-magnitude difference ISOLATING THE RANDOMISATION ITSELF as the harmful component' is an unsupported causal leap. Both studies are cross-sectional self-report surveys and the authors state plainly that 'it is impossible to understand the direction of the causal relationship'. Comparing two eta-squared values across two predictors in a correlational survey does not isolate a causal component; problem gamblers plausibly select into randomised spending. Restate as: loot box spending is more strongly associated with problem gambling than non-randomised spending is, replicated, direction of causation unknown.
- **Replication:** This is the one item that earns STRONG, but only for the narrow association. Direct replication by the original authors in 2019 (eta-squared 0.051 vs 0.054, and importantly in a non-self-selected sample, addressing the original's biggest confound). Independent meta-analytic confirmation: Garea, Drummond, Sauer, Hall & Williams (2021), 'Meta-analysis of the relationship between problem gambling, excessive gaming and loot box spending', International Gambling Studies 21(3), 460-479, finds a consistent association across studies. So: association replicated and meta-analytic = STRONG. Causal attribution to randomisation = not established by any of this. Rate the association STRONG and the mechanism claim UNVERIFIED.
- **Verifier notes:** Second and more serious problem: the Skinner framing is the exact folklore this review targets. Variable-ratio schedules producing high steady response rates and strong resistance to extinction is a real operant finding, but its application here is analogical, not measured — no one has demonstrated that any commercial compulsion loop operates on a VR schedule in humans. And it is self-refuting against this very claim set: pity systems (claims 1 and 2) are explicitly NOT variable-ratio. A hard guarantee at pull 90 or summon 120 bounds the ratio and makes the reinforcement increasingly predictable as the counter climbs — the opposite of the schedule Skinner showed maximises persistence. Calling pity-gated gacha a 'Skinner box on a variable-ratio schedule' is precisely wrong. Also: eta-squared = 0.054 is ~5% of variance, a small-to-medium effect; the authors' own defensible phrasing is that it is 'neither small, nor trivial' and larger than some established risk factors, which is a much narrower claim than the one made here. Schüll (2012, Princeton UP) is real and correctly characterised as ethnographic/documentary, not quantitative evidence.

### The loot rarity colour hierarchy (grey → white → green → blue → purple → orange/gold) is a de facto cross-industry standard with a traceable lineage — Angband → Diablo (1996) → Diablo II → World of Warcraft (2004, the first to place purple immediately above blue) → Borderlands and essentially everything after. Its value is that it is *pre-learned*: players arrive already knowing the ordering, so a colour alone communicates magnitude with zero text and zero tutorial.

- **Status:** UNCHECKED
- **Citation:** Design-history tracing across Angband, Diablo/Diablo II (David Brevik), World of Warcraft item quality tiers; community documentation and dev commentary
- **URL:** https://aggronaut.com/2020/09/03/origins-of-color-coded-loot/
- **Peer reviewed:** None
- **Sample / design:** No study. Convergent design history reconstructed from shipped games and developer statements. The strong version of the claim (that the specific hues are optimal) is unsupported — what is supported is that the *ordering* is now conventional.
- **Effect size:** Not measured. Note the accessibility problem: green/blue/purple sequences fail for deuteranopia and protanopia, and the convention encodes no redundant channel.
- **Kind:** VISIBLE_UI
- **Evidence type:** PRODUCT_OBSERVATION
- **Ethical status:** BENIGN
- **Concrete example:** World of Warcraft: Poor (grey), Common (white), Uncommon (green), Rare (blue), Epic (purple), Legendary (orange). Borderlands adopted the scale wholesale and added pearlescent above it.
- **Mechanism:** An arbitrary ordinal scale becomes free information once it is culturally installed. Colour is pre-attentive — a purple drop is identified before the item name is read — which is why it outperforms any text label for magnitude at a glance.
- **Piano-app application:** Fingerly should adopt an ordinal colour scale for performance tiers on a passage (e.g. below-tempo / at-tempo / at-tempo-clean / mastered) and then never explain it in text — which is how you satisfy SPEC §2's 'text >3 sentences' and 'no praise copy' constraints simultaneously, since colour carries the grade with no adjectives attached. Two adjustments to the borrowed convention: (1) do not use the games palette literally — grey-to-orange imports a loot-drop connotation that fits badly with practice, and the green→blue→purple run is a colourblindness trap; use a single-hue luminance/saturation ramp plus a redundant non-colour channel (fill height, or a tick-count), which stays pre-attentive and stays accessible. (2) Keep the number visible next to the colour; the colour is the glanceable layer, the metric is the honest one.

---

## Gacha/loot-box psychology, dark patterns in games, and documented harms — read as an anti-pattern catalogue for a piano-practice app

### Loot boxes deliver rewards on a random-ratio (commonly mislabelled 'variable-ratio') schedule, which produces high, persistent, extinction-resistant responding. But the schedule alone is not the harm — the harm arises when each 'response' costs real money, so persistence converts directly into spend. Random reward *schedules* are ubiquitous and morally neutral (a shuffled practice queue, a random sight-reading excerpt); random reward *purchases* are the gambling-shaped part.

- **Status:** MISCHARACTERIZED · STRONG
- **Citation:** Schüll, N. D. (2012). Addiction by Design: Machine Gambling in Las Vegas. Princeton University Press. See also King & Delfabbro (2018), 'Predatory monetization schemes in video games (e.g. loot boxes) and internet gaming disorder', Addiction 113(11):1967-1969.
- **URL:** https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/full/10.1111/add.14286
- **Peer reviewed:** None
- **Sample / design:** Schüll: multi-year ethnography of Las Vegas machine gamblers and machine designers. King & Delfabbro: conceptual/definitional editorial in Addiction, widely adopted definition.
- **Effect size:** No single effect size; random-ratio schedules are among the most robust findings in operant conditioning (Ferster & Skinner 1957), but the applied claim to loot boxes is theoretical rather than experimentally demonstrated.
- **Kind:** INVISIBLE_MECHANIC
- **Evidence type:** PEER_REVIEWED
- **Ethical status:** NEEDS_CARE — the schedule is benign; DARK_PATTERN only once each draw is paid for, or once the randomness gates something the user has already earned.
- **Concrete example:** Overwatch, CS:GO and FIFA Ultimate Team packs: a fixed price per box, an unknown payout per box, no cap on repeats. Genshin Impact's base 5-star rate is 0.6% per pull.
- **Mechanism:** Unpredictable reinforcement prevents the learner from forming a stopping rule. Under a fixed schedule you know when to quit; under a random one, every non-reward is evidence that the next response might pay, so responding persists long after it has stopped being rational. Dopaminergic reward-prediction-error signalling is maximal at ~50% uncertainty, not at certainty.
- **Piano-app application:** Randomness in *what* you practise is fine and often pedagogically good (spaced/interleaved retrieval). Randomness in *whether you get credit for practising* is not. Never randomise the reward for a completed practice rep, never gate a piece/exercise behind a random draw, and never let a session's outcome depend on chance rather than on what the user actually played. If the app ever shows a 'roll' animation, that is the tell that something has gone wrong.
- **Verifier correction:** The numbers are right; the inference is not. Verified: n = 7,422, eta-squared = 0.054 for loot box spending vs 0.004 for other microtransaction spending, PLOS ONE 13(11) e0206767, correction published 14 March 2019 (e0214167), and the 2019 replication is real — 'Loot boxes are again linked to problem gambling: Results of a replication study', PLOS ONE 14(3) e0213194, n = 1,172 (SMALLER than the original, not larger), eta-squared = 0.051 in a sample that 'did not self-select into a loot box study'. But 'an order-of-magnitude difference ISOLATING THE RANDOMISATION ITSELF as the harmful component' is an unsupported causal leap. Both studies are cross-sectional self-report surveys and the authors state plainly that 'it is impossible to understand the direction of the causal relationship'. Comparing two eta-squared values across two predictors in a correlational survey does not isolate a causal component; problem gamblers plausibly select into randomised spending. Restate as: loot box spending is more strongly associated with problem gambling than non-randomised spending is, replicated, direction of causation unknown.
- **Replication:** This is the one item that earns STRONG, but only for the narrow association. Direct replication by the original authors in 2019 (eta-squared 0.051 vs 0.054, and importantly in a non-self-selected sample, addressing the original's biggest confound). Independent meta-analytic confirmation: Garea, Drummond, Sauer, Hall & Williams (2021), 'Meta-analysis of the relationship between problem gambling, excessive gaming and loot box spending', International Gambling Studies 21(3), 460-479, finds a consistent association across studies. So: association replicated and meta-analytic = STRONG. Causal attribution to randomisation = not established by any of this. Rate the association STRONG and the mechanism claim UNVERIFIED.
- **Verifier notes:** Second and more serious problem: the Skinner framing is the exact folklore this review targets. Variable-ratio schedules producing high steady response rates and strong resistance to extinction is a real operant finding, but its application here is analogical, not measured — no one has demonstrated that any commercial compulsion loop operates on a VR schedule in humans. And it is self-refuting against this very claim set: pity systems (claims 1 and 2) are explicitly NOT variable-ratio. A hard guarantee at pull 90 or summon 120 bounds the ratio and makes the reinforcement increasingly predictable as the counter climbs — the opposite of the schedule Skinner showed maximises persistence. Calling pity-gated gacha a 'Skinner box on a variable-ratio schedule' is precisely wrong. Also: eta-squared = 0.054 is ~5% of variance, a small-to-medium effect; the authors' own defensible phrasing is that it is 'neither small, nor trivial' and larger than some established risk factors, which is a much narrower claim than the one made here. Schüll (2012, Princeton UP) is real and correctly characterised as ethnographic/documentary, not quantitative evidence.

### The near-miss effect is far weaker than its popular reputation. Clark et al. (2009) showed near-misses recruit win-related reward circuitry and increase desire to continue despite being rated *less* pleasant — but the behavioural persistence effect has repeatedly failed to replicate, including in real-casino field data and in a 2020 three-experiment review that found nulls throughout. Treat 'near-miss' as a plausible but unreliable mechanism, not a law.

- **Status:** CONFIRMED · STRONG
- **Citation:** Clark, L., Lawrence, A. J., Astley-Jones, F., & Gray, N. (2009). 'Gambling near-misses enhance motivation to gamble and recruit win-related brain circuitry.' Neuron 61(3):481-490. Counter-evidence: Sundali, Safford & Croson (2012) casino rapid-roulette field study; Ghezzi et al. (2006); MacLin et al. (2007); Fortes et al. (2015, 2017); and the review + 3 experiments in Journal of Gambling Studies (2020), 'The Near-Miss Effect in Slot Machines: A Review and Experimental Analysis Over Half a Century Later'.
- **Peer reviewed:** None
- **Sample / design:** Clark 2009: behavioural study n=40 healthy volunteers + fMRI n=15; two-reel slot simulator with fixed outcome schedule (16.7% wins, 33.3% near-misses, 50% full-misses). 2020 review: Exp1 pigeons n=8 (p=.136, .193), Exp2 humans n=178 post-exclusion (miss type p=.148), Exp3 pigeons n=8 (p=.985) — all null.
- **Effect size:** Clark: participant-chosen near-misses were less pleasant (t39=-4.21, p<.001) but more motivating (t39=4.69, p<.001) than computer-chosen ones. Replications: repeatedly null on persistence measures; Fortes et al. found pigeons actively *preferred* alternatives without near-misses.
- **Kind:** INVISIBLE_MECHANIC
- **Evidence type:** PEER_REVIEWED
- **Ethical status:** DARK_PATTERN when the near-miss rate is engineered independently of the true odds — that is deception about the process, and it is exploitative whether or not money is involved.
- **Concrete example:** Gacha pull animations that show the rarity aura flicker gold before resolving to purple; slot reels that stop one symbol short of the payline at engineered rates independent of the actual win rate.
- **Mechanism:** Proposed mechanism: near-misses are misread as skill-acquisition feedback, hijacking a learning system evolved for genuinely skill-based tasks. Crucially, Clark found the motivational boost was contingent on *personal control* — it only appeared when the participant chose their own symbol, i.e. it depends on an illusion of agency over a chance outcome.
- **Piano-app application:** The honest analogue matters here. Showing a real near-miss ('you were 12ms late on the left hand, 8ms is the threshold') is genuine skill feedback and exactly what Clark's evolved learning system is *for* — it is true, actionable, and the retry really does have better odds. The dark version is manufacturing the appearance of near-success: an animated progress bar that always stops just short, a 'so close!' screen when the miss was not close. Rule: never display a proximity cue that is not computed from the actual performance data. Also note Clark's control finding — because the user really does control the outcome in a practice app, near-miss feedback is legitimate here in a way it never is in a chance game.
- **Replication:** Verified directly against the full text of Pisklak, Yong & Spetch. It reports three experiments (Exp 1 split 1a/1b pigeons, Exp 2 humans, Exp 3 pigeons); ALL failed to support the near-miss effect, including Exp 3 where the stimuli were first confirmed to function as conditional reinforcers. The paper's own conclusion: 'there is still no experimental paradigm that seems able to consistently demonstrate this effect on gambling behaviour.' It cites and characterises Sundali et al. (2012) real-casino roulette data (no reinforcement evidence), Ghezzi et al. (2006) (1 of 3 experiments positive), MacLin et al. (2007) (non-significant persistence), and Fortes et al. (2017) (pigeons preferred alternatives WITHOUT near misses).
- **Verifier notes:** Clark et al. (2009) verified verbatim: Neuron 61(3):481-490, DOI 10.1016/j.neuron.2008.12.031; near-misses rated less pleasant yet increased desire to play, restricted to personal-control trials. The claim's characterisation is exact. This is the best-evidenced claim in the set and the only one where the skepticism is itself the finding. Minor: 'Fortes et al. (2015, 2017)' — only the 2017 paper appears in the 2020 review; the 2015 cite is unverified. The claim's ethics gloss ('engineered near-miss rates independent of true odds') is a real documented slot-machine practice but is asserted here without a citation.

### 'Losses disguised as wins' (LDWs) — outcomes that celebrate with win-like audio/animation while the user is net negative — are physiologically indistinguishable from real wins. This is the purest form of feedback fraud, and it is inherently exploitative independent of monetisation, because the harm is the false belief itself.

- **Status:** CONFIRMED · MODERATE
- **Citation:** Dixon, M. J., Harrigan, K. A., Sandhu, R., Collins, K., & Fugelsang, J. A. (2010). 'Losses disguised as wins in modern multi-line video slot machines.' Addiction 105(10):1819-1824. Follow-ups: Dixon et al. (2013/2014) on sound, Psychology of Addictive Behaviors / Journal of Gambling Studies.
- **Peer reviewed:** None
- **Sample / design:** Lab studies on multiline slot simulators with skin conductance response (SCR) and post-session win-estimation measures; Dixon's group across several studies with student and community gambler samples.
- **Effect size:** SCRs to LDWs were statistically indistinguishable from SCRs to genuine wins, and both were significantly higher than SCRs to full losses (where the machine goes silent). Players systematically overestimate their number of wins; educational animations correcting this reduce, but do not eliminate, the overestimate.
- **Kind:** BOTH
- **Evidence type:** PEER_REVIEWED
- **Ethical status:** DARK_PATTERN — inherently exploitative. Monetisation makes it costlier but is not what makes it wrong.
- **Concrete example:** A 20-line slot: bet 20 credits, win 5 on one line, machine plays the win jingle and rolling-credit animation. Net -15, felt as +1 win. Mobile equivalent: a session-end screen that shows 'New personal best!' for a metric the user did not actually improve.
- **Mechanism:** Reinforcement is delivered by the sensory celebration, not by the ledger. Because the arousal response is automatic and pre-reflective, the player's body categorises the outcome as a win before the conscious arithmetic ever happens — and the arithmetic is deliberately made hard (multiline bets obscure net position).
- **Piano-app application:** This is the single most dangerous pattern for a practice app because it directly corrupts the learning signal. A session where accuracy fell must not end in a celebration animation. Do not aggregate metrics in a way that manufactures a 'win' (e.g. reporting 'notes hit' while suppressing 'notes missed', or picking whichever of ten tracked metrics happened to improve). The SPEC's 'blunt metrics only, no praise copy' rule is exactly the LDW countermeasure — keep it. If the numbers went down, the screen should say the numbers went down.
- **Verifier correction:** No factual correction — the claim tracks the published abstract almost word for word, including the study structure ('Studies 1-3 examined individuals playing 1, 2 and 4 games respectively'), the outcomes ('game enjoyment, preferences, and changes in well-being pre- to post-play'), and the controls finding ('Competence and autonomy perceptions are also related to the intuitive nature of game controls, and the sense of presence or immersion'). DOI, journal, volume and pages all check out, as does the 2010 Przybylski/Rigby/Ryan Review of General Psychology follow-up. The only wording I would tighten is 'independently predict', which in the paper means unique variance in regression on cross-sectional self-report, not causal prediction.
- **Replication:** SDT's core needs framework is broadly supported across domains and has meta-analytic backing in health and workplace contexts; the games-specific 2006 paper has been extensively cited and its correlational pattern is routinely reproduced, but I found no direct high-powered replication or registered report of these four studies specifically. Notably this framework is NOT among the priming/ego-depletion casualties of the replication crisis — SDT has weathered it comparatively well. MODERATE is the right rating: robust framework, weak individual study design, small effects.
- **Verifier notes:** Do not upgrade this to STRONG. All four studies are cross-sectional or single-session self-report with modest samples; the 'well-being change' is a pre-to-post questionnaire shift across one play session, not a durable outcome. SDT need-satisfaction effects in general run small-to-medium, and SDT meta-analyses in adjacent domains report effect sizes 'small or medium, varied in strength over time, and/or heterogeneous'. Relevant tempering from the same research lineage: when Przybylski and colleagues moved to objective industry telemetry rather than self-report, associations between play and well-being shrank toward zero (the specification-curve work reports estimates ranging roughly -0.27 to 0.17 with a median near -0.03). That does not refute need-satisfaction as a mechanism, but it does mean self-report-based well-being effects in this literature should be discounted, not headlined.

### Loot box spending correlates with problem gambling severity at roughly r = .26-.34 across meta-analyses — a small-to-moderate, highly consistent association. The correlation is real and robust; the *causal story* is much weaker than public discourse implies, and the strongest critique is that most of the evidence is cross-sectional self-report where 'money spent' is confounded with everything else.

- **Status:** MISCHARACTERIZED · STRONG
- **Citation:** Zendle & Cairns (2018), PLOS ONE 13(11):e0206767; Garea, Drummond, Sauer, Hall & Williams (2021), 'Meta-analysis of the relationship between problem gambling, excessive gaming and loot box spending', International Gambling Studies 21(3):460-479; Spicer et al. (2022), 'Loot boxes, problem gambling and problem video gaming: A systematic review and meta-synthesis', New Media & Society; Brooks & Clark (2023) longitudinal gateway study; Delfabbro & King (2020) on inconclusive gateway evidence.
- **URL:** https://journals.plos.org/plosone/article?id=10.1371%2Fjournal.pone.0206767
- **Peer reviewed:** None
- **Sample / design:** Zendle & Cairns: cross-sectional online survey, n=7,422 gamers, PGSI as outcome. Garea et al.: meta-analysis, 15 studies for problem gambling, 7 for excessive gaming. Spicer et al.: systematic review, 12 of 13 publications reporting a positive relationship. Brooks & Clark: 6-month longitudinal, self-identified non-gamblers, baseline n=415, follow-up n=291.
- **Effect size:** Zendle & Cairns: η² = 0.054 for loot box spend vs problem gambling, against η² = 0.004 for spending on non-random in-game items — i.e. an order of magnitude larger for the *random* purchases specifically. Garea meta-analysis: r = 0.26 (0.37 with trim-and-fill) for problem gambling, r = 0.25 for excessive gaming. Spicer: mean r = .27 for problem gambling, r = .40 for problem video gaming (only 6 surveys). Close/Spicer secondary analysis: aggregate ρ = 0.34.
- **Kind:** INVISIBLE_MECHANIC
- **Evidence type:** PEER_REVIEWED
- **Ethical status:** NEEDS_CARE as evidence — DARK_PATTERN as practice. The honest reading: an association this consistent is enough to justify not building the mechanic, but not enough to claim loot boxes cause gambling disorder.
- **Concrete example:** The Zendle contrast is the load-bearing one: buying a £5 cosmetic you can see barely correlates with problem gambling; buying a £5 box that *might* contain it correlates ten times more strongly. The randomness, not the spending, is the active ingredient.
- **Mechanism:** Two live hypotheses, both probably partly true: (a) loot boxes are a gateway that habituates people to paying for chance outcomes — Brooks & Clark found a temporal link from loot boxes to *later* gambling, and found it was the randomised reward mechanism rather than microtransactions generally that drove it; (b) reverse/selection — people already prone to disordered gambling are drawn to and spend more on chance mechanics.
- **Piano-app application:** The transferable lesson is the Zendle contrast, not the gambling framing. Uncertainty about *what you get* is the toxic ingredient, and it is separable from uncertainty about *what you'll face*. A piano app can and should have unpredictable challenge (interleaved practice, randomised excerpt selection) — that's pedagogy. It must never have unpredictable *reward* for equal effort. Two identical 20-minute sessions must produce identical credit.
- **Verifier correction:** The numbers are right; the inference is not. Verified: n = 7,422, eta-squared = 0.054 for loot box spending vs 0.004 for other microtransaction spending, PLOS ONE 13(11) e0206767, correction published 14 March 2019 (e0214167), and the 2019 replication is real — 'Loot boxes are again linked to problem gambling: Results of a replication study', PLOS ONE 14(3) e0213194, n = 1,172 (SMALLER than the original, not larger), eta-squared = 0.051 in a sample that 'did not self-select into a loot box study'. But 'an order-of-magnitude difference ISOLATING THE RANDOMISATION ITSELF as the harmful component' is an unsupported causal leap. Both studies are cross-sectional self-report surveys and the authors state plainly that 'it is impossible to understand the direction of the causal relationship'. Comparing two eta-squared values across two predictors in a correlational survey does not isolate a causal component; problem gamblers plausibly select into randomised spending. Restate as: loot box spending is more strongly associated with problem gambling than non-randomised spending is, replicated, direction of causation unknown.
- **Replication:** This is the one item that earns STRONG, but only for the narrow association. Direct replication by the original authors in 2019 (eta-squared 0.051 vs 0.054, and importantly in a non-self-selected sample, addressing the original's biggest confound). Independent meta-analytic confirmation: Garea, Drummond, Sauer, Hall & Williams (2021), 'Meta-analysis of the relationship between problem gambling, excessive gaming and loot box spending', International Gambling Studies 21(3), 460-479, finds a consistent association across studies. So: association replicated and meta-analytic = STRONG. Causal attribution to randomisation = not established by any of this. Rate the association STRONG and the mechanism claim UNVERIFIED.
- **Verifier notes:** Second and more serious problem: the Skinner framing is the exact folklore this review targets. Variable-ratio schedules producing high steady response rates and strong resistance to extinction is a real operant finding, but its application here is analogical, not measured — no one has demonstrated that any commercial compulsion loop operates on a VR schedule in humans. And it is self-refuting against this very claim set: pity systems (claims 1 and 2) are explicitly NOT variable-ratio.
