A hard guarantee at pull 90 or summon 120 bounds the ratio and makes the reinforcement increasingly predictable as the counter climbs — the opposite of the schedule Skinner showed maximises persistence. Calling pity-gated gacha a 'Skinner box on a variable-ratio schedule' is precisely wrong. Also: eta-squared = 0.054 is ~5% of variance, a small-to-medium effect; the authors' own defensible phrasing is that it is 'neither small, nor trivial' and larger than some established risk factors, which is a much narrower claim than the one made here. Schüll (2012, Princeton UP) is real and correctly characterised as ethnographic/documentary, not quantitative evidence.

### Loot box revenue is structurally concentrated in harmed users: the top 5% of spenders generate roughly half of all loot box revenue, about a third of high spenders meet problem-gambling criteria, and spending does NOT correlate with income. This is the most damning single finding in the literature — it means the business model's profitability depends on the harmed minority, not on wealthy enthusiasts.

- **Status:** MISCHARACTERIZED · MODERATE
- **Citation:** Close, J., Spicer, S. G., Nicklin, L. L., Uther, M., Lloyd, J., & Lloyd, H. (2021). 'Secondary analysis of loot box data: Are high-spending "whales" wealthy gamers or problem gamblers?' Addictive Behaviors 117:106851.
- **Peer reviewed:** None
- **Sample / design:** Secondary analysis pooling multiple existing loot box survey datasets, with spending bands and PGSI classification.
- **Effect size:** Top 5% of spenders (>$100/month) account for ~50% of loot box revenue. ~1/3 of that >$100/month group classify as problem gamblers. Aggregate spend-vs-problem-gambling correlation ρ = 0.34, p < .001. No significant correlation between loot box spend and earnings.
- **Kind:** INVISIBLE_MECHANIC
- **Evidence type:** PEER_REVIEWED
- **Ethical status:** DARK_PATTERN — and the clearest case where monetisation is what makes it exploitative. The same mechanic with no spend attached does not have a harmed-minority revenue base because it has no revenue base.
- **Concrete example:** Mobile F2P 'whale' economics generally: publishers routinely report >50% of revenue from <2% of players. Close et al. show that in the loot box case those players are disproportionately problem gamblers rather than affluent hobbyists.
- **Mechanism:** Any revenue model with an uncapped per-user spend ceiling and a chance-based payout will mechanically select for users with impaired stopping control, because those are the users for whom the stopping rule fails. The concentration is not a side effect of the design — it is the design working.
- **Piano-app application:** Direct generalisation beyond money: check whether any engagement metric is concentrated in a minority who are showing signs of compulsion rather than benefit. For a piano app, that's the user practising at 2am to hold a streak, or logging 6 sessions a day. Build the app so that heavy *unhealthy* use is not the app's best outcome — cap what a day can credit, make the 'ideal user' a 25-minutes-a-day user, and design the top of the usage distribution to look like mastery, not desperation. Fingerly has no monetisation at all, which structurally removes this entire class of harm; that is a feature worth protecting.
- **Verifier correction:** Two errors. (1) The meta-analytic values are r = .26 (Garea et al. 2021, 15 studies, including unpublished) and r = .27 (Spicer et al. 2022, 12 of 13 publications positive) — both verified. The .34 upper bound is NOT from a meta-analysis: it is ρ = 0.34 from Close et al. (2021), a secondary analysis pooling public datasets (claim 5's own citation). Saying '.26-.34 across meta-analyses' silently promotes a secondary analysis to meta-analytic status. (2) 'Ten times more strongly' misreports the metric. Zendle & Cairns (2018) report η² = 0.054 for loot boxes vs η² = 0.004 for other paid in-game items — a ~13.5x ratio in variance explained, which is ~3.7x in correlation units. Ratios of η² are not ratios of association strength.
- **Replication:** The .26/.27 convergence across two independent meta-analyses with different inclusion criteria is genuinely consistent — this part is real and replicated. Spicer also reports r = .40 for loot boxes/problem video gaming (only 6 surveys) and r = .21 for problem gambling/problem video gaming (only 11 of 20 significant).
- **Verifier notes:** The claim's own self-critique is the strongest part of it and is correct: the evidence is overwhelmingly cross-sectional self-report, and the direction of causality is unestablished. The '£5 cosmetic vs £5 box' framing is an illustrative reconstruction, not a design Zendle ran. Keep the qualitative conclusion (randomness, not spending, is the active ingredient); drop the '.34' and 'ten times'.

### Zagal, Björk & Lewis (2013) gave the field its canonical game dark-pattern taxonomy in three families — temporal (grinding, playing by appointment), monetary (pay-to-skip, pre-delivered/pay-to-unlock content, monetised rivalries), and social-capital (social pyramid schemes, impersonation, friend spam). Deterding, Stenros & Montola (2020) then argued 'Against Dark Game Design Patterns': the same formal mechanic can be benign or harmful depending on context, intent and consequence, so a form-based catalogue over-attributes malice and under-specifies harm.

- **Status:** MISCHARACTERIZED · STRONG
- **Citation:** Zagal, J. P., Björk, S., & Lewis, C. (2013). 'Dark Patterns in the Design of Games.' Foundations of Digital Games (FDG) 2013. Critique: Deterding, S., Stenros, J., & Montola, M. (2020). 'Against "Dark Game Design Patterns".' DiGRA 2020. Extension: King & Delfabbro (2018) predatory monetisation; Veiga, Silva, Gadelha & Conte (2025), 'Dark Patterns in Games: An Empirical Study of Their Harmfulness', ICEIS 2025, vol. 2, pp. 470-481.
- **Peer reviewed:** None
- **Sample / design:** Zagal et al.: conceptual/analytical paper with worked game examples; ~293 citations, the field's reference taxonomy. Deterding et al.: conceptual critique. Veiga et al. 2025: survey of 30 participants across engagement levels rating each pattern for harmfulness, problematic-ness and prevalence — small sample, treat as indicative.
- **Effect size:** Not applicable (taxonomic). Veiga et al. found statistically significant differences in rated harmfulness *between* patterns, and that players are often unaware they are being subjected to them.
- **Kind:** BOTH
- **Evidence type:** PEER_REVIEWED
- **Ethical status:** NEEDS_CARE — this is the finding that supplies the exploitative/benign discriminator itself. The taxonomy names forms; only Deterding's consequence test decides status.
- **Concrete example:** Zagal's own examples: FarmVille crops that wither unless harvested in a fixed window (playing by appointment); Angry Birds power-up purchases at artificially raised difficulty (pay-to-skip); games that display friends' actions those friends never took (impersonation/friend spam).
- **Mechanism:** Zagal's definition — 'a user interface or design feature that has been carefully crafted to deceive users into doing things they may not want to do' — locates the wrong in the gap between what the user would endorse on reflection and what the design induces. Deterding's counter is that this gap is a property of outcomes and context, not of mechanics: grinding is exploitative in a game that inflates costs to sell skips, and is simply 'practice' in a game where repetition is the point.
- **Piano-app application:** Use the taxonomy as a checklist and Deterding's test as the verdict. A piano app *will* contain formally identical mechanics: repetition (=grinding), scheduled return (=appointment), locked advanced pieces (=pay-to-unlock's shape). The test to apply to each: (1) Does the repetition serve the user's stated goal, or the app's engagement number? (2) Is the schedule set by pedagogy (spaced repetition intervals are real) or by an arbitrary reset timer? (3) Is the lock a prerequisite (you can't sight-read this until you can do that) or an artificial scarcity? If the answer is the former in each case, the mechanic is fine and renaming it doesn't help.
- **Verifier correction:** The numbers are right; the inference is not. Verified: n = 7,422, eta-squared = 0.054 for loot box spending vs 0.004 for other microtransaction spending, PLOS ONE 13(11) e0206767, correction published 14 March 2019 (e0214167), and the 2019 replication is real — 'Loot boxes are again linked to problem gambling: Results of a replication study', PLOS ONE 14(3) e0213194, n = 1,172 (SMALLER than the original, not larger), eta-squared = 0.051 in a sample that 'did not self-select into a loot box study'. But 'an order-of-magnitude difference ISOLATING THE RANDOMISATION ITSELF as the harmful component' is an unsupported causal leap. Both studies are cross-sectional self-report surveys and the authors state plainly that 'it is impossible to understand the direction of the causal relationship'. Comparing two eta-squared values across two predictors in a correlational survey does not isolate a causal component; problem gamblers plausibly select into randomised spending. Restate as: loot box spending is more strongly associated with problem gambling than non-randomised spending is, replicated, direction of causation unknown.
- **Replication:** This is the one item that earns STRONG, but only for the narrow association. Direct replication by the original authors in 2019 (eta-squared 0.051 vs 0.054, and importantly in a non-self-selected sample, addressing the original's biggest confound). Independent meta-analytic confirmation: Garea, Drummond, Sauer, Hall & Williams (2021), 'Meta-analysis of the relationship between problem gambling, excessive gaming and loot box spending', International Gambling Studies 21(3), 460-479, finds a consistent association across studies. So: association replicated and meta-analytic = STRONG. Causal attribution to randomisation = not established by any of this. Rate the association STRONG and the mechanism claim UNVERIFIED.
- **Verifier notes:** Second and more serious problem: the Skinner framing is the exact folklore this review targets. Variable-ratio schedules producing high steady response rates and strong resistance to extinction is a real operant finding, but its application here is analogical, not measured — no one has demonstrated that any commercial compulsion loop operates on a VR schedule in humans. And it is self-refuting against this very claim set: pity systems (claims 1 and 2) are explicitly NOT variable-ratio. A hard guarantee at pull 90 or summon 120 bounds the ratio and makes the reinforcement increasingly predictable as the counter climbs — the opposite of the schedule Skinner showed maximises persistence. Calling pity-gated gacha a 'Skinner box on a variable-ratio schedule' is precisely wrong. Also: eta-squared = 0.054 is ~5% of variance, a small-to-medium effect; the authors' own defensible phrasing is that it is 'neither small, nor trivial' and larger than some established risk factors, which is a much narrower claim than the one made here. Schüll (2012, Princeton UP) is real and correctly characterised as ethnographic/documentary, not quantitative evidence.

### Streaks demonstrably increase subsequent engagement — but the effect is entirely about the *displayed* streak, not the actual behaviour, and breaking one causes disproportionate abandonment. Silverman & Barasch's seven studies show the boost is mediated by adopting streak-maintenance as a goal, is amplified when the break feels self-caused, and is attenuated when a repair is available.

- **Status:** MISCHARACTERIZED · MODERATE
- **Citation:** Silverman, J., & Barasch, A. (2023). 'On or Off Track: How (Broken) Streaks Affect Consumer Decisions.' Journal of Consumer Research 49(6):1095-1116.
- **URL:** https://academic.oup.com/jcr/article/49/6/1095/6623414
- **Peer reviewed:** None
- **Sample / design:** Seven main studies plus pilots; N≈400-600 per lab study; one field study of fitness-app users with n=980. Key manipulation: participants shown behavioural logs with intact vs broken streaks while *actual* past behaviour was held identical.
- **Effect size:** Study 2: 66.23% continued exercising with an intact streak vs 57.86% with a broken one, OR = 1.43 — with identical real behaviour. Mediation via streak-maintenance goal adoption: indirect effect 0.20, 95% CI [0.05, 0.37]. Study 5: widening vs narrowing what counts as a qualifying action changed engagement, again with identical real behaviour.
- **Kind:** VISIBLE_UI
- **Evidence type:** PEER_REVIEWED
- **Ethical status:** NEEDS_CARE — genuinely benign when the streak counts something the user actually wants to do and repair is cheap; becomes a DARK_PATTERN when the streak is made fragile on purpose so that a paid or ad-watched restore can be sold.
- **Concrete example:** Duolingo increased streak freezes from one to two and saw daily active users rise sharply — i.e. *reducing* streak anxiety increased long-term engagement, which is Silverman & Barasch's 'repair opportunity attenuates the break effect' finding replicated at scale.
- **Mechanism:** A displayed streak converts a diffuse goal ('practise regularly') into a concrete, countable one with a salient loss attached. Loss aversion then does the work. The dark side is in the same mechanism: once the count is the goal, the count is what gets optimised, and once the count is zero the goal is gone — the abstinence-violation / 'what-the-hell' effect turns one missed day into quitting.
- **Piano-app application:** Streaks are usable in Fingerly but only in the repair-available, generously-defined form. Concretely: (1) define the qualifying action broadly enough that a genuine bad day still counts (Study 5 says the definition is a design lever, so pick the forgiving one); (2) grant automatic, silent, unlimited-ish forgiveness for missed days — never sell or gate the restore, never make the user watch anything; (3) never show the counter resetting to 0 with fanfare — a break should be visually unremarkable; (4) prefer a rolling 'days practised in the last 30' to a consecutive counter, which has the motivational property without the cliff. Since Fingerly has no monetisation, the only reason to make a streak fragile would be to manufacture engagement, which is the definition of the dark version.
- **Verifier correction:** The academic claim is accurate; the EXAMPLE is badly overstated and is the reason for this grade. Duolingo's own blog states that allowing learners to equip up to two Streak Freezes 'increased the relative number of active learners on Duolingo every day by +0.38%.' That is not 'DAU rise sharply' — it is a fraction of a percent, meaningful only at Duolingo's scale. It is also an uncontrolled product change shipped alongside many others (Lenny's Newsletter, the canonical account of Duolingo's growth turnaround, mentions streak-freeze changes only in passing among a bundle of retention optimisations and gives no DAU figure). Calling it 'Silverman & Barasch's repair finding replicated at scale' is not defensible: it is a single-arm product observation with no counterfactual, retrofitted to an academic mechanism.
- **Replication:** Silverman & Barasch is seven studies within one paper by one author team — internally consistent, but no independent replication located. Consumer-psychology multi-study packages of this shape are precisely the genre that fared badly in the replication crisis, so seven internal studies should not be read as seven replications.
- **Verifier notes:** Citation verified with one trivial error: pages are 1095-1117, not 1095-1116. All four claimed findings verified against the abstract: intact vs broken logged streaks change subsequent engagement 'independent of actual past behavior'; consumers treat maintaining a logged streak as 'a meaningful goal in and of itself'; the effect is amplified when the break is self-attributed; and it is attenuated when the streak can be repaired. The word 'entirely' in 'entirely about the displayed streak' is stronger than the paper's 'independent of actual past behavior' but is close enough to pass.

### Social streaks (Snapstreaks) are the documented failure mode of streak design: when the counter is shared with another person, breaking it is experienced as interpersonal rejection, and adolescents report stress, obligation, and go to extreme lengths — including sharing account passwords so friends can maintain streaks on their behalf — to protect it. Associated with problematic smartphone use and FoMO in early adolescents.

- **Status:** MISCHARACTERIZED · WEAK
- **Citation:** Bayer, J. B. et al. and follow-ups on Snapchat streaks; specifically Nesi/Utz-line work and 'Snapchat streaks — how are these forms of gamified interactions associated with problematic smartphone use and fear of missing out among early adolescents?' Computers in Human Behavior: Artificial Humans / CHB Reports (2023); Wang & Nurkka, 'Snapchat Streaks: How Adolescents Metagame Gamification in Social Media' (2019).
- **Peer reviewed:** None
- **Sample / design:** Mixed: survey studies of early adolescents linking streak engagement to problematic smartphone use and FoMO, plus qualitative interview studies in Austria and Belgium documenting metagaming behaviours (password sharing, proxy maintenance).
- **Effect size:** Reported as significant positive associations between streak engagement and both problematic smartphone use and FoMO in early adolescent samples; qualitative work reports stress and obligation as dominant themes rather than enjoyment.
- **Kind:** VISIBLE_UI
- **Evidence type:** PEER_REVIEWED
- **Ethical status:** DARK_PATTERN in its social form — the coercion is manufactured by design and is not monetised at all, which makes it a clean counterexample to 'it's only exploitative when monetised'.
- **Concrete example:** Snapstreaks: a fire emoji and day-count next to a friend's name, resetting to zero after 24h without a mutual snap. Documented adolescent workaround: handing over account credentials before going on holiday.
- **Mechanism:** Adding a second person converts an internal commitment device into a social debt. The cost of breaking is no longer 'I lost my number' but 'I let someone down', which loss aversion and social-obligation pressure compound. Users then optimise the metric rather than the relationship — sending a blank photo daily to keep a streak alive is the social equivalent of doing the easiest lesson to keep a Duolingo streak.
- **Piano-app application:** Do not build social streaks, shared streaks, leaderboards with decay, or any mechanic where another person's experience degrades when this user doesn't practise. Fingerly is single-user by spec — keep it that way. If any 'accountability' feature is ever considered, note that the evidence says social obligation produces metric-gaming and stress rather than practice.
- **Verifier correction:** Three citation failures. (1) 'Wang & Nurkka' is WRONG authorship. The paper 'Snapchat Streaks: How Adolescents Metagame Gamification in Social Media' is by Hristova, Dumit, Lieberoth & Slunecko (2019), and it appeared in the Workshop Proceedings of the 4th International GamiFIN Conference — a workshop paper, not a journal article. (2) The problematic-smartphone-use/FoMO paper is real but is published in Telematics and Informatics Reports (2023), not 'Computers in Human Behavior: Artificial Humans' or 'CHB Reports'; the claim hedges across two journals and both are wrong. (3) Bayer et al.'s Snapchat work concerns ephemeral social interaction generally, not streaks; citing it for streak findings is misattribution. EVIDENCE TYPE should not be PEER_REVIEWED — the password-sharing detail is journalism (widely reported since ~2017), not a peer-reviewed finding I could source.
- **Replication:** The Telematics and Informatics Reports (2023) study is cross-sectional survey work on early adolescents; I could not retrieve effect sizes, and cross-sectional FoMO/problematic-smartphone-use associations are a literature with well-known common-method-variance and measurement problems. The Hristova et al. workshop paper is qualitative interview work with Viennese adolescents (themes: reciprocity, mass snaps, black snaps, metagaming) — descriptive, small, not designed to establish 'interpersonal rejection' as a mechanism.
- **Verifier notes:** The underlying phenomenon is real and reasonably documented as PRACTICE — adolescents do metagame Snapstreaks, and this is genuinely useful as a counterexample to 'only monetised mechanics are exploitative.' But it should be labelled QUALITATIVE + JOURNALISM, and the citation block needs rewriting from scratch. As written, one of the three citations has invented authors, which is the single worst citation defect in this set.

### Streaks and XP systems reliably produce goal displacement: the counter becomes the goal and the underlying skill becomes the obstacle. Duolingo users widely report doing the shortest possible lesson purely to preserve the streak — 'performative learning'. This is the specific failure mode that a skill-acquisition app must design against, and it is not caused by monetisation at all.

- **Status:** MISCHARACTERIZED · MODERATE
- **Citation:** Duolingo streak literature and journalism; grounded theoretically in Silverman & Barasch (2023, JCR) — 'consumers adopt maintaining a logged streak as a goal' — and in the wider goal-substitution / surrogation literature. Widely documented in user-reported evidence (Duolingo burnout communities) and product analyses.
- **Peer reviewed:** None
- **Sample / design:** Convergent: the peer-reviewed mediation result (streak-maintenance goal adoption, indirect effect 0.20, 95% CI [0.05,0.37]) plus large-volume qualitative user reports of minimum-effort streak preservation. The mechanism is peer-reviewed; the Duolingo-specific prevalence is observational, not measured.
- **Effect size:** Not quantified for Duolingo specifically — this is the honest gap. Treat the mechanism as established and the magnitude as unknown.
- **Kind:** BOTH
- **Evidence type:** PRODUCT_OBSERVATION
- **Ethical status:** NEEDS_CARE — not malicious, but it is a real harm to the user's actual goal, and it appears in apps with no monetisation pressure whatsoever. The exploitative version is when the developer knows about the displacement and keeps it because DAU is the KPI.
- **Concrete example:** Duolingo: a 1-minute review lesson at 11:55pm preserves a 900-day streak identically to a full session, so that is what users do. The XP number rises, fluency doesn't.
- **Mechanism:** Any easily-measured proxy placed in front of a hard-to-measure goal gets optimised in place of it (Goodhart/surrogation). A streak is maximally vulnerable because it is binary per day: the marginal value of the 30th minute of practice is zero to the counter, while the marginal value of the 1st minute is the entire streak.
- **Piano-app application:** This is the most directly applicable finding for Fingerly. Design rules: (1) never let a trivially-cheap action satisfy the daily metric — if a streak exists, the qualifying bar must be a real practice unit (e.g. a completed exercise at threshold accuracy), not 'opened the app'; (2) surface the skill metric more prominently than the engagement metric — accuracy/timing trend should be the primary display, day-count secondary at most; (3) test the failure explicitly: ask 'what is the laziest action that satisfies this metric, and is that action still worth doing?' If the answer is no, the metric is wrong. SPEC §2's ban on cheerleading copy already points this way — extend it to a ban on any metric whose cheapest satisfying action is pedagogically worthless.
- **Verifier correction:** The academic claim is accurate; the EXAMPLE is badly overstated and is the reason for this grade. Duolingo's own blog states that allowing learners to equip up to two Streak Freezes 'increased the relative number of active learners on Duolingo every day by +0.38%.' That is not 'DAU rise sharply' — it is a fraction of a percent, meaningful only at Duolingo's scale. It is also an uncontrolled product change shipped alongside many others (Lenny's Newsletter, the canonical account of Duolingo's growth turnaround, mentions streak-freeze changes only in passing among a bundle of retention optimisations and gives no DAU figure). Calling it 'Silverman & Barasch's repair finding replicated at scale' is not defensible: it is a single-arm product observation with no counterfactual, retrofitted to an academic mechanism.
- **Replication:** Silverman & Barasch is seven studies within one paper by one author team — internally consistent, but no independent replication located. Consumer-psychology multi-study packages of this shape are precisely the genre that fared badly in the replication crisis, so seven internal studies should not be read as seven replications.
- **Verifier notes:** Citation verified with one trivial error: pages are 1095-1117, not 1095-1116. All four claimed findings verified against the abstract: intact vs broken logged streaks change subsequent engagement 'independent of actual past behavior'; consumers treat maintaining a logged streak as 'a meaningful goal in and of itself'; the effect is amplified when the break is self-attributed; and it is attenuated when the streak can be repaired. The word 'entirely' in 'entirely about the displayed streak' is stronger than the paper's 'independent of actual past behavior' but is close enough to pass.

### Progression systems weaponise sunk cost and the endowed-progress effect: the closer a user is to a visible threshold, the more wasteful stopping feels, regardless of whether continuing is rational. Gacha 'pity' systems are the engineered form — Genshin Impact's rate is 0.6% for pulls 1-73, jumps to ~20%+ from pull 74 ('soft pity'), and is guaranteed at 90 — deliberately manufacturing an escalating-commitment gradient.

- **Status:** CONFIRMED · MODERATE
- **Citation:** Genshin Impact pity mechanics (publicly documented drop rates, required by China's 2017 disclosure law); mechanism grounded in Arkes & Blumer (1985) on sunk cost, Nunes & Drèze (2006) on the endowed-progress effect, and Kivetz, Urminsky & Zheng (2006) on goal-gradient in reward programmes. King & Delfabbro (2018) place this under 'predatory monetization' — schemes that 'disguise or withhold the long-term cost of the activity until players are already financially and psychologically committed'.
- **Peer reviewed:** None
- **Sample / design:** Nunes & Drèze: field experiment on car-wash loyalty cards (8 stamps needed vs 10 stamps with 2 pre-filled) — identical real requirement, different completion rates. Kivetz et al.: café loyalty-card field data plus lab studies showing acceleration near goal. Genshin figures are publisher-disclosed rates, not a study.
- **Effect size:** Nunes & Drèze: the pre-filled (endowed-progress) card roughly doubled completion rate (34% vs 19%) for an identical remaining task. Kivetz et al.: measurable purchase acceleration as the reward threshold approaches.
- **Kind:** BOTH
- **Evidence type:** PEER_REVIEWED
- **Ethical status:** Endowed progress and goal gradient are BENIGN as motivational structure; they become a DARK_PATTERN when the threshold is priced, when progress can expire, or when the developer tunes the gradient to maximise spend rather than completion.
- **Concrete example:** Genshin Impact: a player at pull 80 has spent real money and faces a near-certain 5-star within 10 more pulls; walking away at that point feels like discarding 80 pulls of progress, though the pulls are already spent and confer no ownership.
- **Mechanism:** Two mechanisms stack. Sunk cost: prior investment is irrationally treated as a reason to continue. Goal gradient: motivation rises as perceived distance-to-goal shrinks. A pity counter makes both legible and continuous — the user can see exactly how much they'd 'waste' by stopping, which is precisely the information a rational agent should ignore and a loss-averse one cannot.
- **Piano-app application:** Endowed progress is genuinely useful and Fingerly should use it — show a piece as 60% mastered rather than starting from empty, pre-credit the warm-up. The three lines not to cross: (1) progress must never expire or decay (decay converts the motivator into a coercion, and it is decay that creates the 2am practice session); (2) never place a paywall, ad, or wait timer at a point of high accumulated progress; (3) never inflate the denominator so the bar always looks nearly-full. The test: if the user quit the app forever tonight, would any displayed progress be destroyed? It should not be.
- **Verifier correction:** Two small fixes. (a) The community model is by 'Cgg', published on HoYoLAB June 2021, built on ~25 million pulls from the genshin-wishes.com database (not a HoYoverse dataset) — soft pity 74 on character/standard banner, 63 on weapon banner, per-pull rate rises by base_rate x 10 (= 6pp) until it hits 100% at pull 90 (77 weapon). Model's fitted consolidated rate 1.60521% vs HoYoverse's published 1.600%. (b) The historical narrative — 'which is how the community originally deduced soft pity existed' — is not documented in any of the cited sources; KQM/HoYoLAB both derive soft pity from wish-tally distributions (clustering of 5-stars at 74-80), not from the 1.6% arithmetic. Drop that causal story or downgrade it to 'consistent with'.
- **Replication:** Not a psychological claim; no replication question. Evidentiary status is 'large community datamine, method disclosed' rather than 'developer-documented'. HoYoverse has never published the ramp, so every number for the ramp shape is inferred from player-logged pulls. Multiple independent community models (KQM TCL, paimon.moe, genshin-wishes) converge on 74/+6pp/100%-at-90, which is about as good as undocumented-internals evidence gets — but it remains inference, not disclosure.
- **Verifier notes:** The arithmetic argument itself checks out and I verified it independently: flat 0.6% with a hard guarantee at 90 gives expected pulls = sum_{k=0}^{88} 0.994^k = 69.1, i.e. an effective rate of ~1.45%, not 1.6%. A 1.6% consolidated rate implies ~62.5 pulls average, which requires elevated rates before 90. So the claim's core inference is sound. Regulatory framing is correct: China MoC notice 文市发〔2016〕32号 (Dec 2016) took effect 1 May 2017. Caveat on the Xiao et al. citation: that paper is about aggregate compliance with disclosure rules across the 100 top-grossing PRC iPhone games (loot boxes in 91/100), NOT an analysis of Genshin's soft pity concealment — it supports the regulatory context only, and should not be cited as evidence about HoYoverse specifically.

### FOMO mechanics — limited-time events, expiring offers, countdown timers, and 'playing by appointment' — coerce by manufacturing scarcity in a digital good that is not actually scarce. These are the temporal dark patterns in Zagal's taxonomy and are consistently among the patterns players themselves rate as most frustrating and most exploitative.

- **Status:** CONFIRMED · MODERATE
- **Citation:** Zagal, Björk & Lewis (2013) 'playing by appointment' (temporal dark pattern); Veiga, Silva, Gadelha & Conte (2025), 'Dark Patterns in Games: An Empirical Study of Their Harmfulness', ICEIS 2025; Mathur et al. (2019), 'Dark Patterns at Scale: Findings from a Crawl of 11K Shopping Websites' for the urgency/scarcity pattern family; FoMO scale work (Przybylski et al. 2013) linking FoMO to compulsive phone use.
- **URL:** https://arxiv.org/pdf/1907.07032
- **Peer reviewed:** None
- **Sample / design:** Mathur et al.: automated crawl of 11,000 shopping sites, cataloguing urgency and scarcity patterns at scale including false countdowns. Veiga et al.: n=30 player survey rating harmfulness — small, indicative only. Przybylski et al.: FoMO scale validation linking trait FoMO to problematic phone use.
- **Effect size:** Mathur et al. documented urgency/scarcity as among the most prevalent dark pattern categories, including verifiably false countdown timers that reset. Player-perception studies rank 'playing by appointment' and daily-rewards coercion among the most frustrating patterns.
- **Kind:** VISIBLE_UI
- **Evidence type:** PEER_REVIEWED
- **Ethical status:** DARK_PATTERN when the deadline is arbitrary or fake. BENIGN when the timing constraint is real and serves the user (a scheduled lesson, a spaced-repetition interval that is genuinely time-sensitive).
- **Concrete example:** FarmVille crops withering on a fixed timer (Zagal's canonical case); mobile battle passes expiring on a season clock; players setting alarms overnight to collect timed rewards. Mathur et al. found countdown timers that simply restart on page reload.
- **Mechanism:** Artificial deadlines convert a considered decision into a reflexive one by removing deliberation time, and they exploit anticipated regret — the user acts to avoid a future feeling of exclusion rather than to obtain a present benefit. In games specifically, the scarcity is pure fiction: the content exists on a server and could be offered at any time.
- **Piano-app application:** Ban expiring content, limited-time exercises, seasonal events, and anything that punishes practising at a different hour than yesterday. There is one legitimate temporal mechanic here: spaced repetition intervals are a real pedagogical property of memory, not an invented scarcity — but present them as 'this piece is due for review', never as 'review in the next 4 hours or lose your progress'. The discriminator: a real deadline is derived from the user's learning state; a fake one is derived from the clock.
- **Replication:** Not the kind of claim that replicates — these are taxonomies and position papers, not effects. 'Against Dark Game Design Patterns' is real (Google Scholar lists Deterding & Stenros, 2020) but has only ~24 citations, so describing it as having reframed the field overstates its uptake. Veiga et al. (2025) is a personal-opinion survey with n = 30 participants — far too small to settle anything, and the claim should not lean on it.
- **Verifier notes:** Zagal's three categories independently verified via Veiga et al.'s full text, which adopts them verbatim: Temporal (manipulate players' time), Monetary (exploit financial investment), Social Capital (leverage social relationships coercively). Named patterns (Grinding, Playing by Appointment, Pay to Skip, Pre-delivered Content, Monetized Rivalries, Social Pyramid, Impersonation) all appear in Veiga's catalog derived from Zagal. Veiga et al. citation verified exactly: ICEIS 2025 Vol. 2, pp. 470-481, DOI 10.5220/0013365800003929 — but the citation OMITS an author (Horácio A. B. F. Oliveira; full list is Veiga, Silva, Gadelha, Oliveira, Conte). I could not retrieve Zagal's FDG 2013 full text directly (host returned 503) or confirm Montola as third author on the Deterding paper. EVIDENCE TYPE 'PEER_REVIEWED' is technically true (peer-reviewed conferences) but misleading: none of these are empirical effect studies, and the load-bearing Deterding piece is an argument paper.

### Regulators have reached genuinely contradictory conclusions on loot boxes, and the divergence is instructive: Belgium (2018) held them to be illegal gambling; the Dutch Council of State (2022) overturned the KSA's €10m EA fine on the reasoning that pack-opening is part of a broader game of skill, not a standalone game of chance; the UK DCMS Committee (2019) recommended Gambling Act regulation but the Government (July 2022) declined, citing association-without-established-causation. Japan banned the specific 'kompu gacha' combinatorial mechanic in 2012; China has legally mandated probability disclosure since 2017.

- **Status:** CONFIRMED · MODERATE
- **Citation:** Belgian Gaming Commission, Research Report on Loot Boxes (April 2018); Raad van State (Dutch Council of State) ruling on EA/FIFA, March 2022; DCMS Committee, 'Immersive and Addictive Technologies' (Sept 2019, HC 1846); DCMS 'Government response to the call for evidence on loot boxes in video games' (July 2022); Japan Consumer Affairs Agency ruling on Complete Gacha under the Act against Unjustifiable Premiums and Misleading Representations (18 May 2012); PRC Ministry of Culture disclosure requirement (May 2017). Enforcement precedent: FTC v. Epic Games, $245m order finalised March 2023.
- **URL:** https://www.ftc.gov/news-events/news/press-releases/2023/03/ftc-finalizes-order-requiring-fortnite-maker-epic-games-pay-245-million-tricking-users-making
- **Peer reviewed:** None
- **Sample / design:** Regulatory findings and case law, not empirical studies. The Belgian report examined four titles (Star Wars Battlefront II, Overwatch, FIFA 18, CS:GO) and found three in violation; Battlefront II passed only because EA had already suspended microtransactions.
- **Effect size:** Belgian penalties: up to €800,000 and five years' imprisonment. Dutch fine: €10m, overturned. FTC/Epic: $245m in consumer redress specifically for dark-pattern purchase flows (confusing/inconsistent button configuration causing unwanted single-press charges, plus account-locking of users who disputed charges).
- **Kind:** INVISIBLE_MECHANIC
- **Evidence type:** JOURNALISM
- **Ethical status:** DARK_PATTERN — the regulatory consensus, despite legal disagreement, is that these mechanics cause consumer harm; the disagreement is only about which body of law reaches them.
- **Concrete example:** Japan's kompu gacha ban is the sharpest precedent: it did not ban gacha, it banned the *combinatorial* variant where collecting a full set of random items unlocks a rare prize — because the compound probability is opaque and the expected cost unbounded. Reported cases included a middle-school student spending $5,000 in a month.
- **Mechanism:** The legal fault line is whether the randomised item has real-world value and whether the draw is a 'game' in itself — not whether it is psychologically manipulative. This is why the FTC route (unfair/deceptive practices) succeeded where gambling law repeatedly stalled: the FTC did not need to prove gambling, only that the interface caused charges the user did not intend.
- **Piano-app application:** Two takeaways. First, the FTC/Epic order is the one that binds most broadly and it is about *interface deception*, not gambling — a single confusing button that causes an unintended action is actionable. Fingerly should have no destructive or committing action reachable by one ambiguous tap (resetting progress, deleting a recording). Second, the Japanese kompu gacha reasoning generalises: any mechanic whose total cost-to-completion is not knowable in advance is the thing regulators object to. In a practice app, always show the user the full remaining path — how many exercises, how long — never an open-ended 'keep going until it unlocks'.
- **Verifier correction:** Two small fixes. (a) The community model is by 'Cgg', published on HoYoLAB June 2021, built on ~25 million pulls from the genshin-wishes.com database (not a HoYoverse dataset) — soft pity 74 on character/standard banner, 63 on weapon banner, per-pull rate rises by base_rate x 10 (= 6pp) until it hits 100% at pull 90 (77 weapon). Model's fitted consolidated rate 1.60521% vs HoYoverse's published 1.600%. (b) The historical narrative — 'which is how the community originally deduced soft pity existed' — is not documented in any of the cited sources; KQM/HoYoLAB both derive soft pity from wish-tally distributions (clustering of 5-stars at 74-80), not from the 1.6% arithmetic. Drop that causal story or downgrade it to 'consistent with'.
- **Replication:** Not a psychological claim; no replication question. Evidentiary status is 'large community datamine, method disclosed' rather than 'developer-documented'. HoYoverse has never published the ramp, so every number for the ramp shape is inferred from player-logged pulls. Multiple independent community models (KQM TCL, paimon.moe, genshin-wishes) converge on 74/+6pp/100%-at-90, which is about as good as undocumented-internals evidence gets — but it remains inference, not disclosure.
- **Verifier notes:** The arithmetic argument itself checks out and I verified it independently: flat 0.6% with a hard guarantee at 90 gives expected pulls = sum_{k=0}^{88} 0.994^k = 69.1, i.e. an effective rate of ~1.45%, not 1.6%. A 1.6% consolidated rate implies ~62.5 pulls average, which requires elevated rates before 90. So the claim's core inference is sound. Regulatory framing is correct: China MoC notice 文市发〔2016〕32号 (Dec 2016) took effect 1 May 2017. Caveat on the Xiao et al. citation: that paper is about aggregate compliance with disclosure rules across the 100 top-grossing PRC iPhone games (loot boxes in 91/100), NOT an analysis of Genshin's soft pity concealment — it supports the regulatory context only, and should not be cited as evidence about HoYoverse specifically.

### Disclosure-and-labelling remedies systematically fail in practice. Xiao's compliance audits found only 64.0% of UK games disclosed loot box probabilities under voluntary self-regulation versus 95.6% in China where it is legally mandated; only 1.3% showed odds on the purchase page without extra steps; and 71.0% of popular loot-box games on Google Play lacked the required ESRB/PEGI/IARC 'In-Game Purchases (Includes Random Items)' label.

- **Status:** MISCHARACTERIZED · MODERATE
- **Citation:** Xiao, L. Y., Henderson, L. L., Yang, Y., & Newall, P. W. S. (2023). 'What are the odds? Poor compliance with UK loot box probability disclosure industry self-regulation.' (Behavioural Public Policy / Addiction Research & Theory). Xiao, L. Y. (2023). 'Beneath the label: unsatisfactory compliance with ESRB, PEGI and IARC industry self-regulation requiring loot box presence warning labels by video game companies.' Royal Society Open Science 10(3):230270.
- **URL:** https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC10530011/
- **Peer reviewed:** None
- **Sample / design:** UK disclosure study: 75 games with first-party loot boxes drawn from the top 100 highest-grossing UK iPhone games as of 21 June 2021, coded for presence and prominence of probability disclosures. Label study: preregistered comparison of ESRB- and PEGI-labelled titles plus a Google Play Store audit.
- **Effect size:** UK: 64.0% disclosed (48/75) vs 95.6% under Chinese law. 42.7% in-game only, 21.3% both locations, 36.0% no disclosure found. Only 1.3% (1 game) auto-displayed odds at the purchase page; 6.7% 'reasonably prominent'; 31.3% of website disclosures unreachable from the homepage. Labels: ESRB/PEGI consistency 39.4% preregistered (83.9% in exploratory reanalysis); 71.0% of Google Play loot-box games unlabelled.
- **Kind:** VISIBLE_UI
- **Evidence type:** PEER_REVIEWED
- **Ethical status:** NEEDS_CARE — labels are not themselves exploitative, but relying on them as the harm-mitigation is. The finding is that transparency-as-remedy is largely theatre.
- **Concrete example:** A mobile game showing '0.6% for 5-star' on a webpage linked from a settings submenu, while the purchase button itself shows only the price.
- **Mechanism:** Disclosure remedies fail for the same reason cookie banners fail: the discloser controls placement, and information placed outside the decision moment does not enter the decision. A probability buried three menus deep is technically disclosed and functionally invisible. Compliance also collapses without enforcement — the 64% vs 95.6% gap is the entire argument against self-regulation.
- **Piano-app application:** The generalisable rule: information that affects a decision must appear *at* the decision, not somewhere reachable. In Fingerly, if a mechanic needs an explanation to be acceptable, that is evidence the mechanic is wrong — fix the mechanic rather than adding a disclosure. Concretely: show the actual scoring thresholds on the screen where the score appears; if an exercise is going to take 15 minutes, say so before it starts, not in a help page. And do not treat 'but we told them' as sufficient justification for anything.
- **Verifier correction:** The academic claim is accurate; the EXAMPLE is badly overstated and is the reason for this grade. Duolingo's own blog states that allowing learners to equip up to two Streak Freezes 'increased the relative number of active learners on Duolingo every day by +0.38%.' That is not 'DAU rise sharply' — it is a fraction of a percent, meaningful only at Duolingo's scale. It is also an uncontrolled product change shipped alongside many others (Lenny's Newsletter, the canonical account of Duolingo's growth turnaround, mentions streak-freeze changes only in passing among a bundle of retention optimisations and gives no DAU figure). Calling it 'Silverman & Barasch's repair finding replicated at scale' is not defensible: it is a single-arm product observation with no counterfactual, retrofitted to an academic mechanism.
- **Replication:** Silverman & Barasch is seven studies within one paper by one author team — internally consistent, but no independent replication located. Consumer-psychology multi-study packages of this shape are precisely the genre that fared badly in the replication crisis, so seven internal studies should not be read as seven replications.
- **Verifier notes:** Citation verified with one trivial error: pages are 1095-1117, not 1095-1116. All four claimed findings verified against the abstract: intact vs broken logged streaks change subsequent engagement 'independent of actual past behavior'; consumers treat maintaining a logged streak as 'a meaningful goal in and of itself'; the effect is amplified when the break is self-attributed; and it is attenuated when the streak can be repaired. The word 'entirely' in 'entirely about the displayed streak' is stronger than the paper's 'independent of actual past behavior' but is close enough to pass.

### Extrinsic reward systems measurably undermine intrinsic motivation for the rewarded activity — the overjustification effect. Deci, Koestner & Ryan's 128-study meta-analysis found tangible, expected, engagement-, completion- and performance-contingent rewards all significantly reduced free-choice intrinsic motivation. For a skill app whose entire value depends on the user wanting to play the instrument, this is a larger threat than any gambling mechanic.

- **Status:** CONFIRMED · STRONG
- **Citation:** Deci, E. L., Koestner, R., & Ryan, R. M. (1999). 'A meta-analytic review of experiments examining the effects of extrinsic rewards on intrinsic motivation.' Psychological Bulletin 125(6):627-668. See also Deci, Koestner & Ryan (2001), Review of Educational Research 71(1):1-27, on the education-specific case.
- **URL:** https://depts.washington.edu/techdocs/papers/deciExtrinsicRewardsAndIntrinsicMotivation99.pdf
- **Peer reviewed:** None
- **Sample / design:** Meta-analysis of 128 experiments on reward effects on intrinsic motivation, using free-choice persistence and self-reported interest as outcomes. Note the ongoing dispute with Cameron & Pierce's competing meta-analysis, which reached weaker conclusions; Deci et al. argue that analysis was flawed. The effect is real but contested in magnitude and moderated heavily by reward type.
- **Effect size:** Free-choice intrinsic motivation: engagement-contingent d = -0.40, completion-contingent d = -0.36, performance-contingent d = -0.28. Self-reported interest: engagement-contingent d = -0.15, completion-contingent d = -0.17. Critically, *verbal* rewards and unexpected tangible rewards did NOT undermine, and informational feedback supporting competence can enhance intrinsic motivation.
- **Kind:** BOTH
- **Evidence type:** PEER_REVIEWED
- **Ethical status:** BENIGN mechanism, harmful application. This is not exploitation — a well-meaning developer adding XP to a practice app produces this harm with no bad intent and no monetisation whatsoever. It is the most likely way Fingerly could hurt its user.
- **Concrete example:** The classic Lepper/Greene/Nisbett drawing study: children given an expected 'Good Player' award for drawing subsequently drew less in free time than children given nothing or an unexpected award.
- **Mechanism:** Cognitive evaluation theory: rewards have both a controlling and an informational aspect. When the reward is salient and contingent, the user re-attributes their behaviour from 'I do this because I want to' to 'I do this to get the thing' — and when the thing is removed, so is the reason. The undermining is strongest exactly where gamification is strongest: expected, tangible, contingent on doing the activity.
- **Piano-app application:** This is the finding that most constrains Fingerly's design. The meta-analysis says the safe form of feedback is *informational and competence-supporting*, and the unsafe form is *expected, tangible and contingent*. So: report metrics (accuracy, timing deviation, tempo achieved) because those are informational and support perceived competence; do NOT invent points, coins, badges, levels or XP that are contingent on practising, because those are the exact reward class with d = -0.40. SPEC §2's ban on praise/cheerleading copy in favour of blunt metrics is, whether or not it was derived from this literature, the empirically correct choice — blunt metrics are informational feedback, praise is a verbal contingent reward, and invented currencies are the worst case. The long-term goal is a user who plays piano when the app is uninstalled; every contingent reward added makes that outcome less likely.
- **Replication:** 128 studies verified; effect sizes verified as d = -0.40 (engagement-contingent), -0.36 (completion-contingent), -0.28 (performance-contingent), with all rewards, all tangible rewards and all expected rewards also undermining. Unlike most of the 1990s social-psych canon, this survived the replication crisis reasonably intact. It is not uncontested, however: Cameron & Pierce's (1994) rival meta-analysis found the effect narrow and avoidable, Deci et al. explicitly argued that analysis was seriously flawed, and the dispute ran for years — this is an adversarial meta-analytic literature, not a settled one. Both camps agree that verbal praise and UNEXPECTED rewards do not undermine, which is the moderator that actually matters for design.
- **Verifier notes:** The effect is measured on the laboratory free-choice behavioural paradigm over short horizons; the classic Lepper, Greene & Nisbett drawing study is correctly described. Two things to keep separate from the evidence: (1) 'For a skill app whose entire value depends on the user wanting to play the instrument, this is a larger threat than any gambling mechanic' is the claimant's judgment, not a finding — no study compares those two threats. (2) The specific translation from 'expected tangible reward for drawing' to 'XP counter in a practice app' is an extrapolation across reward type, duration and domain; d ≈ -0.3 to -0.4 in a lab does not license a point prediction about Fingerly. The design implication (avoid expected, tangible, contingent rewards; informational feedback and unexpected acknowledgement are safe) is well supported by the moderator structure and is the part worth acting on.

---

## Gamification in learning: meta-analytic evidence, motivation-crowding, and reward-target design

### Across the four major education meta-analyses, gamification's headline effect on learning is small-to-medium (g ≈ 0.25–0.50), but when Sailer & Homner restrict to methodologically rigorous studies only the COGNITIVE effect survives — motivational and behavioral effects become statistically non-significant.

- **Status:** CONFIRMED · MODERATE
- **Citation:** Sailer, M., & Homner, L. (2020). The gamification of learning: a meta-analysis. Educational Psychology Review, 32(1), 77–112. Corroborating: Bai, Hew & Huang (2020), Educational Research Review 30, 100322; Huang, Ritzhaupt et al. (2020), ETR&D 68(4), 1875–1901.
- **URL:** https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10648-019-09498-w
- **Peer reviewed:** None
- **Sample / design:** Random-effects models over experimental/quasi-experimental gamified-vs-non-gamified comparisons, 2010s literature, mostly classroom/higher-ed. Heterogeneity is severe throughout: I² = 51% in the high-rigor cognitive subsplit, 84% motivational, 79% behavioral; residual variance stayed significant after every moderator.
- **Effect size:** Full sample: cognitive g = .49 [0.30, 0.69] k=19 N=1686; motivational g = .36 [0.18, 0.54] k=16 N=2246; behavioral g = .25 [0.04, 0.46] k=9 N=951. High-rigor subsplit (RCTs + quasi-experiments with pre/posttests): cognitive g = .42 [0.14, 0.68] k=9; motivational g = .22 [−0.11, 0.56] k=7, NS; behavioral g = .27 [−0.16, 0.70] k=5, NS. Bai et al. g = .504 [0.284, 0.723]; Huang et al. g = .464 [0.244, 0.684].
- **Kind:** BOTH
- **Evidence type:** PEER_REVIEWED
- **Ethical status:** BENIGN
- **Mechanism:** The cognitive effect is plausibly not about 'fun' at all — it survives because gamified conditions usually add retrieval practice, immediate feedback, and more time-on-task, which are independently potent. The motivational claim, which is the one vendors sell, is the one that evaporates under rigor.
- **Piano-app application:** Do not budget engineering time against an expected motivation lift; the defensible expectation is a modest learning lift that comes from the practice structure (spaced retrieval of passages, immediate per-note feedback), not from the reward layer. Treat any points/badges layer in Fingerly as unproven-to-neutral and hold it to the same 'visible jank is a release blocker' bar of evidence: if it doesn't measurably improve a mastery metric on the tablet, it's dead weight.
- **Verifier correction:** The claim should carry the authors' own caveat, which it omits: Sailer & Homner explicitly write that 'the subsplit analyses showed that the summary effects for the motivational and behavioral outcomes were not robust. However, given the small sample sizes in the subgroup analyses, these findings were highly likely to be underpowered and should be viewed with caution,' and separately that 'nonsignificant results do not necessarily indicate the absence of an effect but could be explained by a lack of statistical power.' Read the subsplit as 'not demonstrated under rigor,' not as 'shown to be zero.'
- **Replication:** No independent replication of the subsplit exists — it is one moderator analysis in one meta-analysis. The three later meta-analyses cited do not run an equivalent rigor-restricted subsplit, so the pattern is corroborated for the headline effect sizes only, not for the differential survival of the cognitive effect.
- **Verifier notes:** Verified against the full CC-BY PDF. Summary effects exact: cognitive g=.49 [0.30,0.69] k=19 N=1686; motivational g=.36 [0.18,0.54] k=16 N=2246; behavioral g=.25 [0.04,0.46] k=9 N=951. High-rigor subsplit (experimental designs, or quasi-experimental with pre+posttest) exact: cognitive g=.42, SE=.14, p<.01, [0.14,0.68], k=9, N=686 — SURVIVES; motivational g=.22, SE=.17, p=.20, [-0.11,0.56], k=7, N=1063 — NON-SIGNIFICANT; behavioral g=.27, SE=.22, p=.22, [-0.16,0.70], k=5, N=667 — NON-SIGNIFICANT. Both corroborating citations verified exact via Crossref (Bai/Hew/Huang: ERR 30, 100322, DOI 10.1016/j.edurev.2020.100322; Huang/Ritzhaupt/Sommer/Zhu/Stephen/Valle/Hampton/Li: ETR&D 68(4), 1875-1901, DOI 10.1007/s11423-020-09807-z). The 0.25–0.50 band is consistent with Li, Hew & Du (2024) g=.257 at the low end. Not STRONG because the load-bearing subsplit rests on k=5–9.

### The gamification literature's cognitive effect is largely an artifact of researcher-built outcome measures and non-randomized designs. In Sailer & Homner, 18 of 20 cognitive-outcome samples used self-developed tests (g = .50); the one study using an adapted standardized instrument found g = .14, non-significant. For motivation, adapted standardized instruments gave g = .13 [−0.26, 0.54] (k=5, N=999) versus g = .39 for self-developed scales.

- **Status:** CONFIRMED · MODERATE
- **Citation:** Sailer, M., & Homner, L. (2020). The gamification of learning: a meta-analysis. Educational Psychology Review, 32(1), 77–112 (Tables 2–3, instrument and randomization moderators).
- **URL:** https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10648-019-09498-w
- **Peer reviewed:** None
- **Sample / design:** Moderator meta-regressions within the same 2020 meta-analysis; several moderator cells have k = 1, so the instrument moderator could not be formally tested for cognitive or behavioral outcomes.
- **Effect size:** Cognitive: quasi-experimental g = .56 [0.26, 0.88] vs randomized experimental g = .29 [0.07, 0.51] — randomization roughly halves the effect. Posttest-only designs (k=14 of 20) I² = 90%. Publication-bias checks (funnel plots, selection model χ²(1) = .45/1.04/.44, all p > .30; Rosenberg fail-safe N) found no strong small-study bias, so the inflation is coming from measurement and design, not the file drawer.
- **Kind:** BOTH
- **Evidence type:** PEER_REVIEWED
- **Ethical status:** BENIGN
- **Mechanism:** Self-developed posttests are usually written against the exact content the gamified condition drilled, so they measure treatment-aligned practice rather than transferable skill. Non-random assignment additionally loads volunteer enthusiasm into the treatment group.
- **Piano-app application:** Fingerly's own evaluation must avoid this trap. Judge features against an instrument the feature did not shape — e.g. sight-reading accuracy on a piece never practiced in-app, or timing/velocity error on a cold first play-through — not against in-app XP, streak length, or scores on the exact drilled passage. Anything that only moves the app's own counters is measuring itself.
- **Verifier correction:** The claim should carry the authors' own caveat, which it omits: Sailer & Homner explicitly write that 'the subsplit analyses showed that the summary effects for the motivational and behavioral outcomes were not robust. However, given the small sample sizes in the subgroup analyses, these findings were highly likely to be underpowered and should be viewed with caution,' and separately that 'nonsignificant results do not necessarily indicate the absence of an effect but could be explained by a lack of statistical power.' Read the subsplit as 'not demonstrated under rigor,' not as 'shown to be zero.'
- **Replication:** No independent replication of the subsplit exists — it is one moderator analysis in one meta-analysis. The three later meta-analyses cited do not run an equivalent rigor-restricted subsplit, so the pattern is corroborated for the headline effect sizes only, not for the differential survival of the cognitive effect.
- **Verifier notes:** Verified against the full CC-BY PDF. Summary effects exact: cognitive g=.49 [0.30,0.69] k=19 N=1686; motivational g=.36 [0.18,0.54] k=16 N=2246; behavioral g=.25 [0.04,0.46] k=9 N=951. High-rigor subsplit (experimental designs, or quasi-experimental with pre+posttest) exact: cognitive g=.42, SE=.14, p<.01, [0.14,0.68], k=9, N=686 — SURVIVES; motivational g=.22, SE=.17, p=.20, [-0.11,0.56], k=7, N=1063 — NON-SIGNIFICANT; behavioral g=.27, SE=.22, p=.22, [-0.16,0.70], k=5, N=667 — NON-SIGNIFICANT. Both corroborating citations verified exact via Crossref (Bai/Hew/Huang: ERR 30, 100322, DOI 10.1016/j.edurev.2020.100322; Huang/Ritzhaupt/Sommer/Zhu/Stephen/Valle/Hampton/Li: ETR&D 68(4), 1875-1901, DOI 10.1007/s11423-020-09807-z). The 0.25–0.50 band is consistent with Li, Hew & Du (2024) g=.257 at the low end. Not STRONG because the load-bearing subsplit rests on k=5–9.

### Gamification reliably raises perceived AUTONOMY and RELATEDNESS but barely touches perceived COMPETENCE — the SDT need that most directly predicts persistence at a hard skill. Its overall effect on intrinsic motivation is small (g = .257).

- **Status:** MISCHARACTERIZED · WEAK
- **Citation:** Li, L., Hew, K. F., & Du, J. (2024). Gamification enhances student intrinsic motivation, perceptions of autonomy and relatedness, but minimal impact on competency: a meta-analysis and systematic review. Educational Technology Research and Development, 72(2), 765–796.
- **URL:** https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s11423-023-10337-7
- **Peer reviewed:** None
- **Sample / design:** 35 independent interventions, ~2,500 participants, gamified vs non-gamified learning, 2011–2022.
- **Effect size:** Overall intrinsic motivation g = .257; autonomy g = .638; relatedness g = 1.776 (very large but rests on few, socially-designed studies); competence g = .277. Qualitative synthesis of 31 studies identified lack of perceived competence and lack of perceived autonomy as the two recurring failure modes of gamified classes.
- **Kind:** BOTH
- **Evidence type:** PEER_REVIEWED
- **Ethical status:** NEEDS_CARE
- **Mechanism:** Points and leaderboards signal relative standing, not capability growth. Competence in SDT terms requires evidence that you can now do something you previously could not — badges awarded for showing up don't supply that evidence, so the need stays unmet even as the reward layer fires.
- **Piano-app application:** Fingerly is single-user with no social layer, so the relatedness lever (the biggest effect in this meta-analysis) is unavailable by design — meaning the imported evidence base overstates what gamification can do here. The competence lever is the one that's both available and under-served: show the user a concrete before/after they can hear or see (last week you could not play bars 9–16 at 72bpm clean; today you did, 3/3 attempts). Autonomy is cheap to supply and evidence-backed — but SPEC §2's ban on 'decision menus before playing' means autonomy must come from mid-session control (retry, slow down, drop a hand), not from a pre-session config screen.
- **Verifier correction:** Fix the attribution: the AHQ-D validation paper has no author named Grinblat. It is Hupfeld KE, Osborne JB, Tran QT, Hyatt HW, Abagis TR, Shah P (2024), Scientific Reports 14(1):19460, PMID 39169147, DOI 10.1038/s41598-024-70028-y. Then drop 'terminates externally rather than volitionally' — that is a component of the operational DEFINITION Ashinoff & Abu-Akel PROPOSE for future researchers, not a measured property. Their paper's thesis is the opposite of settled: hyperfocus 'is poorly defined within the literature', 'in many cases goes undefined', 'there is no single consensus to what constitutes hyperfocus', and 'research into its effect on cognitive and neural functioning is limited'. The claim cites a paper about the absence of evidence as if it were the evidence. Also soften 'empirically real and measurable': what is demonstrated is a reliable SELF-REPORT trait scale, not a measured behavioural state.
- **Replication:** Mixed. Positive: Hupfeld 2019 was preregistered and self-replicated across a pilot (n=251) and replication sample (n=372), and the ADHD correlation replicated again in 2024 (n=347) — the questionnaire-level association is solid. Negative: Dwyer P, Sillas A, Saron CD, Rivera SM (2025), Research in Autism 127:202683, 'Investigating autistic hyperfocus and monotropism: Limited convergence of event-related potentials, laboratory tasks, and questionnaire responses' — self-reported hyperfocus does not converge with ERP or lab-task measures. That is a direct hit on the 'empirically real and measurable' framing: what replicates is that people endorse the items, not that the state exists as described.
- **Verifier notes:** Hupfeld 2019 and Ashinoff & Abu-Akel 2021 are cited correctly on all fields. Genuine support does exist for two sub-parts: HF correlates with ADHD symptomatology (CAARS r=0.53 in the 2024 sample) and the AHQ-D has strong psychometrics (single factor, loadings 0.57–0.81, Cronbach's alpha 0.93, split-half 0.93, IRT-checked discrimination, and a social-desirability control). The 'NOT flow' sub-claim is actually the best-supported part: HF correlated with flow at only r=0.12 in the 2024 validation — near-orthogonal. But note HF also correlated r=0.39 with MIND-WANDERING and r=-0.29 with grit, which complicates reading hyperfocus as productive deep engagement. Crucially, the entire construct rests on self-report: no validated behavioural or neural index of hyperfocus exists, and internal consistency is reliability, not construct validity.

### Tangible, expected rewards reliably reduce free-choice intrinsic motivation; verbal positive feedback reliably increases it. The undermining effect is real, replicated across 128 experiments, and scales with how controlling the reward feels.

- **Status:** CONFIRMED · STRONG
- **Citation:** Deci, E. L., Koestner, R., & Ryan, R. M. (1999). A meta-analytic review of experiments examining the effects of extrinsic rewards on intrinsic motivation. Psychological Bulletin, 125(6), 627–668.
- **URL:** https://home.ubalt.edu/tmitch/642/articles%20syllabus/Deci%20Koestner%20Ryan%20meta%20IM%20psy%20bull%2099.pdf
- **Peer reviewed:** None
- **Sample / design:** 128 laboratory experiments, meta-analysed with moderator breakdowns by reward contingency, expectancy, tangibility, and feedback valence.
- **Effect size:** Free-choice intrinsic motivation: engagement-contingent rewards d = −.40; completion-contingent d = −.36; performance-contingent d = −.28. Positive/verbal feedback: free-choice d = +.33, self-reported interest d = +.31. Tangible rewards were more damaging for children than college students; verbal rewards were less enhancing for children.
- **Kind:** BOTH
- **Evidence type:** PEER_REVIEWED
- **Ethical status:** NEEDS_CARE
- **Mechanism:** Cognitive evaluation theory: every reward carries a controlling aspect ('I'm doing this to get the thing') and an informational aspect ('this tells me I'm getting good'). Tangible expected rewards make the controlling aspect salient and shift the perceived locus of causality outward; informational feedback does the opposite.
- **Piano-app application:** The undermining risk is real for Fingerly because a self-directed adult learner practising piano already has non-trivial baseline intrinsic interest — exactly the high-initial-interest condition where undermining shows up. Avoid announced, tangible, completion-contingent rewards ('finish 5 sessions, unlock the gold theme'). The safe form is informational: unannounced, competence-conveying, and delivered as data. Note this cuts against naive reading of SPEC §2's praise ban — the meta-analysis says positive FEEDBACK helps (d = +.33); what it condemns is content-free cheerleading, which is a different thing from telling the user their error rate dropped.
- **Replication:** 128 studies verified; effect sizes verified as d = -0.40 (engagement-contingent), -0.36 (completion-contingent), -0.28 (performance-contingent), with all rewards, all tangible rewards and all expected rewards also undermining. Unlike most of the 1990s social-psych canon, this survived the replication crisis reasonably intact. It is not uncontested, however: Cameron & Pierce's (1994) rival meta-analysis found the effect narrow and avoidable, Deci et al. explicitly argued that analysis was seriously flawed, and the dispute ran for years — this is an adversarial meta-analytic literature, not a settled one. Both camps agree that verbal praise and UNEXPECTED rewards do not undermine, which is the moderator that actually matters for design.
- **Verifier notes:** The effect is measured on the laboratory free-choice behavioural paradigm over short horizons; the classic Lepper, Greene & Nisbett drawing study is correctly described. Two things to keep separate from the evidence: (1) 'For a skill app whose entire value depends on the user wanting to play the instrument, this is a larger threat than any gambling mechanic' is the claimant's judgment, not a finding — no study compares those two threats. (2) The specific translation from 'expected tangible reward for drawing' to 'XP counter in a practice app' is an extrapolation across reward type, duration and domain; d ≈ -0.3 to -0.4 in a lab does not license a point prediction about Fingerly. The design implication (avoid expected, tangible, contingent rewards; informational feedback and unexpected acknowledgement are safe) is well supported by the moderator structure and is the part worth acting on.

### The opposing camp argues the undermining effect is narrow, not pervasive: rewards damage intrinsic motivation only on already-high-interest tasks when they are tangible, expected, and loosely tied to performance. Rewards for low-interest tasks, verbal rewards, and rewards genuinely contingent on performance level either help or do no harm.

- **Status:** CONFIRMED · STRONG
- **Citation:** Cameron, J., Banko, K. M., & Pierce, W. D. (2001). Pervasive negative effects of rewards on intrinsic motivation: The myth continues. The Behavior Analyst, 24(1), 1–44. Original: Cameron & Pierce (1994), Review of Educational Research 64, 363–423. Rebuttals: Deci, Koestner & Ryan (2001), RER 71(1), 1–27; Lepper, Henderlong & Gingras (1999).
- **URL:** https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC2731358/
- **Peer reviewed:** None
- **Sample / design:** Competing meta-analyses over an overlapping study pool; the dispute is about coding rules, control-group choice, outlier handling, and whether the free-choice-persistence measure is a valid operationalisation of intrinsic motivation.
- **Effect size:** Cameron et al. report that performance-contingent rewards leave intrinsic motivation equal to or above unrewarded controls, and that rewards on low-interest tasks enhance free-choice motivation. Deci et al. counter that Cameron & Pierce's 1994 analysis collapsed across theoretically meaningful interactions without moderator tests, used inappropriate control groups, and discarded ~20% of studies as outliers; they also document misclassified conditions and numerical recording errors.
- **Kind:** BOTH
- **Evidence type:** PEER_REVIEWED
- **Ethical status:** BENIGN
- **Mechanism:** Both sides actually agree on the boundary conditions; they disagree on whether those conditions are common in real settings. Behaviour-analytic reading: reward tied tightly to demonstrated competence functions as information, not control.
- **Piano-app application:** The two camps converge on a single usable design rule for Fingerly: if you reward at all, tie it tightly and visibly to a performance STANDARD the user just met, never to mere attendance or elapsed minutes. 'Cleared bars 9–16 at 100% target tempo, 0 wrong notes' is the reward-eligible event; 'practised 20 minutes' is not. This is also the version both meta-analyses agree does not undermine.
- **Replication:** 128 studies verified; effect sizes verified as d = -0.40 (engagement-contingent), -0.36 (completion-contingent), -0.28 (performance-contingent), with all rewards, all tangible rewards and all expected rewards also undermining. Unlike most of the 1990s social-psych canon, this survived the replication crisis reasonably intact. It is not uncontested, however: Cameron & Pierce's (1994) rival meta-analysis found the effect narrow and avoidable, Deci et al. explicitly argued that analysis was seriously flawed, and the dispute ran for years — this is an adversarial meta-analytic literature, not a settled one. Both camps agree that verbal praise and UNEXPECTED rewards do not undermine, which is the moderator that actually matters for design.
- **Verifier notes:** The effect is measured on the laboratory free-choice behavioural paradigm over short horizons; the classic Lepper, Greene & Nisbett drawing study is correctly described. Two things to keep separate from the evidence: (1) 'For a skill app whose entire value depends on the user wanting to play the instrument, this is a larger threat than any gambling mechanic' is the claimant's judgment, not a finding — no study compares those two threats. (2) The specific translation from 'expected tangible reward for drawing' to 'XP counter in a practice app' is an extrapolation across reward type, duration and domain; d ≈ -0.3 to -0.4 in a lab does not license a point prediction about Fingerly. The design implication (avoid expected, tangible, contingent rewards; informational feedback and unexpected acknowledgement are safe) is well supported by the moderator structure and is the part worth acting on.

### In the best-known longitudinal classroom test, adding a leaderboard and badges REDUCED intrinsic motivation, satisfaction and empowerment over a semester, and lower final exam scores were mediated by the motivation drop.

- **Status:** CONFIRMED · WEAK
- **Citation:** Hanus, M. D., & Fox, J. (2015). Assessing the effects of gamification in the classroom: A longitudinal study on intrinsic motivation, social comparison, satisfaction, effort, and academic performance. Computers & Education, 80, 152–161.
- **URL:** https://www.sciencedirect.com/science/article/abs/pii/S0360131514001195
- **Peer reviewed:** None
- **Sample / design:** Two sections of the same course over a 16-week semester; one gamified with a leaderboard and badges, one not; repeated measures. Complementary evidence: Christy & Fox (2014), Computers & Education 78, 66–77, found classroom leaderboards produced stereotype-threat and upward-social-comparison effects harming women's math performance.
- **Effect size:** Gamified cohort showed declining motivation/satisfaction/empowerment across four measurement points over 16 weeks; final exam difference mediated by intrinsic motivation. Design is quasi-experimental (two intact course sections), so effect magnitudes should be treated as indicative, not definitive.
- **Kind:** VISIBLE_UI
- **Evidence type:** PEER_REVIEWED
- **Ethical status:** DARK_PATTERN
- **Mechanism:** Leaderboards force social comparison and make relative standing the salient goal. For anyone not near the top, the standing signal is repeated evidence of inadequacy — competence-thwarting in SDT terms, and self-focused feedback in Kluger & DeNisi's terms. It also converts a mastery goal into a performance goal.
- **Piano-app application:** Fingerly has no other users, so a leaderboard is impossible — good. But the same mechanism reappears as comparison against your own past best: a persistent 'personal best' bar shown during play is a leaderboard with one competitor. Show the past-best comparison AFTER a run, as data, never as a live ranking gauge during performance, and never in a form that makes a bad day read as a rank drop.
- **Verifier correction:** Do not present this as strong causal evidence. It is a single quasi-experiment comparing two intact course sections with non-random assignment, roughly 80 students, one instructor, one institution, one semester — precisely the low-rigor design profile that Sailer & Homner's subsplit exists to filter out. Also note the selection problem in citing it: it points opposite to the meta-analytic average (claim 1's g=.36 motivational), so treating it as the canonical longitudinal result is choosing by narrative fit rather than by evidential weight. Citation count is prominence, not validity.
- **Replication:** No designed replication. It sits in tension with the pooled meta-analytic estimates, and Sailer & Homner found competitive-only gamification (leaderboards) underperforms competitive+collaborative designs — which offers a mechanism-consistent reading of Hanus & Fox rather than a contradiction, but that reading is post hoc.
- **Verifier notes:** Citation verified exact (Computers & Education 80, 152-161, DOI 10.1016/j.compedu.2014.08.019; 1,779 citations, so 'best-known' is fair as a description of prominence). Core finding independently confirmed via the Semantic Scholar summary of the paper: 'students in the gamified course showed less motivation, satisfaction, and empowerment over time than those in the non-gamified class.' I could NOT independently verify the mediation sub-clause — the abstract is elided by Elsevier and no OA copy exists (unpaywall returns no OA locations). Treat 'mediated by the motivation drop' as unverified pending access to the full text.

### The 'novelty effect kills gamification in 4–8 weeks' claim is folklore that outruns the evidence. Sailer & Homner found duration was NOT a significant moderator for cognitive (Q(2)=0.17, p=.92) or behavioral (Q(1)=0.28, p=.59) outcomes — and where duration mattered, LONGER interventions did better, not worse.

- **Status:** CONFIRMED · MODERATE
- **Citation:** Sailer & Homner (2020), Educational Psychology Review 32(1), 77–112 (period-of-time moderator). Rodrigues, L., Toda, A. M., Oliveira, W., Palomino, P. T., Avila-Santos, A. P., & Isotani, S. (2022). Gamification suffers from the novelty effect but benefits from the familiarization effect: findings from a longitudinal study. International Journal of Educational Technology in Higher Education, 19, 13.
- **URL:** https://educationaltechnologyjournal.springeropen.com/articles/10.1186/s41239-022-00314-0
- **Peer reviewed:** None
- **Sample / design:** Meta-regression across k=9–19 samples per outcome (many intervention-duration cells sparse), plus a single longitudinal within-subjects study for the U-shape. Widely-circulated '4 to 8 weeks' figures attributed to Koivisto & Hamari (2019) are vendor-blog paraphrase, not a statistic in that review — Koivisto & Hamari's actual conclusion is that results are highly mixed and the field lacks coherent models.
- **Effect size:** Sailer & Homner: for motivational outcomes, interventions lasting up to half a year significantly OUTPERFORMED interventions of one day or less (Q(1)=4.93, p<.05). Rodrigues et al. report a U-shape: effect declines starting around week 4, trough for roughly 2–6 weeks, then partial recovery between weeks 6 and 10 as users familiarize.
- **Kind:** BOTH
- **Evidence type:** PEER_REVIEWED
- **Ethical status:** BENIGN
- **Mechanism:** Novelty decay is real for shallow surface elements, but familiarization runs the other way: once the reward system is legible, users can plan around it. Net effect over months is the sum of two opposing curves, which is why single-number decay claims don't replicate.
- **Piano-app application:** Don't design Fingerly around an assumed 6-week cliff, and don't chase the cliff with an escalating novelty treadmill (new badge types, seasonal events) — that's the mechanism that turns a learning app into a slot machine. Expect a dip somewhere in weeks 4–10 and plan for the familiarization recovery: keep the reward semantics STABLE so the user can learn to plan against them, rather than refreshing cosmetics.
- **Verifier correction:** The claim should carry the authors' own caveat, which it omits: Sailer & Homner explicitly write that 'the subsplit analyses showed that the summary effects for the motivational and behavioral outcomes were not robust. However, given the small sample sizes in the subgroup analyses, these findings were highly likely to be underpowered and should be viewed with caution,' and separately that 'nonsignificant results do not necessarily indicate the absence of an effect but could be explained by a lack of statistical power.' Read the subsplit as 'not demonstrated under rigor,' not as 'shown to be zero.'
- **Replication:** No independent replication of the subsplit exists — it is one moderator analysis in one meta-analysis. The three later meta-analyses cited do not run an equivalent rigor-restricted subsplit, so the pattern is corroborated for the headline effect sizes only, not for the differential survival of the cognitive effect.
- **Verifier notes:** Verified against the full CC-BY PDF.
