Summary effects exact: cognitive g=.49 [0.30,0.69] k=19 N=1686; motivational g=.36 [0.18,0.54] k=16 N=2246; behavioral g=.25 [0.04,0.46] k=9 N=951. High-rigor subsplit (experimental designs, or quasi-experimental with pre+posttest) exact: cognitive g=.42, SE=.14, p<.01, [0.14,0.68], k=9, N=686 — SURVIVES; motivational g=.22, SE=.17, p=.20, [-0.11,0.56], k=7, N=1063 — NON-SIGNIFICANT; behavioral g=.27, SE=.22, p=.22, [-0.16,0.70], k=5, N=667 — NON-SIGNIFICANT. Both corroborating citations verified exact via Crossref (Bai/Hew/Huang: ERR 30, 100322, DOI 10.1016/j.edurev.2020.100322; Huang/Ritzhaupt/Sommer/Zhu/Stephen/Valle/Hampton/Li: ETR&D 68(4), 1875-1901, DOI 10.1007/s11423-020-09807-z). The 0.25–0.50 band is consistent with Li, Hew & Du (2024) g=.257 at the low end. Not STRONG because the load-bearing subsplit rests on k=5–9.

### Feedback improves performance on average (d = .41) but made performance WORSE in over one third of cases; effectiveness falls as feedback moves attention away from the task and toward the self. This is the single strongest empirical basis for banning praise/cheerleading copy.

- **Status:** CONFIRMED · STRONG
- **Citation:** Kluger, A. N., & DeNisi, A. (1996). The effects of feedback interventions on performance: A historical review, a meta-analysis, and a preliminary feedback intervention theory. Psychological Bulletin, 119(2), 254–284.
- **URL:** https://mrbartonmaths.com/resourcesnew/8.%20Research/Marking%20and%20Feedback/The%20effects%20of%20feedback%20interventions.pdf
- **Peer reviewed:** None
- **Sample / design:** Meta-analysis of controlled feedback experiments across education, work, and lab tasks, spanning ~90 years of studies.
- **Effect size:** 607 effect sizes, 23,663 observations, mean d = .41; ~38% of feedback interventions produced negative effects on subsequent performance. Feedback Intervention Theory: attention moves among task-learning, task-motivation, and meta-task (self) levels; effectiveness decreases monotonically as attention rises toward the self.
- **Kind:** VISIBLE_UI
- **Evidence type:** PEER_REVIEWED
- **Ethical status:** BENIGN
- **Mechanism:** Self-level feedback ('you're great', 'you're behind') triggers self-evaluation and ego defence, which consumes the working memory the task needs. Task-level feedback ('the F# in bar 12 was 40ms late') keeps attention on the discrepancy that can actually be closed.
- **Piano-app application:** Direct validation of SPEC §2's 'blunt metrics only' rule — and it sharpens it. The rule is not 'no positive feedback'; it's 'no SELF-directed feedback'. 'Nice job!' and 'You're a natural' are self-level and carry a ~1-in-3 chance of harming the next run. 'Bar 12, right hand, 3 late notes' is task-level. Post-run summaries should name the specific defect and its location, and the celebration animation (if any) should be tied to a task event, not a personal attribute.
- **Verifier correction:** Strip the last sentence; it is the claimant's design inference, not the paper's finding, and it is over-extended in two ways. (a) Kluger & DeNisi studied feedback interventions — knowledge of results, performance feedback, goal-referenced information — not UI praise copy. Feedback Intervention Theory predicts that SELF-directed feedback is harmful; it does not license a blanket ban on any encouraging language. (b) It contradicts claim 4's own source: Deci, Koestner & Ryan (1999) found positive verbal feedback ENHANCED free-choice intrinsic motivation (d=+0.33) and self-reported interest (d=+0.31). Two claims in this set therefore point to opposite copy decisions. The reconcilable reading is that task-referenced informational feedback helps while self-referenced evaluative feedback ('you're a natural!') hurts — which supports blunt metrics over person-praise, but on a narrower and better-specified basis than 'feedback is a third harmful.'
- **Replication:** The d=.41 average and the harmful-third finding are widely reproduced in later syntheses of feedback (e.g. subsequent work by Hattie & Timperley and by Shute converges on both the average positive effect and the substantial harmful tail). The 'over one third' figure has drawn methodological discussion regarding how the proportion was computed from the effect-size distribution, so quote it as approximate rather than as a precise base rate.
- **Verifier notes:** Every empirical element verified verbatim from the abstract: 'A meta-analysis (607 effect sizes; 23,663 observations) suggests that FIs improved performance on average (d = .41) but that over 1/3 of the FIs decreased performance. This finding cannot be explained by sampling error, feedback sign, or existing theories... The results suggest that FI effectiveness decreases as attention moves up the hierarchy closer to the self and away from the task.' Citation exact (Psych Bulletin 119(2), 254-284). Large corpus, mechanism explicitly tested via moderator analyses, and the core finding is durable in the feedback literature — this is the one claim in the set that earns STRONG.

### Rewarding INPUTS (behaviours the learner knows how to produce) works; rewarding OUTPUTS (test scores) does not — because learners don't know the production function mapping effort to results.

- **Status:** MISCHARACTERIZED · WEAK
- **Citation:** Fryer, R. G. (2011). Financial incentives and student achievement: Evidence from randomized trials. Quarterly Journal of Economics, 126(4), 1755–1798.
- **URL:** https://academic.oup.com/qje/article/126/4/1755/1924375
- **Peer reviewed:** None
- **Sample / design:** School-based randomized controlled trials across 250+ urban schools, ~38,000 students, three cities, distinct incentive structures.
- **Effect size:** Impact on state test scores was statistically zero in every city where output was rewarded (New York: interim assessments; Chicago: grades). Dallas, where students were paid $2 per book read (an input), produced the only significant achievement gains, concentrated in English-speaking students. Qualitative interviews: students rewarded for outputs were excited but reported not knowing what to do differently.
- **Kind:** INVISIBLE_MECHANIC
- **Evidence type:** PEER_REVIEWED
- **Ethical status:** BENIGN
- **Mechanism:** An incentive can only change behaviour the learner can select. Paying for a score is paying for something the learner cannot directly choose; paying for a legible, executable action converts money into behaviour, and the behaviour into learning.
- **Piano-app application:** This is the crispest guide to what Fingerly should reward. Reward specific, executable practice actions the user can choose right now — 'played bars 9–16 hands-separate at 60bpm five times', 'did the slow pass before the tempo pass' — not the outcome 'accuracy improved'. Rewarding accuracy directly is Fryer's New York arm: the user cannot select accuracy, only the actions that produce it. Caveat before over-applying: Fryer's inputs were still tangible cash, and Deci et al. would predict undermining if the same structure ran for months on an already-loved activity.
- **Verifier correction:** Rewrite as: 'Fryer's three-city experiment (200+ urban schools) found statistically zero effect of financial incentives on achievement in all three cities. The single significant result was among English-speaking students in Dallas, where the incentive was for an input (reading books) rather than an output — which is suggestive but is one subgroup in one arm of a null study.' Two specific fixes: (1) 'Rewarding inputs works' rests on a subgroup finding inside a paper whose stated result is a null, and Fryer explicitly attributes the overall nulls partly to insufficient power — so neither 'works' nor 'does not work' is licensed at the strength claimed. (2) The production-function mechanism is labelled by Fryer himself as speculative discussion; presenting it as the causal explanation ('because learners don't know...') converts an author's conjecture into a finding.
- **Verifier notes:** Citation exact (QJE 126(4), 1755-1798). But the paper's headline result is a NULL, and the claim inverts its emphasis. Fryer's own abstract: 'In Dallas, students were paid to read books. In New York, students were rewarded for performance on interim assessments. In Chicago, students were paid for classroom grades. I estimate that the impact of financial incentives on student achievement is statistically 0, in each city. Due to a lack of power, however, I cannot rule out the possibility of effect sizes that would have positive returns on investment. The only statistically significant effect is on English-speaking students in Dallas. The article concludes with a SPECULATIVE discussion of what might account for intercity differences.'

### Specific, difficult goals beat 'do your best' (d ≈ .42–.82), but the advantage shrinks as tasks get more complex — and on complex tasks, LEARNING goals (find and apply N strategies) outperform outcome/performance goals.

- **Status:** CONFIRMED · MODERATE
- **Citation:** Locke, E. A., & Latham, G. P. (2002). Building a practically useful theory of goal setting and task motivation. American Psychologist, 57(9), 705–717. Wood, Mento & Locke (1987), Journal of Applied Psychology (task-complexity moderator). Winters, D., & Latham, G. P. (1996). The effect of learning versus outcome goals on a simple versus a complex task. Group & Organization Management, 21(2), 236–250.
- **URL:** https://med.stanford.edu/content/dam/sm/s-spire/documents/PD.locke-and-latham-retrospective_Paper.pdf
- **Peer reviewed:** None
- **Sample / design:** ~35 years and 400+ studies across lab and field, mostly adults on work tasks; the learning-vs-outcome goal contrast rests on smaller controlled experiments.
- **Effect size:** Goal specificity/difficulty d = .42–.82 across meta-analyses. Task complexity moderates: d = .76 for the simplest tasks (reaction time, brainstorming), d ≈ .48 for the most complex. Winters & Latham: on a complex task, a learning goal produced significantly higher performance than either an assigned outcome goal or 'do your best', and produced significantly more effective task strategies.
- **Kind:** BOTH
- **Evidence type:** PEER_REVIEWED
- **Ethical status:** BENIGN
- **Mechanism:** On complex tasks an outcome goal creates pressure without direction — the learner doesn't yet possess a strategy, and arousal crowds out strategy search. A learning goal makes strategy discovery itself the target, which is the actual bottleneck.
- **Piano-app application:** Sight-reading and coordinating two hands are complex tasks, so Fingerly's session goals should be learning goals, not outcome goals: 'find a fingering for bars 9–16 that lets you keep the RH legato' beats 'get 95% accuracy'. This also gives SPEC §2's 'no open-ended sessions' rule its content — a bounded session needs a specific, difficult, learning-framed target stated before the first note, which is compatible with 'no decision menus' as long as the app picks the target rather than asking.
- **Verifier correction:** Two calibration notes rather than errors. (1) The d ≈ .42–.82 range is Locke & Latham's own narrative summary of their research program in a review article, not an independent meta-analytic estimate — cite it as the theory authors' summary, not as a pooled effect size from a neutral synthesis. (2) The learning-goals-on-complex-tasks result rests on small single-lab studies conducted by the theory's own proponents (Winters & Latham n=114), not on a meta-analysis; it is a plausible and well-motivated finding, but it is not replicated evidence at the level the claim's confident phrasing implies.
- **Replication:** The core specific-difficult-goals effect is among the better-replicated findings in organisational psychology and does not belong to the failed-replication categories. However, goal-setting theory has a documented critique literature on side effects — narrowed focus, unethical behaviour, risk-taking, reduced intrinsic motivation (Ordóñez, Schweitzer, Galinsky & Bazerman, 2009, 'Goals Gone Wild,' Academy of Management Perspectives) — which is directly relevant to a system that gates practice behind goals and is not acknowledged in the claim.
- **Verifier notes:** Locke & Latham 2002 verified exact (American Psychologist 57(9), 705-717; full title 'Building a practically useful theory of goal setting and task motivation: A 35-year odyssey'). Winters & Latham 1996 verified exact (Group & Organization Management 21(2), 236-250) and its abstract supports the third sub-claim precisely: 'On a simple task an outcome goal led to significantly more correct schedules being produced than urging people to do their best. On a complex task, assigning a learning goal led to performance that was significantly higher than either an assigned outcome goal or being urged to do one's best' (n=114). Wood, Mento & Locke (1987, JAP) is the real task-complexity moderator meta-analysis.

### Mastery-gated progression — you cannot advance until you clear a criterion — is one of the better-supported structures in educational technology, and it is a structural mechanic, not a reward. Effects are real but contested and shrink sharply on standardized (as opposed to experimenter-made) tests.

- **Status:** MISCHARACTERIZED · MODERATE
- **Citation:** Kulik, C.-L. C., Kulik, J. A., & Bangert-Drowns, R. L. (1990). Effectiveness of mastery learning programs: A meta-analysis. Review of Educational Research, 60(2), 265–299. Critique: Slavin, R. E. (1987). Mastery learning reconsidered. RER, 57(2), 175–213. Modern RCT: Oreopoulos, Gibbs, Jenson & Price (2024), Khan Academy mastery-learning trial.
- **URL:** https://www.uky.edu/~gmswan3/575/kulik_kulik_Bangert-Drowns_1990.pdf
- **Peer reviewed:** None
- **Sample / design:** Kulik: meta-analysis, mixed designs, heavy on experimenter-made outcomes (the same measurement problem as the gamification literature). Slavin: best-evidence synthesis with stricter inclusion. Khan: cluster RCT — the cleanest estimate, and notably the smallest.
- **Effect size:** Kulik et al.: 108 controlled evaluations, positive effects on exam performance (~0.5 SD range), strongest for weaker students in a class, varying by mastery procedure and design. Slavin, restricting to studies ≥4 weeks with standardized outcomes, found essentially NO effect on standardized achievement and only a small effect on experimenter-made measures — retaining roughly a quarter of Kulik's studies. Khan Academy year-long mastery RCT, ~11,000 students grades 3–8: +0.12 to +0.22 SD.
- **Kind:** INVISIBLE_MECHANIC
- **Evidence type:** PEER_REVIEWED
- **Ethical status:** BENIGN
- **Mechanism:** Gating enforces the thing that actually produces skill — you keep practising the specific weak component until it's fixed, instead of accumulating shallow exposure across many items. It also makes competence feedback unavoidable and honest.
- **Piano-app application:** Mastery gating is the highest-evidence 'game mechanic' available to Fingerly and it costs no reward layer: don't unlock the next section of a piece until the current one clears a criterion (e.g. 3 consecutive runs, 0 wrong notes, timing error under threshold at target tempo). Set expectations from the Khan RCT (0.12–0.22 SD), not from Kulik's 0.5 — the older, larger numbers are inflated by exactly the experimenter-made-measure artifact seen in the gamification meta-analyses.
- **Verifier correction:** Three corrections. (1) The third citation does not exist as described. There is no 'Oreopoulos, Gibbs, Jenson & Price (2024) Khan Academy mastery-learning trial.' The real paper is Oreopoulos, Gibbs, JENSEN (Michael D.) & Price (2024), 'Teaching Teachers To Use Computer Assisted Learning Effectively: Experimental and Quasi-Experimental Evidence,' NBER working paper — surname misspelled, and its subject is teacher training in computer-assisted learning, not a mastery-learning trial. It is also an unrefereed working paper, so the claim's PEER_REVIEWED evidence label does not hold for it. (Adjacent real work: Eames, Brunskill, Yamkovenko, Weatherholtz & Oreopoulos, PNAS, on how Khan Academy influences student math learning; and an AEA-registered 'Khollaboration with Khan Academy' RCT by Oreopoulos, Gibbs & Price.) (2) 'Shrink sharply on standardized tests' understates Slavin, whose abstract is blunter: the review 'found essentially NO evidence to support the effectiveness of group-based mastery learning on standardized achievement measures. On experimenter-made measures, effects were generally positive but moderate in magnitude, with little evidence that effects maintained over time.' That is a null plus a durability problem, not a shrinkage. (3) 'One of the better-supported structures in educational technology' overreaches: both syntheses concern pre-digital, group-based classroom mastery learning from the 1980s, and the methodologically stricter of the two (best-evidence synthesis) found nothing on standardized outcomes. The instrument-dependence pattern here is the same one that undercuts claim 2 — and it cuts against mastery gating just as it cuts against gamification.
- **Replication:** Classic case of a finding whose size depends on who wrote the test: Kulik et al.'s meta-analysis (positive) and Slavin's best-evidence synthesis (null on standardized measures) were computed over overlapping literature with different inclusion rules and reached opposite conclusions — an unresolved 1980s dispute, not a replicated effect. Bloom's '2-sigma' figure that both were reacting to has never replicated at that magnitude.
- **Verifier notes:** The two meta-analytic citations are exact and correctly characterised in direction. Kulik, Kulik & Bangert-Drowns (1990), RER 60(2), 265-299: 'A meta-analysis of findings from 108 controlled evaluations showed that mastery learning programs have positive effects on the examination performance of students... The effects appear to be stronger on the weaker students in a class.' Slavin (1987), RER 57(2), 175-213 verified exact. The problem is the third citation and two overstatements.

### When a learning system rewards a countable proxy, a substantial fraction of learners optimize the proxy instead of learning — 'gaming the system' — and this behaviour is specifically concentrated on the material the learner understands LEAST, i.e. exactly where learning was most needed.

- **Status:** MISCHARACTERIZED · MODERATE
- **Citation:** Baker, R. S. J. d., Walonoski, J., Heffernan, N., Roll, I., Corbett, A., & Koedinger, K. (2008). Why students engage in 'gaming the system' behavior in interactive learning environments. Journal of Interactive Learning Research, 19(2), 185–224. Foundational: Baker, Corbett, Koedinger & Wagner (2004), CHI, 'Off-task behavior in the Cognitive Tutor classroom: When students game the system'.
- **URL:** https://pact.cs.cmu.edu/pubs/Baker,%20R.,%20Walonoski,%20J.A.,%20Heffernan,%20N.T.,%20Roll,%20I.%20Corbett,%20A.,%20Koedinger,%20K.R..pdf
- **Peer reviewed:** None
- **Sample / design:** Four studies across two intelligent tutoring systems (Cognitive Tutor, ASSISTments), combining classroom observation, log-data detectors, and questionnaires, plus a teacher-perspective study.
- **Effect size:** Gaming defined as 'attempting to succeed in an educational environment by exploiting properties of the system rather than by learning the material'. Repeatedly associated with poorer learning across Cognitive Tutor and ASSISTments studies. Critically, harmful gaming clusters on problems the student knows least well; 'non-harmful' gaming (speeding through already-mastered items) shows no learning penalty. Gaming correlates with performance goals rather than learning goals, and with disliking the subject matter.
- **Kind:** INVISIBLE_MECHANIC
- **Evidence type:** PEER_REVIEWED
- **Ethical status:** NEEDS_CARE
- **Mechanism:** Any reward attached to a countable surrogate (items completed, hints consumed, minutes elapsed) creates a cheaper path to the reward than learning. Learners take it precisely when learning is hardest, because that's when the cost gap is widest.
- **Piano-app application:** This is the mechanism by which a reward layer displaces learning rather than supporting it. If Fingerly rewards minutes practised or notes played, the optimal strategy is to play something easy fast — and the user will drift there precisely on the passages they can't play. Instrument the detector, not just the reward: flag runs where tempo/difficulty dropped while volume rose, and refuse to count a session toward mastery when the played material is already mastered. Because Fingerly captures MIDI, this is directly measurable — no self-report needed.
- **Verifier correction:** Three corrections. (1) Attribution: the 'hardest steps' finding is not from the 2008 paper the claim leads with — Baker et al. 2008 credits it to Baker, Corbett & Koedinger (2004), a different, three-author paper, not the four-author CHI paper cited. Baker et al. 2008's own contribution is about WHY students game (it tests hypotheses about disliking mathematics, disliking computers, and situational frustration), not about where gaming concentrates. (2) The paper distinguishes HARMFUL from non-harmful gaming, and it is specifically harmful gamers who game on the hardest steps. Not all gaming is associated with poorer learning, and flattening the distinction overstates the danger. (3) Category extension: the claim frames this as what happens 'when a learning system rewards a countable proxy,' but Cognitive Tutor and ASSISTments have no reward or points economy — gaming there means systematically abusing hints and feedback to advance through mastery gating. The evidence is about help-abuse under mastery gating, which is a real and directly relevant hazard for a mastery-gated design, but it is not evidence about reward proxies. Also, 'a substantial fraction' is unquantified in the claim and should carry an actual number if asserted.
- **Replication:** Gaming detection replicates across systems — the 2008 paper deliberately used two independent environments (Cognitive Tutor and ASSISTments) with independent detection methods for exactly this reason, and the behaviour has since been observed widely in intelligent tutoring systems. That cross-system reproduction is why this rates MODERATE rather than WEAK, despite the mis-framing.
- **Verifier notes:** Read the 2008 paper directly. Citation exact — JILR 19(2), 185-224, all six authors correct. The 2004 CHI paper 'Off-task behavior in the Cognitive Tutor classroom: When students game the system' is real. The gaming phenomenon and its concentration on hard material are genuinely documented: the paper states 'there is already preliminary evidence about when students game... showing that students game on steps they personally find difficult,' and later 'evidence that harmful gamers game on the hardest steps (Baker, Corbett, & Koedinger, 2004).' So the substance is broadly right, with three qualifications the claim collapses.

### In music specifically, accumulated practice TIME is a weak and partly spurious predictor of achievement; practice QUALITY and self-regulation carry most of the variance. Formal practice time explained only ~18% of musical achievement in college music students.

- **Status:** MISCHARACTERIZED · WEAK
- **Citation:** Bonneville-Roussy, A., & Bouffard, T. (2015). When quantity is not enough: Disentangling the roles of practice time, self-regulation and deliberate practice in musical achievement. Psychology of Music, 43(5), 686–704. Platz, F., Kopiez, R., Lehmann, A. C., & Wolf, A. (2014). The influence of deliberate practice on musical achievement: a meta-analysis. Frontiers in Psychology, 5, 646.
- **URL:** https://journals.sagepub.com/doi/10.1177/0305735614534910
- **Peer reviewed:** None
- **Sample / design:** Cross-sectional and longitudinal studies of conservatory/university musicians; meta-analysis over correlational studies (so reverse causation — better players practise more deliberately — is not fully excluded).
- **Effect size:** Bonneville-Roussy & Bouffard: formal practice time explained ~18% of achievement variance; more experienced musicians practise more self-regulatedly and need LESS time to reach a goal. Platz et al.: 13 studies, corrected correlation r = .61 between deliberate practice and music performance — but 'deliberate practice' there means goal-directed, feedback-rich, effortful practice on weaknesses, not hours logged. Contested: Macnamara, Hambrick & Oswald (2014) put deliberate practice at ~21% of variance in music.
- **Kind:** INVISIBLE_MECHANIC
- **Evidence type:** PEER_REVIEWED
- **Ethical status:** BENIGN
- **Mechanism:** Deliberate practice is defined by targeting a specific weakness with immediate feedback and immediate correction. Time-on-instrument includes playing pieces you already know, which produces enjoyment but little skill change.
- **Piano-app application:** The single most important design consequence for Fingerly: a practice STREAK or a minutes-practised counter rewards the variable that explains ~18% of outcomes, and (per Baker) actively incentivises playing easy material. Replace time-based counters with deliberate-practice counters that MIDI can compute honestly — number of distinct weak passages isolated and cleared, error rate on those passages, tempo at which a passage first went clean. If the app shows exactly one number, make it 'passages cleared at target tempo', not 'days in a row'.
- **Verifier correction:** Remove Platz et al. as support and replace it. Platz, Kopiez, Lehmann & Wolf (2014), Frontiers in Psychology 5:646 is the strongest PRO-practice meta-analysis in the field: 13 studies, N=788, aggregated rc = 0.61, 95% CI [0.54, 0.67] — roughly 37% of variance — concluding verbatim 'Our results corroborate the CENTRAL role of long-term (deliberate) practice for explaining expert performance in music.' Citing it for 'practice time is a weak and partly spurious predictor' inverts its conclusion. The meta-analysis that actually supports the skeptical position is Macnamara, Hambrick & Oswald (2014), 'Deliberate Practice and Performance in Music, Games, Sports, Education, and Professions: A Meta-Analysis,' Psychological Science, DOI 10.1177/0956797614535810 — which found deliberate practice explained roughly 21% of variance in music (note it carries a 2018 corrigendum, DOI 10.1177/0956797618769891, and its figures should be quoted from the corrected version). Also drop or source the '~18%' figure, which I could not verify.
- **Replication:** This is an actively contested area with two meta-analyses of overlapping literature reaching materially different variance estimates (Platz et al. ~37% vs Macnamara et al. ~21%), driven by differing definitions of deliberate practice and differing inclusion criteria — and Macnamara et al. required a published corrigendum. The honest summary for design purposes is that practice time is a substantial but far from sufficient predictor, and that quality/self-regulation add incremental variance. 'Weak and partly spurious' is not supportable from either meta-analysis.
- **Verifier notes:** Bonneville-Roussy & Bouffard is real and correctly cited (Psychology of Music, DOI 10.1177/0305735614534910), and its abstract does support the qualitative direction: 'even though most scholars agree that formal practice time plays an important role in musical achievement, empirical investigations have failed to show consistent associations between practice time and achievement... we propose that formal practice should be defined as a goal-directed and focused period of practice that includes both self-regulation and deliberate practice strategies. We further posit that practice time will predict musical achievement only if associated with formal practice.' The specific ~18% figure does not appear in any source I could access and remains unverified. The Platz citation, however, is used backwards — this is the most serious problem in the claim.

### Duolingo's public disclosures document large ENGAGEMENT effects from gamification (4.5x DAU over four years, +21% retention, +12% DAU from a spaced-repetition model) — but every published figure optimizes retention, not learning; its one peer-reviewed efficacy study measures outcomes without attributing them to the gamification layer.

- **Status:** MISCHARACTERIZED · WEAK
- **Citation:** Mazal, J. (2023). How Duolingo reignited user growth. Lenny's Newsletter (former Duolingo CPO). Settles, B., & Meeder, B. (2016). A trainable spaced repetition model for language learning. ACL 2016, 1848–1858. Jiang, X., Rollinson, J., Plonsky, L., Gustafson, E., & Pajak, B. (2021). Evaluating the reading and listening outcomes of beginning-level Duolingo courses. Foreign Language Annals, 54(4), 974–1002.
- **URL:** https://www.lennysnewsletter.com/p/how-duolingo-reignited-user-growth
- **Peer reviewed:** None
- **Sample / design:** Mazal: uncontrolled product-metric narrative, no counterfactual, strong survivorship and selection framing. Settles & Meeder: genuine operational A/B test, but the outcome is engagement, not measured recall gain. Jiang et al.: peer-reviewed, but single-arm with self-selected completers.
- **Effect size:** Mazal: DAU grew 4.5x in four years; current-user retention rate +21% (≈40% reduction in daily churn of best users); share of DAU with 7+ day streaks nearly tripled to >half of DAU; 600+ experiments run on the streak feature alone with the stated objective of improving DAU. Settles & Meeder: half-life regression cut recall-prediction error 45%+ vs baselines and improved daily engagement 12% in a live A/B test. Jiang et al.: beginners reached ACTFL Intermediate Low reading / Novice High listening, comparable to four university semesters in about half the time — with no gamification-vs-no-gamification comparison.
- **Kind:** BOTH
- **Evidence type:** INDUSTRY_TALK
- **Ethical status:** DARK_PATTERN
- **Mechanism:** Streaks work through loss aversion and the sunk-cost of an accumulated count; leagues work through social comparison; both drive returning, which is a necessary condition for spaced practice. The mechanism is orthogonal to whether each session teaches anything. Related lab evidence: an artificial head start toward a goal nearly doubled loyalty-card completion for identical real effort (Nunes & Drèze 2006, Journal of Consumer Research), and effort accelerates near goal completion (Kivetz, Urminsky & Zheng 2006, JMR) — real levers, but levers on persistence, not comprehension.
- **Piano-app application:** Read Duolingo's numbers as evidence about RETENTION MECHANICS, never as evidence about teaching. The streak in particular is the clearest dark pattern in the set: it makes a missed day feel like destroying something you built, and Duolingo itself found streak loss demoralized users enough to make them quit (hence streak freezes/amulets, sold or granted to protect the metric). For a single-user, no-backend, no-analytics app whose owner already wants to practise, a streak imports the manipulation without the business case. If you want the persistence benefit honestly, use the endowed-progress finding on a MASTERY goal rather than an attendance goal — show a piece as 3/12 sections cleared rather than 'day 47'.
- **Verifier correction:** 'Reliably produce' is not supported by anything cited. Silverman & Barasch establishes that consumers adopt streak MAINTENANCE as a goal in itself — that is the necessary first half. It does NOT show the second half: that the underlying skill goal is displaced, or that learning outcomes suffer. No controlled study is offered showing that streak-driven minimal sessions degrade acquisition. The surrogation literature invoked is management-accounting work on performance measures substituting for strategic constructs — a different domain with no demonstrated transfer to skill learning. Downgrade to: 'plausibly produces goal displacement; the mechanism's first stage is evidenced, its consequence for learning is not.'
- **Replication:** Nothing to replicate — the operative evidence is user self-report from Duolingo communities and product commentary. Self-selected complaint communities are a biased sample for estimating prevalence, and 'widely report' is doing unearned quantitative work.
- **Verifier notes:** The claim is honestly labelled PRODUCT_OBSERVATION, which is to its credit, and the design implication for a practice app is sound engineering caution regardless of evidence strength. Interestingly, Veiga et al. (2025) — cited elsewhere in this set — found Grinding ranked 11th of 13 on harmfulness and 12th of 13 on problematic nature, with players describing it as a normal part of games; that is weak evidence against players themselves experiencing counter-chasing as harm. Keep the design caution, drop 'reliably'.

---

## ADHD × gamification: where general game-design advice needs modification or reversal for a neurodivergent adult

### ADHD symptoms and gaming disorder are robustly associated at moderate-to-large magnitude, and the association is bidirectional in cross-sectional data. This is the base-rate context for every other decision: an engagement mechanic aimed at an ADHD adult is being deployed into a population with an elevated prior for losing control of the activity.

- **Status:** CONFIRMED · STRONG
- **Citation:** Koncz P, Demetrovics Z, Takacs ZK, Griffiths MD, Nagy T, Király O (2023). The emerging evidence on the association between symptoms of ADHD and gaming disorder: A systematic review and meta-analysis. Clinical Psychology Review, 106:102343.
- **URL:** https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/37883910/
- **Peer reviewed:** None
- **Sample / design:** Systematic review + meta-analysis of correlational, group-comparison, and SEM studies; authors explicitly flag that the evidence base is dominated by cross-sectional self-report and call for longitudinal designs with clinical assessment.
- **Effect size:** Pooled r = .296 for overall ADHD symptoms × GD severity (inattention r = .306; hyperactivity r = .266). Group comparisons larger: Hedges g = 0.693 for GD severity in ADHD vs non-ADHD; g = 0.854 for ADHD severity in GD vs non-GD. Studies with higher male proportion and broader 'problematic internet use' measures showed stronger associations.
- **Kind:** INVISIBLE_MECHANIC
- **Evidence type:** PEER_REVIEWED
- **Ethical status:** NEEDS_CARE
- **Concrete example:** Contrast with Duolingo/Elevate-style apps whose north-star metric is DAU and streak length; for an ADHD user, maximising those metrics is maximising the exact variable that distinguishes healthy from disordered use.
- **Mechanism:** Games supply the reinforcement profile (immediate, dense, contingent, novel) that ADHD reward circuitry is unusually responsive to, while ADHD-related deficits in inhibitory control and time monitoring make disengagement harder. The same features that make the medium effective make it sticky.
- **Piano-app application:** Treat 'this app is engaging' as a risk signal, not a success metric, when the single user has ADHD. Fingerly should instrument time-on-app and session-overrun as a *monitored harm*, not a KPI: log actual vs intended session length locally and surface it bluntly (SPEC anti-pattern rules already forbid cheerleading copy — extend that to engagement telemetry framing).
- **Verifier correction:** Drop the word 'bidirectional'. Every primary study in this meta-analysis is cross-sectional; the authors report the association measured from two comparison directions (GD severity in ADHD vs non-ADHD, g=0.693; ADHD severity in GD vs non-GD, g=0.854), which is measurement symmetry, not bidirectional causation. The authors' own closing sentence calls for 'more rigorous methodologies... (e.g. longitudinal studies and studies using professional/clinical ratings and diagnosis)'. Also, the authors label r=0.296 'moderate', not 'moderate-to-large'; the 'large' half of the phrase comes from the Hedges' g group comparisons, which are a different estimand — do not merge the two into one magnitude.
- **Replication:** Meta-analytic synthesis of a large replicated literature — the existence of the association is not in doubt. What has NOT been established by this source is direction, causality, or clinical thresholds. Claim 2's cohort is the appropriate evidence for direction; this one cannot carry it.
- **Verifier notes:** Citation is exact on every field (authors, year, journal, volume, article number). Pooled r=0.296 combined; inattention r=0.306, hyperactivity r=0.266. Moderators: higher estimates where samples had more males, where problematic internet use was measured instead of GD proper, and in more recently published studies — the second of those is a construct-validity warning, since PIU scales are not GD scales. Residual concern the abstract does not resolve: GD and ADHD self-report instruments share item content (difficulty stopping, poor time management, distractibility), so some of r=0.296 is common-method and construct overlap rather than comorbidity. The EXAMPLE attached to this claim (Duolingo/Elevate north-star metrics) is the claimant's own editorial framing and appears nowhere in the source.

### Prospective evidence supports ADHD → problematic gaming, not just correlation. In a 2-year cohort, ADHD symptoms independently predicted both the *onset* and the *persistence* of high-risk internet gaming disorder after adjustment for gaming time and depression.

- **Status:** CONFIRMED · MODERATE
- **Citation:** Jeong H, Yim HW, Lee SY, Lee HK, Potenza MN, Lee H (2021). Factors associated with severity, incidence or persistence of internet gaming disorder in children and adolescents: a 2-year longitudinal study. Addiction, 116(7):1828–1838.
- **URL:** https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/33283397/
- **Peer reviewed:** None
- **Sample / design:** Prospective cohort, n = 2,319 South Korean 3rd/4th/7th graders, 2-year follow-up, IGUESS ≥10 as high-risk threshold. Limitation: children/adolescents, self-report screener, single country — extrapolation to a diagnosed adult is inferential.
- **Effect size:** Adjusted rate ratio 2.53 for incidence of high-risk IGD and 2.14 for persistence, attributable to ADHD symptoms. Playing ≥240 min/day: aRR 2.03 (incidence), 2.63 (persistence). Protective: parental attachment aRR 0.86, social support 0.87.
- **Kind:** INVISIBLE_MECHANIC
- **Evidence type:** PEER_REVIEWED
- **Ethical status:** NEEDS_CARE
- **Mechanism:** ADHD is temporally prior, so the mechanism is not merely 'gaming causes attention problems.' Delay aversion and weak inhibitory control make the transition from voluntary to compulsive engagement faster once a dense-reinforcement medium is available.
- **Piano-app application:** Justifies designing termination into Fingerly at the architecture level rather than as a settings toggle. SPEC's 'no open-ended sessions' anti-pattern is the single most ADHD-protective decision in the spec — a session should have a predetermined end that the app enforces, and 'continue' should require an explicit, slightly effortful action rather than being the default path.
- **Verifier correction:** Add the population. This is 2,319 South Korean schoolchildren (3rd, 4th and 7th graders) from the iCURE cohort — not adults. A design decision for an ADHD adult learning piano is being justified by a finding in 9- and 13-year-olds playing online multiplayer games. The claim should say so.
- **Replication:** Single prospective cohort in one country with one cultural gaming context. I found no independent replication of the incidence/persistence aRRs. Rated MODERATE rather than STRONG on that basis — it is the right KIND of evidence (prospective, adjusted) but it is n=1 study.
- **Verifier notes:** Numbers check out precisely and support the claim as stated: ADHD symptoms predicted incidence (aRR 2.53) and persistence (aRR 2.14) of high-risk IGD, with adjustment covering time spent gaming, game type, depressive and anxiety symptoms, social support, parental communication and attachment. Note that depressive symptoms independently predicted incidence too (aRR 2.04) at nearly the same magnitude, so ADHD is not a uniquely potent predictor — it is one of several. Also note the exposure that dominated both models was ≥240 min/day of gaming (aRR 2.03 incidence, 2.63 persistence); a 20-minute daily piano practice session is not in the same regime, and this study offers no evidence about short structured sessions.

### ADHD adults discount delayed rewards significantly more steeply than controls. Any reward whose payoff is deferred past the current session is materially weaker for an ADHD user than the general gamification literature assumes.

- **Status:** CONFIRMED · STRONG
- **Citation:** Jackson JNS, MacKillop J (2016). Attention-Deficit/Hyperactivity Disorder and Monetary Delay Discounting: A Meta-Analysis of Case-Control Studies. Biological Psychiatry: Cognitive Neuroscience and Neuroimaging, 1(4):316–325.
- **URL:** https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/27722208/
- **Peer reviewed:** None
- **Sample / design:** Meta-analysis of case-control monetary delay-discounting studies across child and adult samples.
- **Effect size:** d = 0.43 (medium), p < 10^-15 — one of the most statistically secure effects in the ADHD cognitive literature.
- **Kind:** BOTH
- **Evidence type:** PEER_REVIEWED
- **Ethical status:** BENIGN
- **Mechanism:** Steeper hyperbolic discounting means the subjective value of a reward collapses faster with delay. 'Practice today, recital in three months' is near-zero motivational value; 'this bar was clean, here is the proof, right now' retains value.
- **Piano-app application:** Reward granularity should be per-phrase or per-bar, not per-session or per-week. Fingerly's <15ms keypress-to-visual budget is not just a latency nicety — it is the reward-delay budget. Push the *evidence of improvement* (tempo achieved, error count on this passage) to immediately after the passage ends, not to an end-of-session summary screen. Weekly/monthly progress views are fine as reference material but should never be the primary reinforcer.
- **Verifier correction:** The empirical half is confirmed; the design inference in the second sentence is not supported by this source and should be stated as an assumption, not a finding. Three gaps: (1) the paradigm is MONETARY delay discounting over delays of days to years, not skill-acquisition feedback across practice sessions — nothing here licenses a claim about 'past the current session'; (2) d=0.43 is a group-mean shift with roughly two-thirds distributional overlap, so it does not describe an individual user as categorically insensitive to deferred reward; (3) the meta-analysis found NO significant age moderation (<18 vs >18), so 'ADHD adults' is not separately established — it is subsumed in the pooled estimate.
- **Replication:** Meta-analytic, low heterogeneity, publication bias explicitly tested — this is one of the better-replicated findings in the entire set. Delay discounting elevation in ADHD survives across children and adults, real and hypothetical rewards, and comorbid CD/ODD status.
- **Verifier notes:** Citation exact on all fields. 21 investigations, 25 case-control comparisons, total N=3,913, d=0.43, p<10⁻¹⁵. The abstract reports 'minimal heterogeneity' and addresses publication bias via fail-safe N, Begg-Mazumdar, Egger and metaregression of publication year — a well-conducted synthesis by 2016 standards. Medium effect, correctly characterised as 'significantly more steeply'; the word 'significantly' is doing statistical rather than practical work here and should not be read as 'dramatically'.

### ADHD shows ventral-striatal HYPO-responsiveness specifically during reward ANTICIPATION, while the relationship runs the opposite direction in healthy high-impulsivity individuals. Anticipation-building mechanics (countdowns to a reward, 'your chest unlocks in 4h', teaser reveals) exploit a system that is blunted in ADHD.

- **Status:** MISCHARACTERIZED · WEAK
- **Citation:** Plichta MM, Scheres A (2014). Ventral–striatal responsiveness during reward anticipation in ADHD and its relation to trait impulsivity in the healthy population: A meta-analytic review of the fMRI literature. Neuroscience & Biobehavioral Reviews, 38:125–134.
- **Peer reviewed:** None
- **Sample / design:** Meta-analytic review of fMRI monetary-incentive-delay-type studies; heterogeneous ADHD samples, mostly medication-washout designs.
- **Effect size:** d = 0.48–0.58 (medium) for VS hyporesponsiveness during reward anticipation in ADHD vs controls; opposite (positive) VS–impulsivity relationship in healthy samples.
- **Kind:** BOTH
- **Evidence type:** PEER_REVIEWED
- **Ethical status:** NEEDS_CARE
- **Mechanism:** If the anticipatory dopaminergic signal is weakened, 'wanting' generated by a promised-but-delayed reward is a weak lever. What remains comparatively intact is the response to reward *delivery* — feedback that has already happened.
- **Piano-app application:** REVERSAL: skip the anticipation-hype layer entirely. No 'unlock in X', no reveal animations before a result, no 'come back tomorrow for your bonus'. Instead spend the design budget on delivery-side density: instantaneous, unambiguous per-note/per-phrase feedback. A mechanic that is a proven retention driver in mainstream F2P (scheduled anticipation) is predicted to underperform here while still carrying compulsion risk.
- **Verifier correction:** The source says the opposite about certainty. Its final sentence is: 'we conclude that at the present stage the number of existing studies in the healthy population as well as in ADHD groups is too small for a final answer.' The paper presents THREE competing models to reconcile the contrast (inverted-U, genetic moderator, and an 'unrelated' model) and endorses none. Presenting this as an established mechanism inverts the authors' own hedge. Separately, the design inference is doubly broken: (a) scale mismatch — ventral-striatal reward anticipation in the Monetary Incentive Delay task is a 1.5–4 second cue-to-outcome window, which is not the same construct as a 4-hour game timer or a multi-day teaser campaign; (b) internal incoherence — if the anticipation system is BLUNTED, anticipation mechanics should be LESS effective on this population, not more exploitative. 'Exploits a system that is blunted' does not parse as a harm mechanism.
- **Replication:** This is the weakest evidence class in the set masquerading as the strongest-sounding. Small-N fMRI meta-analyses from this era have a poor replication record generally, and reward-circuit hypoactivation in ADHD has had inconsistent support in later, larger samples. Treat the direction as a hypothesis. Do not build a design prohibition on a reverse inference from BOLD signal.
- **Verifier notes:** Citation is accurate on authors, year, journal, volume, pages. Reported effect d=0.48–0.58 (described as medium) for VS hyporesponsiveness in ADHD; positive correlation between impulsivity scores and VS activation in healthy samples is correctly reported. But the abstract never states a study count, and the authors flag the literature as too small — a 2014 fMRI meta-analysis assembled from a handful of small-N studies is precisely the genre most inflated by the imaging replication crisis (typical per-group n of 15–25, uncorrected thresholds, flexible contrasts).

### Immediate contingent reinforcement improves task performance MORE in ADHD than in controls. This is a genuine reversal of the usual gamification caution that points/feedback are shallow motivators — for ADHD they are closer to a performance prosthesis.

- **Status:** MISCHARACTERIZED · WEAK
- **Citation:** Luman M, Oosterlaan J, Sergeant JA (2005). The impact of reinforcement contingencies on AD/HD: A review and theoretical appraisal. Clinical Psychology Review, 25(2):183–213.
- **Peer reviewed:** None
- **Sample / design:** Narrative + quantitative review of contingency-manipulation experiments in children with ADHD; child samples, laboratory tasks, so ecological transfer to adult skill practice is an inference.
- **Effect size:** Review of 22 studies / 1,181 children: reinforcement improved performance and motivation in both ADHD and controls, with the performance effect 'somewhat more prominent' in ADHD. Manipulating reward magnitude, immediacy and response cost can normalise or markedly improve ADHD task performance.
- **Kind:** BOTH
- **Evidence type:** PEER_REVIEWED
- **Ethical status:** BENIGN
- **Mechanism:** Under optimal-stimulation and dynamic-developmental accounts, ADHD performance deficits are partly motivational-state deficits rather than capacity deficits. Dense external contingency substitutes for the internally generated signal that sustains effort on low-interest tasks.
- **Piano-app application:** WEAK GENERALLY, STRONG FOR ADHD: per-note visual/haptic confirmation, live accuracy readout, and immediate 'that bar was clean' marking. In a neurotypical practice app this reads as gimmicky and risks overjustification; for an ADHD adult it is likely the difference between practising and not. Fingerly's Canvas/GL note-highway layer is the right place to invest — make correctness legible at the note level, in blunt metric form (SPEC §2 forbids praise copy, which is fine: the reinforcer should be *information*, not flattery).
- **Verifier correction:** The source's actual wording is far weaker: 'There is evidence that the effect related to task performance is somewhat more prominent in AD/HD.' 'Somewhat more prominent' is not 'a performance prosthesis'. Three omissions that cut against the claim: (1) the same review reports the headline finding that reinforcement 'ha[s] a positive impact on task performance and levels of motivation for BOTH children with AD/HD and normal controls' — the differential is a secondary qualifier on a shared effect, not a reversal; (2) the review's psychophysiological evidence runs in the OPPOSITE direction: 'children with AD/HD seem less sensitive to reinforcement compared to controls'; (3) the authors' own conclusion is that 'results show a discrepancy between the theoretical models and the behavioural findings' — they are reporting incoherence, not a clean design rule. Also state the population: 22 studies, N=1181 CHILDREN. There is no adult evidence here.
- **Replication:** Directly undercut by the same field's later and better work: Hulsbosch et al. (2021) JAACAP 60(11):1367–1381, a systematic review of 19 instrumental-learning studies, found 'no difference in basic instrumental learning... nor effects of form or magnitude of reinforcement' between ADHD and typically developing children, and rated the study quality as poor on sample sizes and missing-data reporting. A 2005 narrative review's hedged differential does not survive that.
- **Verifier notes:** This is a 2005 NARRATIVE review, not a meta-analysis — no pooled effect size, no heterogeneity statistics, no formal bias assessment. The claim's EVIDENCE TYPE of PEER_REVIEWED is technically true but hides that qualitative-review evidence sits well below the meta-analyses cited elsewhere in this set. The review also flags that comorbid ODD/CD are likely confounders that most included studies did not examine. Two of the three defensible sub-findings — 'high intensity of reinforcement is highly effective in AD/HD' and 'children with AD/HD prefer immediate over delayed reward' — are hedged with 'some evidence' in the original.

### Variable-ratio / partial reinforcement — the workhorse schedule of mainstream game design — is the clearest candidate for reversal. Douglas & Parry's frustrative-non-reward account predicts ADHD performance DETERIORATES under partial relative to continuous reward, with heightened frustration to omitted expected rewards and faster extinction (a diminished partial-reinforcement extinction effect).

- **Status:** CONFIRMED · STRONG
- **Citation:** Douglas VI, Parry PA (1994), as reviewed in Luman et al. (2005) Clin Psychol Rev 25:183–213; contested by Hulsbosch A-K et al. (2023). Instrumental learning and behavioral persistence in children with ADHD: does reinforcement frequency matter? Journal of Child Psychology and Psychiatry, 64(11).
- **Peer reviewed:** None
- **Sample / design:** Child laboratory studies with marble/money exchange schedules; the more recent well-powered replication is null, so this is a 'design conservatively' finding rather than a settled one.
- **Effect size:** Direction and existence of the effect are contested. Older work: reliable performance decrement under 50% vs 100% reward in hyperactive children. Hulsbosch (2023) found neither an acquisition deficit under partial reinforcement nor a diminished PREE. Treat as a plausible risk, not an established effect size.
- **Kind:** INVISIBLE_MECHANIC
- **Evidence type:** PEER_REVIEWED
- **Ethical status:** DARK_PATTERN
- **Mechanism:** Two competing readings: (a) heightened frustration to reward omission makes intermittent schedules aversive and performance-degrading; (b) the same schedule is precisely what drives compulsive persistence in slot machines and loot boxes. Both readings argue against using it in a practice tool — either it demotivates or it hooks.
- **Piano-app application:** BENIGN-FOR-NT / RISKY-FOR-ADHD: do not use randomised rewards, surprise bonuses, random 'critical hit' XP multipliers, or any schedule where identical performance yields different feedback. Fingerly should be strictly deterministic: the same played passage produces the same evaluation and the same reward, every time. Determinism also removes any suspicion that the app is manipulating the user — important for a tool the user builds trust in over years.
- **Verifier correction:** Drop the word 'bidirectional'. Every primary study in this meta-analysis is cross-sectional; the authors report the association measured from two comparison directions (GD severity in ADHD vs non-ADHD, g=0.693; ADHD severity in GD vs non-GD, g=0.854), which is measurement symmetry, not bidirectional causation. The authors' own closing sentence calls for 'more rigorous methodologies... (e.g. longitudinal studies and studies using professional/clinical ratings and diagnosis)'. Also, the authors label r=0.296 'moderate', not 'moderate-to-large'; the 'large' half of the phrase comes from the Hedges' g group comparisons, which are a different estimand — do not merge the two into one magnitude.
- **Replication:** Meta-analytic synthesis of a large replicated literature — the existence of the association is not in doubt. What has NOT been established by this source is direction, causality, or clinical thresholds. Claim 2's cohort is the appropriate evidence for direction; this one cannot carry it.
- **Verifier notes:** Citation is exact on every field (authors, year, journal, volume, article number). Pooled r=0.296 combined; inattention r=0.306, hyperactivity r=0.266. Moderators: higher estimates where samples had more males, where problematic internet use was measured instead of GD proper, and in more recently published studies — the second of those is a construct-validity warning, since PIU scales are not GD scales. Residual concern the abstract does not resolve: GD and ADHD self-report instruments share item content (difficulty stopping, poor time management, distractibility), so some of r=0.296 is common-method and construct overlap rather than comorbidity. The EXAMPLE attached to this claim (Duolingo/Elevate north-star metrics) is the claimant's own editorial framing and appears nowhere in the source.

### Streak and response-cost mechanics carry asymmetric risk in ADHD: response cost drives performance harder than reward but measurably DEPRESSES self-rated motivation on low-interest tasks, and ADHD samples show elevated behavioural punishment sensitivity. Combined with the abstinence-violation dynamic, a broken streak is a plausible practice-abandonment trigger rather than a motivator.

- **Status:** MISCHARACTERIZED · WEAK
- **Citation:** Carlson CL, Tamm L (2000). Responsiveness of children with ADHD to reward and response cost. Journal of Consulting and Clinical Psychology, 68(1):73–83; plus Sagvolden-tradition punishment-sensitivity work (e.g. ADHD Atten Def Hyp Disord, 2019, response-allocation study in Japanese children). Streak-break abandonment itself is product observation, not RCT evidence.
- **Peer reviewed:** None
- **Sample / design:** Small-n child laboratory experiments with contingency manipulation; the adult-app extrapolation is mine, not the authors'.
- **Effect size:** Response cost > reward for performance improvement, but with decreased self-rated motivation on the low-interest task. Punishment-sensitivity study: ADHD children allocated significantly more responses away from the more-punished alternative and responded more slowly to it.
- **Kind:** VISIBLE_UI
- **Evidence type:** PEER_REVIEWED
- **Ethical status:** NEEDS_CARE
- **Concrete example:** Duolingo's streak is explicitly built on loss aversion and is its strongest retention tool; Duolingo itself has had to bolt on streak freezes, Super streak repair, and (2026) event-based revival for 30+ day streaks — an admission that the raw mechanic produces abandonment on break.
- **Mechanism:** A visible streak counter converts every missed day into a loss event. Loss aversion (~2x) makes that a strong short-run retention lever for the general population — but for a user with elevated punishment sensitivity plus all-or-nothing appraisal, the counter reaching zero removes the entire scaffold at once, and the extrinsic motivator that had displaced intrinsic interest is now gone.
- **Piano-app application:** BENIGN-FOR-NT / RISKY-FOR-ADHD. If Fingerly shows any continuity metric, make it non-resettable and non-punitive: 'days practised this month: 11' or a rolling 30-day density heatmap, never a consecutive-day counter that zeroes. Never send a 'your streak is about to die' notification. If a streak-like display is kept at all, implement the equivalent of a streak freeze silently and by default rather than as a purchasable/earnable scarcity item.
- **Verifier correction:** The Carlson & Tamm half omits the qualifier that reverses its design implication. The abstract states the depression of motivation is RELATIVE TO REWARD — 'relative to R, RC showed stronger effects but at the expense of decreased self-rated motivation on the low-interest task' — and then explicitly adds: 'Neither contingency decreased motivation measures relative to NR for either group.' Response cost did NOT push motivation below the no-contingency baseline. So the finding is 'response cost is less motivating than reward', not 'response cost is demotivating'. That is a materially different design conclusion. The punishment-sensitivity half is UNVERIFIABLE as cited: no author, no title, no volume, no page — I could not locate a 2019 ADHD Atten Def Hyp Disord response-allocation study in Japanese children from that description. Replace it with a real reference or delete the sub-claim.
- **Replication:** The 'elevated behavioural punishment sensitivity in ADHD' premise is contradicted by newer work from the Hulsbosch/Van der Oord group (2024, Research on Child and Adolescent Psychopathology), which found children with ADHD showed altered EMOTIONAL responding to punishment but 'did not show an altered response bias under punishment' — i.e. the behavioural sensitivity effect the claim needs did not appear. There is also an active scoping review (Oguchi et al. 2024, BMJ Open) motivated precisely by the poor agreement between task-based and questionnaire measures of reward/punishment sensitivity in ADHD, which is a field-level admission that this construct is not settled.
- **Verifier notes:** Carlson & Tamm 2000 is real and the field data are exact (JCCP vol 68, pp 73–83, 2000), though the claim truncates the subtitle that literally announces the nuance it drops. Sample is 22 ADHD children + 22 controls, within-subject, single lab study, never directly replicated to my knowledge. The claim is commendably honest that 'streak-break abandonment itself is product observation, not RCT evidence' — that framing is correct and should be preserved. The Duolingo EXAMPLE is partly unverifiable: Streak Freeze and streak repair are real, well-known features, but I could not verify the asserted '(2026) event-based revival for 30+ day streaks' (the Duolingo blog URL returned 404 and my web-search budget was exhausted). Treat that specific detail as unconfirmed, and note that a company shipping mitigation features is evidence of a perceived retention problem, not evidence of an ADHD-specific abandonment mechanism.

### Hyperfocus is empirically real and measurable as a dispositional trait correlated with ADHD — but it is NOT flow, and it terminates externally rather than volitionally. Designing an app to induce hyperfocus is therefore designing an app the user cannot stop using.

- **Status:** MISCHARACTERIZED · WEAK
- **Citation:** Grinblat N et al. (2024). Validation of the dispositional adult hyperfocus questionnaire (AHQ-D). Scientific Reports, 14. + Hupfeld KE, Abagis TR, Shah P (2019). Living 'in the zone': hyperfocus in adult ADHD. ADHD Attention Deficit and Hyperactivity Disorders, 11:191–208. + Ashinoff BK, Abu-Akel A (2021). Hyperfocus: the forgotten frontier of attention. Psychological Research, 85:1–19.
- **Peer reviewed:** None
- **Sample / design:** Online questionnaire validation (Prolific) plus a qualitative/survey study of adults with ADHD. Self-report throughout; Ashinoff & Abu-Akel note hyperfocus is poorly operationalised across the literature and propose four criteria: induced by task engagement, sustained/selective attention, diminished perception of non-task stimuli, improved task performance.
- **Effect size:** AHQ-D validation, n = 347 adults: single-factor structure, α = 0.93; hyperfocus correlated r = 0.53 with CAARS ADHD traits; self-reported ADHD diagnosis M = 45.64 vs 36.02. Critically, correlation with dispositional FLOW was only r = 0.12, while correlation with internet addiction was r = 0.44.
- **Kind:** INVISIBLE_MECHANIC
- **Evidence type:** PEER_REVIEWED
- **Ethical status:** NEEDS_CARE
- **Mechanism:** Hyperfocus involves more extreme detachment from environment than flow, and empirically clusters with internet addiction rather than with flow. Hupfeld et al.'s participants reported hyperfocusing at the cost of eating and sleeping, time blindness during episodes, inability to stop of their own accord, and irritability when interrupted — especially by physical touch.
- **Piano-app application:** REVERSAL OF STANDARD ADVICE: mainstream design treats 'we induced flow' as an unqualified win. For this user, an immersive full-screen note highway with escalating difficulty is a hyperfocus induction device, and the empirical picture says the user will not self-terminate. Fingerly should therefore own termination: a hard session end that closes the practice surface, an unmissable non-tactile pre-warning (visual + audio, not haptic — irritability to touch interruption is documented), and a deliberate wind-down rather than an abrupt cut. Also worth logging: 'overshoot minutes' as a private diagnostic the user can review.
- **Verifier correction:** Three citation failures. (1) 'Wang & Nurkka' is WRONG authorship. The paper 'Snapchat Streaks: How Adolescents Metagame Gamification in Social Media' is by Hristova, Dumit, Lieberoth & Slunecko (2019), and it appeared in the Workshop Proceedings of the 4th International GamiFIN Conference — a workshop paper, not a journal article. (2) The problematic-smartphone-use/FoMO paper is real but is published in Telematics and Informatics Reports (2023), not 'Computers in Human Behavior: Artificial Humans' or 'CHB Reports'; the claim hedges across two journals and both are wrong. (3) Bayer et al.'s Snapchat work concerns ephemeral social interaction generally, not streaks; citing it for streak findings is misattribution. EVIDENCE TYPE should not be PEER_REVIEWED — the password-sharing detail is journalism (widely reported since ~2017), not a peer-reviewed finding I could source.
- **Replication:** The Telematics and Informatics Reports (2023) study is cross-sectional survey work on early adolescents; I could not retrieve effect sizes, and cross-sectional FoMO/problematic-smartphone-use associations are a literature with well-known common-method-variance and measurement problems. The Hristova et al. workshop paper is qualitative interview work with Viennese adolescents (themes: reciprocity, mass snaps, black snaps, metagaming) — descriptive, small, not designed to establish 'interpersonal rejection' as a mechanism.
- **Verifier notes:** The underlying phenomenon is real and reasonably documented as PRACTICE — adolescents do metagame Snapstreaks, and this is genuinely useful as a counterexample to 'only monetised mechanics are exploitative.' But it should be labelled QUALITATIVE + JOURNALISM, and the citation block needs rewriting from scratch. As written, one of the three citations has invented authors, which is the single worst citation defect in this set.

### Time-perception impairment in ADHD is a consistent, meta-analytically supported deficit — so a session-length limit is not paternalism, it is an accessibility accommodation for a measured perceptual deficit.

- **Status:** MISCHARACTERIZED · MODERATE
- **Citation:** Meta-analysis: Altered Perceptual Timing Abilities in Attention-Deficit/Hyperactivity Disorder. Journal of the American Academy of Child & Adolescent Psychiatry (2021/2022), 55 studies, >2,200 participants.
- **Peer reviewed:** None
- **Sample / design:** Meta-analysis pooling laboratory timing tasks across ADHD and control groups; mostly children/adolescents.
- **Effect size:** Medium effect size deficit in time discrimination (25 studies, n = 1,633); small-to-medium increase in absolute error on time estimation (8 studies, n = 1,024). Deficits most pronounced for time discrimination and time reproduction, especially sub-second intervals.
- **Kind:** INVISIBLE_MECHANIC
- **Evidence type:** PEER_REVIEWED
- **Ethical status:** BENIGN
- **Mechanism:** If interval perception is measurably degraded, the internal 'I've been at this long enough' signal is unreliable. Asking the user to self-limit is asking them to use the faculty that is impaired. External timekeeping substitutes for the deficient internal clock.
- **Piano-app application:** Make elapsed and remaining time continuously ambient in the practice UI — a thin depleting bar at the edge of the note highway, always visible, never modal. Pair with the enforced session end. Frame it in-app as an accommodation, not a restriction, and never gate 'more time' behind anything (no ads, no purchase, no earning). Also: sub-second timing deficits are directly relevant to rhythm scoring — consider a slightly wider default timing tolerance with an explicitly user-adjustable window rather than a fixed one calibrated to neurotypical performance.
- **Verifier correction:** Two citation errors and one inferential error. (1) The '>2,200 participants' figure is a misreading of the PRISMA flow: 55 studies were retained FROM 2,266 SCREENED RECORDS. 2,266 is a count of database hits, not people. Delete that number — you do not have the participant total. (2) The citation gives no authors and hedges the year; it is Marx, Cortese, Koelch & Hacker, 2022, JAACAP 61(7):866–880. (3) The inference is a scale mismatch of three or more orders of magnitude. What the meta-analysis measured is discrimination of 'stimuli of very brief durations, especially in the sub-second range', variability in estimating durations 'lasting several seconds', and time estimation/production/reproduction accuracy in lab paradigms. Nothing in it measures whether someone misjudges how long a 45-minute practice session lasted. Sub-second discrimination deficits do not establish that an ADHD adult cannot tell an hour has passed.
- **Replication:** Meta-analytic across 55 studies with meta-regression, so the timing deficits themselves are well replicated at the construct level. I found no failed-replication literature contradicting the core timing finding. Rated MODERATE rather than STRONG only because the specific proposition the claim needs — misperception of elapsed time over multi-minute engaging tasks — is not what was meta-analysed.
- **Verifier notes:** The underlying finding is real and reasonably strong — random-effects models with meta-regression across 55 studies, deficits found across four timing paradigms, with the authors interpreting the estimation/production pattern as an accelerated internal clock and the reproduction pattern as reflecting attentional (distraction) and motivational (delay aversion) contributions. Note the JAACAP venue: this is predominantly a child and adolescent literature. Also note the honest reading of the reproduction result — the deficit there is partly attributed to attention and delay aversion, i.e. it is not a pure perceptual deficit, which weakens the 'measured perceptual deficit' framing the accommodation argument leans on. A session-length limit may well be a good design decision; it is simply not licensed by THIS evidence, and dressing a product judgement in a citation it does not fit is the failure mode to avoid.

### Optimal-stimulation theory: added task-irrelevant stimulation IMPROVES performance in ADHD on low-interest tasks while being neutral-to-harmful for controls. Sensory/visual richness that general UX guidance calls 'distracting clutter' can be functional here.

- **Status:** MISCHARACTERIZED · WEAK
- **Citation:** Zentall SS, Zentall TR (1975/1983). Optimal stimulation as theoretical basis of hyperactivity / Optimal stimulation: a model of disordered activity and performance in normal and deviant children. American Journal of Orthopsychiatry / Psychological Bulletin. Extended by extra-task stimulation studies (e.g. Extra-Task Stimulation on Mathematics Performance in Children with and without ADHD) and Geissler et al. (2014), ADHD Atten Def Hyp Disord, on hyperactivity/sensation-seeking as autoregulatory arousal stabilisation.
- **Peer reviewed:** None
- **Sample / design:** Multiple small child-sample experiments across decades; classroom and lab settings. Treat as a well-replicated direction with unreliable magnitude.
- **Effect size:** Classic finding is a crossover interaction: added stimulation (colour, music, movement) benefits ADHD performance on repetitive/low-interest tasks and does not benefit — sometimes impairs — controls. Effect sizes vary widely by study; the theory is well-supported directionally but the empirical base is old and heterogeneous.
- **Kind:** VISIBLE_UI
- **Evidence type:** PEER_REVIEWED
- **Ethical status:** BENIGN
- **Mechanism:** Hyperactivity is modelled as a homeostatic attempt to raise arousal toward an optimum. Supplying stimulation exogenously removes the need to self-generate it, freeing capacity for the task.
- **Piano-app application:** REVERSAL: the minimalist, distraction-free practice UI that general design advice prescribes may be actively worse here. Fingerly's particle/GL layer earns its keep — but make it *task-coupled* (particles emitted by the notes the user actually plays, colour/intensity keyed to accuracy) rather than ambient decoration, so it raises arousal without competing for attention. Make stimulation intensity a first-class user-facing slider, since the individual optimum varies day to day and with medication state.
- **Verifier correction:** All three named citations are real and correctly located, but NONE of them is empirical evidence for the effect. Zentall 1975 proposes 'that hyperactive behavior may result from a homeostatic mechanism that functions to increase stimulation' — a theoretical proposal. Zentall & Zentall 1983 is a Psychological Bulletin model paper. Geissler et al. 2014 is explicitly a review advancing the vigilance-regulation model, whose own conclusion is a proposal ('We propose vigilance regulation as a converging biomarker') and which notes the key antimanic prediction was still under test in an ongoing RCT. Citing three theory papers as if they established an empirical effect is the core error here. Also correct 'neutral-to-harmful for controls' — the best-matching empirical study found controls were simply unaffected, with no harm observed.
- **Replication:** The underarousal/optimal-stimulation account is a 1970s–80s framework that has been substantially superseded by state-regulation and default-mode-interference models. Its live descendant — the white-noise/stochastic-resonance benefit in ADHD — has a notably mixed replication record, with several null and small-sample-inflated results. An order-dependent effect in n=20 per group is exactly the profile of a finding that does not survive scaling up. Do not treat 'clutter is functional for ADHD' as established; if you want the visual richness, justify it on its own design merits.
- **Verifier notes:** The 'extra-task stimulation' citation is given only as an approximate title with no authors or year. The closest real study I could locate is Abikoff H, Courtney ME, Szeibel PJ, Koplewicz HS (1996), 'The effects of auditory stimulation on the arithmetic performance of children with ADHD and nondisabled children', J Learn Disabil 29(3):238–246, PMID 8732885 — and it is a thin reed: 40 boys total (20 ADHD, 20 control), and the benefit of music over speech/silence 'occurred only for ADHD participants who received music as the initial condition'. That is an order-dependent subgroup effect in a cell of roughly ten children, in 1996, with no correction for the multiplicity that an order-conditional finding implies. The authors themselves say it provides 'some support' for the theory. Note also the scope mismatch: 'task-irrelevant auditory stimulation during arithmetic worksheets' is not 'visual richness in a note-highway renderer', and nothing here speaks to whether particle effects help or hurt sight-reading.

### Novelty and switching split in opposite directions: novelty WITHIN a task is an engagement asset in ADHD, but actual task-set switching is measurably inefficient in adult ADHD. 'Keep it fresh by mixing activities' is the wrong operationalisation of the novelty finding.

- **Status:** MISCHARACTERIZED · WEAK
- **Citation:** King JA, Colla M, Brass M, Heuser I, von Cramon DY (2007). Inefficient cognitive control in adult ADHD: evidence from trial-by-trial Stroop test and cued task switching performance. Behavioral and Brain Functions, 3:42.
- **Peer reviewed:** None
- **Sample / design:** Adult ADHD vs control, trial-by-trial Stroop and cued task-switching paradigms. Modest sample; single study rather than meta-analysis, so weight accordingly.
- **Effect size:** Adult ADHD group showed abnormal processing of task-irrelevant stimulus features on both Stroop and cued task-switching, with generally slower and less accurate responding across most analyses. (Reported as group differences; the paper is modest-n.)
- **Kind:** BOTH
- **Evidence type:** PEER_REVIEWED
- **Ethical status:** BENIGN
- **Mechanism:** Novelty-seeking is a motivational pull, but set-shifting is an executive cost. When a design satisfies the novelty appetite by changing the task, it charges an executive-function tax the ADHD user pays at a worse rate than a neurotypical user — the switch feels productive while output quality drops.
- **Piano-app application:** Deliver novelty as variation inside one stable interaction loop — new passage, new tempo target, new voicing, changed visual skin — while keeping the input model, scoring rules and UI layout invariant. Explicitly avoid a 'practice carousel' that rotates between sight-reading mode / rhythm mode / theory mode within a session; batch mode changes across sessions instead. SPEC §2's 'no decision menus before playing' is exactly right and should extend to no mid-session mode menus.
- **Verifier correction:** Half the claim is uncited. King et al. 2007 supports (weakly and conditionally) the SWITCHING half only; it says nothing whatever about novelty within a task being an engagement asset. That first proposition — which is the half that actually drives a design recommendation — has no source attached and should either get one or be labelled as the author's inference. On the switching half, the source is more conditional than the flat assertion implies: interference effects 'were found to be dependent on the time allotted to prepare for an upcoming task', and sustained task-set maintenance and transient task-set updating differences were likewise 'dependent on experimental manipulation of task preparation processes'. Rewrite as: switching costs in adult ADHD appear under limited preparation time, not unconditionally.
- **Replication:** No replication evidence was checked for or found for this specific study, and I would not expect a 22-vs-22 conditional interaction from 2007 to be robust on its own. Executive-function deficits in adult ADHD are meta-analytically supported in general, but with small effect sizes and substantial heterogeneity, and switching specifically is one of the less consistent domains. This is a plausible design heuristic supported by an underpowered single study, not a measured fact.
- **Verifier notes:** Citation fields all verify (Behav Brain Funct vol 3, article 42, 2007; the record lists von Cramon D rather than von Cramon DY, a trivial variance). Sample is 22 adults with persistent ADHD vs 22 matched controls — small for a between-groups executive-function contrast, and from the era before task-switching effect sizes in ADHD were systematically meta-analysed. The authors explicitly could not disentangle top-down control failure from bottom-up task engagement with their data, which is precisely the ambiguity that matters for a design claim: if the deficit is engagement rather than control, 'mixing activities' might fail for motivational reasons rather than switch-cost reasons, and the remedy differs.

### Loot boxes, limited-time offers and in-game purchases sit at the intersection of every ADHD vulnerability (impulsivity, delay discounting, variable-ratio schedules, scarcity urgency). Direct ADHD-specific causal evidence is thin, but the convergent picture is strong enough to treat these as prohibited rather than merely risky.

- **Status:** MISCHARACTERIZED · STRONG
- **Citation:** Zendle D, Meyer R, Over H (2019). Adolescents and loot boxes: links with problem gambling and motivations for purchase. Royal Society Open Science, 6:190049. + Garea SS et al. / Spicer S et al. loot-box scoping review, PLOS ONE (2022) 17(1):e0263177. + Inaguma et al. (2024), Psychiatry and Clinical Neurosciences Reports 3:e167 — case report of gaming disorder with heavy loot-box spend in ADHD.
- **Peer reviewed:** None
- **Sample / design:** Mostly large cross-sectional surveys of gamers; one prospective study found earlier loot-box experience did NOT predict later gambling problems, so causality is unresolved. Be honest that the ADHD-specific claim is inference from convergent risk factors.
- **Effect size:** Consistent small-to-moderate positive correlation between loot-box spend and problem-gambling severity across dozens of studies; association strengthened when loot-box items are available only for a limited time. Loot-box purchasers show elevated severe psychological distress. ADHD-specific evidence is case-report level plus the general impulsivity/IGD link — no ADHD-stratified experiment exists.
- **Kind:** BOTH
- **Evidence type:** PEER_REVIEWED
- **Ethical status:** DARK_PATTERN
